Now, Nagan and Thaththai next offer numerous bells as garlands before Lord Muruga's shrine and pray. They offer beautiful Kadamba garlands to Muruga. They perform Kuravai Koothu (a type of folk dance) for Muruga, perform grand Anangadal (a ritual dance), and pray by holding grand festivals. Kuravai Koothu, or Kuravai, is a type of dance. Adiyarkkunallar, the commentator on Silappathikaram, writes that it falls under the category of "Vinodha Koothu" (amusing dance). Kuravai Koothu means people praising the prowess of their deity and dancing according to the specific land. As per "Seyon Meya Mai Varai Ulagamum," this is the Kurinji land. Therefore, the mountain-dwelling hunters sing and dance the praises of Velan, the deity of Kurinji land. This is called Kuravai Koothu. The women performing this dance bathe in waterfalls and springs. Then they beat the Thondakapparai (a drum) and play instruments like the Kodu (a horn). They sing songs belonging to the Kurinji land. They spread fragrant smoke using aromatic substances found in the mountainous region. They offer flowers to God using various flowers found in the Kurinji land. They scatter mixed flowers. Then they perform Veriyadal (a ritual dance) and praise Muruga, the spear in his hand, and Muruga's bravery, by dancing and singing. This Kundrakkuravai, a Kuravai Koothu praising Muruga, can be seen in detail in ancient Tamil texts like Thirumurugatruppadai and Silappathikaram. "Perumthan Kanaveera Narunthan Maalai Thunaiyura Varuththu Thoonga Naatri Nalimalai Silambin Nannagar Vaazhththi Narum Pugai Eduththu Kurinji Paadi Imilizhisai Aruviyodu Inniyam Karanga Uruvappal Pooththuuy Veruvara Kuruthich Chenthinai Parappik Kuramagal Murugiya Niruththu Muran Inar Atka Murugatruppaduththa Urukezhu Viyanagar Aadugalan Silamba Paadi Palavudan Koduvaai Vaiththuk Kodumani Iyakkki Odaap Pootkai Pinimugam Vaazhththi Vendunar Vendiyangu Eythinr Vazhipadu" is from Thirumurugatruppadai. The explanation of this verse is what we saw above. In addition, in Silappathikaram, there are also verses about Kundrakkuravai Koothu: "Uravu Neer Maa Kondra Veleththi Kuravai Thoduththondru Paadugam Vaa Thozhi" and "Seerkezhu Sentilum Sengodum Venkundrum Eragamum Neenga Iraivan Kai Vel Andre Paarirum Pavvaththinul Pukku Pandroru Naal Sormaa Thadindha Sudarilaiya Velale" and "Animugangal Oraarum Eeraaru Kaiyum Inaiyindri Thaan Udaiyaan Endhiya Velandre Pinimugam Mel Kondu Avunar Peedu Azhiyum Vannam Ani Visumbil Konetha Maaratta Velale" and "Saravana Poom Poigaiyinil Thaaymaar Aruvar Thirumulaip Paal Undaan Thirukkai Vel Andre Varuthi Kiriyon Avunan Maarbum Pilandhu Kurugu Peyar Kundram Kondra Neduvellale." Ilango Adigal has composed songs about Kundrakkuravai Koothu in Silappathikaram. Similarly, we see in Silappathikaram that the people of the Mullai land, as per "Maayon Meya Kaadurai Ulagam," perform Aychchiyar Kuravai, praising the glory of Kannan, their deity.

Thus, Nagan and Thaththai perform Kuravai Koothu and offer their prayers. After that, they perform Anangadal, a Veriyadal, remembering Lord Muruga. Performing Veriyadal means invoking and bringing Lord Muruga into a trance. In this Veriyadal, those who perform it will hold the Vel (spear), praise Muruga, and dance. Lord Muruga will enter a trance in them. When Lord Muruga enters a trance in the person performing the Veriyadal, people will pray to him for what they desire. It is a tradition that he will also grant boons in accordance with the people's prayers. Here, Sekkizhar Swamigal states that they perform Anangadal, a Veriyadal, using Anangu. Anangu means a woman, that is, a divine woman. Therefore, they perform Veriyattam with a great divine woman and celebrate a grand festival for Lord Muruga. Thus, Nagan and Thaththai offer roosters and peacocks to Lord Muruga. They adorn him with garlands of bells and Kadamba flowers. They pray to Lord Muruga by holding festivals like Kuravaiyadal, Kuravai Paadal, and Anangadal. Lord Muruga accepts their prayers and bestows his grace upon them. In this verse, Sekkizhar Peruman beautifully describes Lord Muruga. Sekkizhar states what kind of person Lord Muruga is. Sekkizhar Swamigal describes Lord Muruga as "Eyil Udai Purangal Setra Enthaiyaar Maintharaana Mayil Udai Kotra Oorthi Varai Uram Kiziththa Thinmai Ayil Udai Thadakkai Venri Annanaar." Lord Shiva is the one who burned the three cities (Thiripuram) with great walls. Lord Muruga is his son. Lord Muruga is referred to here as "Mayil Udai Kotra Oorthi Varai Uram Kiziththa Thinmai Ayil Udai Thadakkai Venri Annanaar." Sekkizhar Swamigal gives so many epithets to Lord Muruga. Let's look at this in detail. Lord Muruga has a victorious peacock as his vehicle. "Kotram" means bravery and victory. Lord Muruga has a peacock with this "Kotram" as his vehicle. Everyone knows that the peacock is Lord Muruga's vehicle. But Lord Muruga has three types of peacocks as his vehicles. Firstly, Pranava itself is Lord Muruga's peacock vehicle. It is called Pranava Mayil. Next, Indra becomes a peacock vehicle for Lord Muruga. The third peacock vehicle is Surapadman, one of the two, who transforms into a peacock and becomes Lord Muruga's vehicle. Thus, Lord Muruga has three peacock vehicles: Pranava Mayil, Indra Mayil, and Asura Mayil. Here, Sekkizhar Swamigal says that Lord Muruga has a peacock with "Kotram" as his vehicle. We saw that "Kotram" means bravery and victory. This epithet applies to all three peacock vehicles. Are not the wise truly brave? Are not the wise those who have transcended all principles? Therefore, is not Nada Tattva (the principle of sound) the foremost of all principles? Does not Pranava originate from that Nada Tattva? Therefore, for the wise who have transcended principles, Pranava assists in that bravery to transcend principles, and Pranava is the cause of the victory of those wise who transcend principles. Therefore, Pranava Mayil is the peacock with "Kotram." Now, a war takes place between Surapadman and Lord Muruga. In the war, Surapadman, with his illusory powers, takes the form of a Chakravaka bird and a Nemi bird and opposes Lord Muruga. At that time, Indra transforms into a peacock and supports Lord Muruga. Lord Muruga, riding on this Indra peacock, fights with Surapadman, who has transformed into a Chakravaka bird. He splits his illusory form. Therefore, Devendra himself transformed into a peacock and supported Muruga here. Devendra is the leader of all Devas, isn't he a victorious one? Therefore, Devendra Mayil is the peacock with "Kotram." Next, at the end of the war, Surapadman transforms into a huge mango tree. Lord Muruga's Velayudham (spear) splits his form into two. One of these two parts becomes a peacock, and the other becomes a rooster. Muruga takes the peacock as his vehicle and the rooster as his banner. Therefore, this Asura Mayil is also a vehicle with "Kotram," a peacock with "Kotram." This can be seen in Kandapuranam, in the Surapadman Vadai Padalam, in verses 378, 494, and 499. Therefore, the peacock is Lord Muruga's vehicle, isn't it? Lord Shiva, Muruga's father, has the bull as his vehicle. "Aasai theera koduppaar alangam vidai mel varuvaar" is from Thirukkadavoor Mayanam Aazhudaiya Pillaiyar Thevaram. Therefore, Lord Shiva comes riding on a white bull to grant the wishes of those who desire them. But his son, Lord Muruga, to grant the wishes of those who desire them, comes faster than his father, having the peacock as his victorious vehicle. Lord Shiva comes walking on a bull. Lord Muruga comes flying on a peacock to grant the wishes of those who desire them. We will continue to reflect on the greatness of Lord Muruga through Sekkizhar Peruman's verses. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 08 — Kannappa Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Periya Puranam, Part 131, Ilai Malindha Sarukkam, Kannappa Nayanar Puranam. Nagan and Thaththai perform various prayers to Lord Muruga, desiring a child. By the grace of Lord Muruga, Kannappa Nayanar is about to be born as their son. In this section, Sekkizhar Swamigal praises and describes Lord Muruga with various epithets. Sekkizhar Swamigal states that Lord Muruga has a victorious peacock as his vehicle. According to Saiva Siddhanta, Anava Mala (ego), a flaw, is always present with the soul. Therefore, Anava Mala is called Sahaja Mala (innate impurity). Furthermore, the nature of the soul is to take on the color of what it associates with, as stated in Saiva Siddhanta. This means that the soul will manifest the characteristics of the object it is associated with. If a coral is placed near a crystal, the crystal will take on the red color of the coral and appear red. Saiva Siddhanta elders use this as an example to illustrate that the soul takes on the color of what it associates with. When God redeems the soul, like a coral embedded in a crystal, He manifests Himself in the soul and redeems it. At that time, the soul's quality of "asat" (non-being) will disappear. The soul's quality of "sat" (being) will prevail. Because God has redeemed the soul, the Anava Mala that had been obscuring the soul will become powerless and subside, causing no harm to the soul. As a result, the soul will experience God's bliss. The reference that Lord Muruga took Suran as his peacock vehicle clearly illustrates this truth of Saiva Siddhanta. The impurities of souls are three: Anava, Karma, and Maya. Surapadman, Simhamukhan, and Tharagan are examples of these three Asuras. Among them, Surapadman was the embodiment and locus of Anava Mala. The other two are examples of Karma and Maya. When God redeems the soul and grants it liberation, He destroys Maya Mala and Karma Mala. However, even in the state of liberation, Anava Mala does not perish. But before the presence of God, it remains inactive and subdued. Here, in Kandapuranam, Lord Muruga also destroys the two Asuras, Simhamukhan and Tharagan, who were the embodiments of Karma Mala and Maya Mala. He does not destroy Surapadman, the embodiment of Anava Mala, like the other two, but transforms him into a peacock and a rooster and keeps him with Him. He keeps him under His sacred feet. Therefore, just as Anava Mala does not perish even in the state of liberation but remains subdued at the sacred feet of God, Suran here transforms into a peacock and remains forever at the sacred feet of God, illustrating this Saiva Siddhanta concept. It is said that a peacock has seven colors. Here, the peacock, that is, the Asura peacock, is a symbol of Anava, isn't it? Even though Anava Mala is one, it expands into seven when it functions, according to Saiva Siddhanta. The seven functions of Anava are Moham (delusion), Madham (pride), Ragam (attachment), Kavalai (worry), Thabam (suffering), Vaattam (weariness), and Visiththiram (strangeness). Just as a peacock has seven colors, the functions of Anava, symbolized by the Asura peacock, are also seven. Even when God places the soul under His lotus feet, Anava does not perish there but remains powerless, which is a principle of Gnana Sastra. Just as his father, Lord Shiva, burned the three cities (Thiripuram), which were symbols of the three impurities, and purified the souls, so too his son, Lord Muruga, purifies the souls and places them under his sacred feet, demonstrating his victorious power by riding the peacock as his vehicle. This reference is noteworthy here. Next, Sekkizhar Swamigal describes Lord Muruga as one who wields the "Thinmai Ayil" (powerful spear) that split the mountain. "Varai" means mountain. Here, it refers to Mount Krauncha. It was in this Mount Krauncha that Tharagan hid and performed illusions. On his way to Mahendrapuri for the Surasamharam (destruction of Suras), Lord Muruga, with his army of Bhootas, fought Tharagan, who was hiding in the illusion of Mount Krauncha. Therefore, Lord Muruga split Mount Krauncha and killed Tharagan. The details of this can be found in Kandapuranam. It was the "Thinmai Ayil," the powerful spear, that split Mount Krauncha and killed Tharagan. "Ayil" means spear. The spear is the Gnana Shakti (power of wisdom) of Lord Muruga. According to Tamil grammar, the word "Vel" itself evolved from the verb "Vel" (to win) as a primary long verbal noun. Therefore, here, Lord Muruga's Velayudham (spear) also brings only victory. The Vel is the Gnana Shakti of Lord Muruga. Among the three powers – Iccha Shakti (power of will), Gnana Shakti (power of wisdom), and Kriya Shakti (power of action) – Gnana Shakti is the most excellent. "Eththiram Gnanam Ullathu Aththiram Icchai Seithi" is the 62nd verse in the first chapter of Sivagnana Siddhiyar Pramana Iyal. Its meaning is that the special functions of Gnana Shakti are Iccha Shakti and Kriya Shakti. Gnana is the very nature of Shiva Shakti. It is Gnana that knows and reveals in a general way to bestow grace upon living beings. That is called Para Shakti. That Gnana performs the five actions to bring about the maturation of impurities in living beings. Therefore, Para Shakti manifests in the form of the Velayudham in the sacred hand of Lord Kumara. In accordance with this, there is also the tradition that Para Shakti gave the Vel to Muruga. The unobstructed knowledge that manifests as one with Sivaguhaperuman is called Gnana Shakti. That one Para Shakti, out of great compassion for souls, exists in three forms. To illustrate that he possesses these three powers, Lord Muruga manifested with Valliammaiyar, Deivanaiammaiyar, and the Velayudham. Iccha Shakti is praised as Valliammaiyar, Kriya Shakti as Deivanaiammaiyar, and Gnana Shakti as Velayudham by the pure Vidyas. Therefore, this Vel destroys the three groups of impurities, namely Suran and his army, their illusions, and their strengths. Kandapuranam states that the 11 weapons in Lord Muruga's 11 sacred hands – Thomaram, Thuvasham, Kulisam, Angusum, Ambu, Pangayam, Mani, Mazhu, Thandu, Vaal, Vil – are the 11 Rudras, and the Velayudham is Shiva Shakti. All other weapons are inanimate objects, but the Vel in Muruga's hand is a conscious entity. Just as Para Shakti is inseparable from Lord Shiva and thus Para Shakti is the Supreme Being, similarly, this Velayudham in Lord Muruga's sacred hand is said to be Kandaswami himself. The Velayudham is not conquered by any other weapon. The Velayudham, taking many forms, conquers everything else. The Velayudham destroys bondages, as it was the Velayudham that dispelled the delusion of Veerabahu Devar and others when they were bound by the illusory bondages of Bhanukopan and Simhamukhan. To bestow liberation upon souls by severing their bonds with this Para Shakti, the Gnana Shakti of such greatness, and to grant them His sacred feet, Lord Muruga, the Supreme Acharya who took a divine form, holds the Gnana Shakti, the Velayudham, in His right sacred hand, and places His left sacred hand, which resembles a lotus flower, on His thigh. This divine form, with one sacred face and two sacred eyes, is praised in texts as Gnana Shakti Dharan, Gnana Shakti Dhara Swamy. That Vel, the Gnana Shakti of Lord Muruga, possessed the strength to destroy the powerful Suras who had received boons that made them unconquerable by any power other than God's. Sekkizhar Swamigal specifically praises it as "Thinmai Ayil." "Tharaganum Maayathadankiriyum Thoolaaga Veeravadivel Viduththone" is from Kandar Kali Venpa. "Kurugu Peyar Kundram Kondra Neduvellale" is from Kundrakkuravai in Silappathikaram. "Veeravel Tharaivel Vinnor Siraimitta Theeravel Sevvel Thirukkaivel Vaari Kuliththa Vel Kotravel Soor Maarbum Kundrum Thulaiththa Vel Undae Thunai" is a unique poem about the Vel. Arunagirinathar Peruman also composed a Vel Vaguppu. Thus, Sekkizhar Peruman praises the Vel in Lord Muruga's hand as "Varai Uram Kiziththa Thinmai Ayil." Now, Sekkizhar Swamigal gives the next epithet to Lord Muruga, saying that he has such a Vel in his "Thadankai" (large hand). Lord Muruga's "Thadankai" punished the Suras. It relieved the sufferings of the Devas. It restored their world to the Devas. Such is the greatness of Lord Muruga's "Thadankai." "Vel Kezhu Thadankai Saal Perunjelva Kundram Kondra Kundraa Kotraththu" is from Thirumurugatruppadai. Thus, by the grace of Lord Muruga, who has a victorious peacock as his vehicle, a powerful spear that split Mount Krauncha, and large sacred hands of great glory, Nagan, the leader of the hunter clan who prayed to Lord Muruga for a child, had his wife Thaththai conceive. Lord Muruga, who is united with Valliammaiyar, bestows good grace, granting appropriate enjoyment and liberation to all living beings in the world. As if to illustrate this, those who prayed to Lord Muruga for a child began to receive what they desired. Sivakavimani further explains the nuances of these verses. While the Vedas praise Lord Shiva once as "Sadashivom," they praise Lord Kumara thrice as "Subrahmanyom, Subrahmanyom, Subrahmanyom." Similarly, here, the author, in this Tamil Veda, Periya Puranam, praises Lord Shiva once as "Purangal Setra Enthaiyaar." But he praises Lord Muruga thrice, associating him with his power of grace, the Vel, as "Sevvel Murugavel Porani Neduvellon Ayil Udai Thadankai Venri Annanaar." We must observe this uniqueness. Sekkizhar Swamigal has placed the news of the birth of a child by the grace of Lord Muruga as the twelfth verse in the Puranam of Kannappa Nayanar. As per "Pannirandu Kannudaiya Pillai Thondrum," Sekkizhar Swamigal, blessed with divine grace, has placed this meaning, which describes the manifestation of the grace of Lord Muruga, who has twelve sacred eyes and twelve sacred hands, in the twelfth verse of the Puranam of Kannappa Nayanar in the twelfth Thirumurai, Periya Puranam. This arrangement is celebrated as one of the divine special arrangements made by Lord Nataraja, the divine speaker, in the author's divine words. Similarly, the divine aspect of Lord Nataraja is found in other places as well. In the Puranam of Thirukurippu Thonda Nayanar, in the 51st verse, he describes the incident where the Mother (Parvati) performed penance in Kanchi and worshipped God. According to the saying "Aathi Ezhuththu Avai 50 Odu Ondru Enbar," the letters are 51. Therefore, Sekkizhar Swamigal places the incident where Goddess Parvati performed penance in Kanchi and worshipped God in the 51st verse of the Puranam of Thirukurippu Thonda Nayanar. There are 28 Shiva Agamas. Sekkizhar Swamigal has composed the history of Thirumoolar Nayanar, who revealed the meaning of those 28 Shiva Agamas in Tamil as Thirumantras, in 28 verses. All these are divine special arrangements made by Lord Nataraja. Therefore, this Periya Puranam, composed by Sekkizhar Swamigal, is not ordinary human speech. We must study it, realizing that it is the word of the Supreme Being, Pathi. We will continue to reflect. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 09 — Kannappa Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Periya Puranam, Part 132, Ilai Malindha Sarukkam, Kannappa Nayanar Puranam. By the grace of Lord Muruga, Kannappa Nayanar incarnates. Sekkizhar Swamigal describes this. Kannappa Nayanar incarnates to bring glory to the Kaanavar clan. Kaanavar are hunters, the people of the Kurinji land. By having Kannappa Nayanar, that hunter clan gained glory. It is the conviction of our elders that if a great soul, a great person, incarnates in a clan, that entire clan will be enlightened and saved by him. Arunachala Puranam states that Lord Shiva said, "We will grant liberation for 21 generations in the lineage of one who witnesses the Karthigai Jyothi in Thiruvannamalai." Therefore, if someone in a clan witnesses the Karthigai Jyothi in Thiruvannamalai, 21 generations in his lineage attain liberation through him. Thus, it is our ancestral tradition that if a great person incarnates in a clan, that entire clan will be enlightened and saved by him. By the grace of Lord Muruga, Thaththai conceives. Due to this, the entire clan rejoices. Since she is pregnant, the people of the hunter clan perform the appropriate rituals each month, praying for the pregnancy to progress well and for a healthy child to be born. Generally, in our tradition, when a woman in a household becomes pregnant, it was customary to perform various prayers and pujas each month, praying for the healthy growth of the fetus. This is because there are many chances for harm to occur to the fetus in each month. Therefore, it is customary for the pregnant woman and her family to pray that God protect the fetus from any harm specific to that month. Even in Thiruvachagam, in the Potti Thiru Agaval, Manikkavasagar Swamigal lists all the harms the fetus faces in each month and sings that they have survived all these harms. "Maanudap pirappil maatha udaraththu eenamil kirumich cheruvinil pizhaiththu orumathi thaanriyil irumaiyil pizhaiththum irumathi vilaivin orumaiyil pizhaiththum mummathi thannul ammatham pizhaiththum eeriru thingalil perirul pizhaiththum anju thingalil munjuthal pizhaiththum aaru thingalil ooralar pizhaiththum ezhu thingalil thaazh puvi pizhaiththum ettu thingalil kattamum pizhaiththum onpathil varutharu thunpamum pizhaiththum thakka thasamathi thaayodu thaanpadum thukka saagarath thuyaridai pizhaiththum" is from Potti Thiru Agaval. Let's look at its explanation. First, Manikkavasagar says, "Maanudap pirappinul maatha udaraththu eenamil kirumich cheruvinil pizhaiththum." He says that the fetus survives the battle waged by worms, which are germs, inside the mother's womb, without being destroyed. When the mother and father unite, the living organisms in the white fluid released from the father's body enter the mother's womb. At that point, to unite with the mother's ovum in the red fluid in the womb, a battle ensues. One organism pushes aside all the other organisms that came with it and, without being destroyed, goes into the mother's ovum and unites with it, doesn't it? Thus, one organism pushes aside various competing organisms and unites with the ovum inside the mother's womb. This is what he first refers to as "maatha udaraththu eenamil kirumich cheruvinil pizhaiththum." Next, he speaks about the suffering that occurs in the first month. He says, "orumathi thaanriyin irumaiyil pizhaiththum." That is, in the first month, the fetus is the size of a Thaanri fruit inside the mother's womb. Now, what harm can befall this fetus? If the mother's and father's energies do not unite well and there is a difference, then harm can befall the fetus. There is a chance for the pregnancy to miscarry. This is what he refers to as "orumathi thaanriyin irumaiyil pizhaiththum." Now, what harm can occur in the second month? From what harm do we escape and survive, says Manikkavasagar. He says, "irumathi vilaivin orumaiyil pizhaiththum." The explanation for this is that in the second month, the fetus escapes a certain kind of harm that occurs when the remaining bindu (seminal fluid) enters the place where nadam (sound) and bindu have united and developed. Even where the father's and mother's gametes have united, if other male gametes enter and cause harm, there is a chance for the fetus to be destroyed. To prevent this, the mother's ovum needs to be sealed. At this time, due to illness, dizziness, or other reasons, if a male gamete enters the mother's womb, there is a chance that it could be dangerous to the existing fetus. Therefore, Manikkavasagar Swamigal sings that we have survived such suffering. A similar verse appears in Potti Palthodai: "Eppirappum munsei iruvinaiyal nischayiththu porppudaiya thanthai thaai pogaththul garppamaai pullil panipol pugunthu valaikku utpadungal ellaipadaraa udarath theendiya thee palvagaiyal ange kidantha anaathi uyir thampasiyal engeyumaga edukkum ena vem kumbhi kaayak karukkuzhiyil kaaththirunthum." This is a verse from the sacred text Potti Palthodai, lines 13 to 19. This is the meaning of the phrase "irumathi vilaivin orumaiyil pizhaiththum." Next, Manikkavasagar Swamigal sings about the danger we have escaped from in the third month. He says, "mummathi thannil ammatham pizhaiththum." As mentioned above, when the gametes unite and the opening is sealed, and the fetus grows, the mother's uterine fluid will secrete inside the womb for the growth of the fetus. This uterine fluid should secrete in the required amount. If it secretes excessively, there is a chance for the womb to open and for a miscarriage to occur. This is the test or suffering that comes to the fetus in the third month. The Swamigal sings that we have escaped and survived this. Next, he sings about the fourth month. He says, "eeriru thingalil perirul pizhaiththum." In the fourth month, the uterine fluid secreted inside the womb, after the necessary portion for fetal growth, remains as waste and appears dark. If this dark waste accumulates excessively, there is a high chance of harm to the fetus due to its toxicity. This is what he refers to as "eeriru thingalil perirul pizhaiththum." "Irul" means darkness, doesn't it? Next, in the fifth month, he sings, "anju thingalil munjuthal pizhaiththum." In the fifth month, if the uterine fluid and its dark waste, which accumulates in the womb, become excessive, it is difficult for the fetus to survive. Therefore, he says, "anju thingalil munjuthal pizhaiththum." Now, in the sixth month, he sings, "aaru thingalil ooralal pizhaiththum." In the sixth month, the fetus, which was a lump, develops organs like hands well. At that time, the womb needs to expand to accommodate the growing fetus. If the womb does not provide enough space for this expansion, then the fetus will be harmed. Manikkavasagar Swamigal says that they survived this by saying, "aaru thingalil ooralal pizhaiththum." Next, he says, "ezhu thingalil thaazh puvi pizhaiththum." This is the suffering that can occur in the seventh month. In the seventh month, almost all the organs of the fetus would have developed and grown. Therefore, there is a chance that the womb might not be able to bear the fetus and the opening might occur, causing harm to the fetus. Manikkavasagar Swamigal sings that they survived this by saying, "ezhu thingalil thaazh puvi pizhaiththum." Next, he sings about the suffering that comes in the eighth month as "ettam thingalil kattamum pizhaiththum." The Sanskrit word "kashtam" becomes "kattam" in Tamil. In the eighth month, due to the very rapid growth of the fetus, the excessive accumulation of waste products from the uterine fluid that nourishes it, and the density of the food consumed by the mother, the fetus experiences distress. Manikkavasagar Swamigal sings that they survived all these sufferings by saying, "ettam thingalil kattamum pizhaiththum." Next, he sings about the suffering that comes in the ninth month as "onpathil varutharu thunpamum pizhaiththum." He describes escaping from the sufferings that arise from the excess of the previously mentioned factors in the ninth month. The tenth month, that is, the tenth month, is the appropriate time for the birth of the child. Therefore, he says, "thakka dasamathi thaayodu thaanpadum thukka saagara thuyaridai pizhaiththum." He sings that they survived the great ocean-like suffering experienced by the mother giving birth in the tenth month, and also the suffering that the fetus itself, as a child, experiences when coming out into the world. Thus, Manikkavasagar Swamigal, in Potti Thiru Agaval, sings about all the sufferings a child escapes and survives from the time it is conceived until it is born into the world. "Maanudap pirappinul maatha udaraththu eenamil kirumich cheruvinil pizhaiththum orumathi thaanriyin irumaiyil pizhaiththum irumathi vilaivil orumaiyil pizhaiththum mummathi thannul ammatham pizhaiththum eeriru thingalil perirul pizhaiththum anju thingalil munjuthal pizhaiththum aaru thingalil ooralal pizhaiththum ezhu thingalil thaazh puvi pizhaiththum ettu thingalil kattamum pizhaiththum onpathil varutharu thunpamum pizhaiththum thakka dasamathi thaayodu thaanpadum thukka saagara thuyaridai pizhaiththum" - these are all sung by Manikkavasagar Swamigal in Potti Thiru Agaval. He does not stop there but continues to sing about the sufferings a child experiences at each stage after birth. However, the explanations of those parts are beyond the scope of the section we are currently looking at in Periya Puranam. This is because Sekkizhar Swamigal states, doesn't he, that the hunters perform worship every month to protect the fetus from the harms that come each month, so that Thaththai's pregnancy grows beautifully without any suffering? That is why we reflected on this part of Thiruvachagam, which is related to it. Devotees can find other parts of Manikkavasagar Swamigal's Potti Thiru Agaval, which lists all the sufferings that come to a child even after birth. Thus, Thaththai's pregnancy grew beautifully each month, and by the penance performed in previous births, Thaththai gave birth to a beautiful baby boy. By the grace of Lord Muruga, Kannappadeva Nayanar incarnated there. Sekkizhar Swamigal continues to sing about how Kannappadeva Nayanar grew up. We will continue to reflect. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 10 — Kannappa Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Periya Puranam, Part 133, Ilai Malinda Sarukkam, Kannappa Nayanar Puranam. Sekkizhar Swamigal states that Kannappa Nayanar incarnated due to immeasurable penance. Tattai and Naga performed immeasurable penance to have Kannappa Nayanar as their son. The hunter tribe performed immeasurable penance to have Kannappa Nayanar born into their lineage. The southern region performed great penance for Kannappa Deva Nayanar to incarnate there. Not only that, all living beings performed penance for Kannappa Deva Nayanar to be born on this earth. Thus, Kannappa Deva Nayanar incarnated due to the strength and benefit of various immeasurable penances. When the Milk Ocean was churned, didn't the moon emerge from it? Similarly, just as the dark ocean, desiring to give birth to a moon like the Milk Ocean, performed penance and received a moon, Tattai gave birth to Kannappa Deva Nayanar. The moon born from the Milk Ocean is a white moon, a pale moon. But this moon, born to Tattai, is Thinnanar, Kannappa Nayanar, who is dark-skinned. The moon born from the Milk Ocean waxes and wanes. But this moon, Kannappa Deva Nayanar, born to Tattai, grows to 16 kalais (phases), meaning he grows to 16 years of age, and then attains the bliss of liberation, a state where there is no waxing or waning. That moon was protected by God. This moon, Kannappa Deva, is always by God's side, never leaving Him, and acts as His protector. Therefore, here the ocean is compared to Tattai, and the moon emerging from the ocean is compared to the Nayanar. When a full moon appears, it first shows a reddish hue. Children, as they grow, will in later days attain their true color. But any child, immediately after coming out of the mother's womb, will have a reddish color due to the fresh blood circulation. Here too, Sekkizhar Swamigal, who described the child as a "milk moon" when the mother gave birth, compares Kannappa Nayanar to a dark cloud when his father lifted him in his arms. Furthermore, Sivakavimani writes another reason why Kannappa Deva Nayanar is called the moon. Lord Muruga incarnated from the spark of fire that emerged from Shiva's third eye. He shines like the sun, as described by "palar pugazh nyayiru kadarkkandaangu" (the sun praised by many, seen in the ocean). This child, Kannappa Nayanar, was born and developed through the grace of Lord Muruga. Therefore, he is like the moon. Isn't it a scientific truth now that the moon shines only with the light of the sun? Similarly, it is highly appropriate to compare this child, who appeared by receiving the light of Lord Muruga, the sun, and by His grace, to the moon. The hunters, upon learning that a male child was born to their leader Naga, rejoiced greatly. They showered pearls from large elephant tusks like rain. Similarly, they showered pearls of rich moisture from bamboo like rain. The hunters, in their joy, showered all other gems found in the mountains like rain and celebrated. Our leader was without a child for a long time. Now, a male child has been born to illuminate our leader's lineage and to signify the arrival of a new young leader for us, hasn't it? The meaning of this is that Naga, the leader of the hunters, and all the hunters, his subjects, showered pearls and other gems like rain upon beggars in their joy. Gems might be priceless objects for those living in cities and towns. But for the mountain-dwelling hunters, numerous gems are naturally available in the mountainous regions. That is why they shower them like rain and give them to beggars. Here, the subjects of the hunter king rejoice and celebrate the birth of his son. Then the king invites his subjects to his son's archery festival and honors them. Afterwards, the subjects present their grievances to the king. Then Naga asks them to accept his son as their king. We must note these acts of royal loyalty between the king and his subjects. We can see this same message in the Puranic histories of Manu Neethi Chozhan, Kalarittarivar, Murthi Nayanar, and others. In these days, we must compare and reflect on the internal enmity that arises, spreading throughout the country, aiming to destroy and harass the crowns of kings, which is contrary to this. Now, with the incarnation of Kannappa Deva Nayanar, the Devas from the heavens shower Kalpaka flower rain. Similarly, when the Nayanar attained liberation, the Devas showered Kalpaka flower rain. When other Nayanars attained liberation, the Devas would similarly shower Kalpaka flower rain from the heavens. We have read that divine drums would resound. But in this Purana, the Devas shower Kalpaka flower rain even when the Nayanar incarnates. Generally, the Saivite tradition is to celebrate the day when great souls join the feet of God and attain liberation. Celebrating birthdays (Jayanti) is not a Saivite tradition. That is why Guru Puja is performed for each Nayanar on the auspicious star day of their liberation. Similarly, we have read that when each Nayanar attains liberation, these Devas shower Kalpaka flower rain and divine drums resound. But here, these events occur even when Kannappa Deva Nayanar incarnates. This is because the Nayanar's incarnation itself is as glorious as liberation, which is why Sekkizhar Swamigal has sung about the showering of flowers from the heavens and the resounding of divine drums. We must also note that this happens similarly during the incarnation of Thirugnanasambandha Murthy. Father Naga arrives. He holds the newborn child in his sacred hands. This is like a dark cloud resting on a large dark mountain. Naga, with his strength, valor, and physique, is like a dark mountain. The child he holds in his hand is also dark-skinned. Sekkizhar Swamigal compares it to a dark cloud waiting to shower rain on a mountain peak. This sight of a dark cloud laden with water standing on a mountain peak is like the world being saved by the great rain showered by the cloud. Didn't this child, in later times, save the entire world with the rain of love showered by the cloud, due to the penance performed on the mountain peak? Perhaps that is why Sekkizhar Swamigal made this comparison. The newborn child is dark-skinned. It has a beautiful dark body. This dark sacred body is a radiant body that emits light. Generally, objects of other colors, when struck by the sun's rays, are said to reflect the light of their own color from the seven colors in the ray. Therefore, they appear in that color. But black color, unlike that, absorbs all the rays of the sun and does not reflect them outwards, as optical scientists now state, don't they? But here, Sekkizhar Swamigal sings that even though the Nayanar's body is dark, it shines brightly. This child, born with a dark complexion, is a beautiful child. It is a child that evokes affection in all who see it. Can dark-colored objects also be beautiful? Yes. Isn't Manmatha, who is praised as the most beautiful in this universe, dark-skinned? Isn't Thirumal, the father of Manmatha, also dark-skinned? Don't we read that the bodies of Rama and Krishna, two incarnations praised as the best among the ten incarnations of Thirumal, were very dark and beautiful? Therefore, black is also beautiful. That is why Sekkizhar Swamigal praises the Nayanar as "karum kadir virikkum meni kaamar kuzhavi" (a beautiful child with a dark, radiant body). The newborn child is like a tiger cub. This child possesses all the good characteristics described in the sciences of body features like Samudrika Lakshanam and palmistry. There are many signs in the child that indicate great and rare qualities, difficult to measure or obtain in this world. When we reflect on the glory of the Thirukalathi Puranam, we recall how many great personalities praised and revered Kannappa Deva Nayanar in various texts, didn't we? Sekkizhar Swamigal subtly sings here that this child has incarnated in a way that is praised by all great and wise people. Father Naga takes the child Nayanar in his sacred hands. Since the child is difficult to hold, very strong, heavy, and firm, father Naga names this child Thinnan. Therefore, this is a name given based on the Nayanar's characteristic. It is a common practice in the world to name someone based on various reasons such as ancestral name, family name, grandfather's name, or a characteristic. Since this child was firm (thin), his father named him Thinnan. Indeed, his father named him Thinnanar based on the firmness of the child's body. In reality, this name Thinnanar is also very appropriate due to the firmness of the penance the Nayanar performed in many previous births and the firmness of the intense love the Nayanar possessed. Now, the mountain-dwelling people worship the mountain deities to protect this child, and accordingly, they perform all the necessary rituals according to their ancient customs and traditions. They place iron-mixed ornaments on the child's hands and feet. Thus, this act of protection varies from land to land and tradition to tradition. Sekkizhar Swamigal, after mentioning the naming ceremony, the first ritual for the child, in the first verse, is now going to describe the child's growth through subsequent stages. He first describes the "kaappu paruvam" (protection stage), which is the first of these stages. Similarly, Sekkizhar Swamigal will sing about the other stages up to the first year in the following verses, and about his development beyond the first year, including his archery training, in subsequent verses. Let us continue to reflect. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 11 — Kannappa Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Periya Puranam, Part 134, Ilai Malinda Sarukkam, Kannappa Nayanar Puranam. The parents perform the "kaappu" (protection ritual) for the child Thinnanar. They fashion neem seeds and pieces of neem wood into beads, stringing them together to form a multi-beaded waist-cord. Sivakavimani writes that in ancient times, people living in different regions led natural lives, using the resources available in their respective lands. Thus, they fashioned ornaments and protective charms for their children from the materials found in their areas, thereby shaping their way of life. Tamil grammar categorized these as "mudhal," "karu," and "uriporutkal" (primary, secondary, and inherent elements) for each "thinai" (landscape). There are no records anywhere indicating that even a small item was imported from foreign countries for use. He writes that it is regrettable that people now, under the guise of civilization, use all kinds of products available from all countries and make it their way of life, which is an obscene and uncivilized way of living. Thus, they perform protection rituals and adorn the children. Not only that, but as the child grows through different stages, they perform grand pujas to the forest deities. They pile up and offer various items like fruits, tubers, and meat to the forest deities, worshipping them. Thus, the child completes its first year and enters its second year. The end of the first year is the stage of unsteady walking. The second year is the stage when the child walks and plays. We still have sayings like "ettavathu maadham eduthu adi vaikkkum" (takes a step in the eighth month) and "ettavathu maadham ettu vaikkkum" (places eight steps in the eighth month). So, in the eighth month, the child learns to walk by holding the hands of its mother, nurses, etc. After four months, when the first year ends and the second year begins, the child reaches the stage of walking unsteadily without any support. For such a growing and walking child, they tie a tiger's claw as a "chutti" (ornament). According to the hunter tradition, a "chutti" is an ornament worn on the forehead by young boys. Even today, it is customary in many families to tie a single tiger's claw or two claws facing each other, set in gold, and wear it on the chest of young boys. This is a child who has completed one year, so the hair on its head will be short and sparse. Therefore, we must remember that Sekkizhar sings "punthalai siru magaarkal" (small children with sparse hair). This sparse, small hair will later grow into curly, well-defined locks, which is common. They cut the long quills of a porcupine into small pieces of equal length and string them into a garland for the child. They adorn the child with a "sathangai" (anklet with bells) made of many beads. They adorn it with a "thandai" (anklet) made of elephant tusk. "Kudambai" is a type of earring, an ornament worn on the ear. They adorn it with "kudambai" earrings made of various beads. Thus adorned, the child, possessing great Shiva-punyam (merit from devotion to Shiva) that removes all faults of those who see it, plays there, displaying its meritorious form. The child plays in the street. When it sees its parents, it runs quickly to them and cuddles with them. This makes its father and mother very happy and joyful. The child speaks in babbling language. As per Thirukkural, "Makkal mei theendal udalukku inbam matru avar sol kettal inbam sevikku" (The touch of children is joy to the body, and hearing their words is joy to the ear), and "Kuzhal inithu yaazh inithu enbar tham makkal mazhalai sol kelaathavar" (Those who have not heard the babbling words of their children say that the flute is sweet and the lyre is sweet). It is explained that parents are very happy to hear the babbling language of children, isn't it? Naga and Tattai experience such happiness. Hearing the babbling words of Kannappa Nayanar, Thinnanar, how do these babbling words of Thinnanar come? They come mixed with the sacred water in his mouth. Lord Shiva later says in his history that the water in Thinnanar's mouth seems more sacred than the Ganges to him. So, these babbling words come from Thinnanar's mouth water, which is more sacred than the Ganges water. It is natural for babbling words to come mixed with the child's saliva, isn't it? Thus, Thinnanar grows slowly. It is a hunter settlement, isn't it? We have already seen that hunters keep watch animals in their homes, haven't we? Similarly, there is a tiger as a watch animal in Naga's house. Tigers and other such animals usually have the habit of keeping their mouths wide open. This watch animal, the tiger, also keeps its mouth wide open. Thinking this widely open mouth to be a cave, the child Thinnanar puts his golden hand into the tiger's open mouth. What kind of hand is Thinnanar's hand? A golden hand. In later days, this hand hunted and offered food to Lord Shiva and protected Him. This hand removed the suffering of the two luminaries, the sun and the moon. In later days, didn't Kannappa Nayanar pluck out his own eyes and apply them to stop the blood flowing from the Lord's sacred eyes? Aren't the sun and moon the two sacred eyes of the Lord? Therefore, wasn't Kannappa Devanayar the one who saw the flow of blood from the Lord's eyes, which are the sun and moon, and removed it? Wasn't it Kannappa Nayanar's golden hands that offered food to the Lord, protected Him, and removed the suffering of the sun and moon? The child Thinnanar, thinking the tiger's wide-open mouth to be a cave, puts his golden hand inside it. The affectionate father sees this. This is Thinnanar, his son, born after a long period of penance, destined to inherit his position. So, if he puts his hand in the tiger's mouth, it could cause great harm, couldn't it? Seeing this, he became agitated and pretended to strike him with a soft branch. His father pretends to hit him as a punishment. Naga, instead of raising a strong stick to hit his son who put his hand in the tiger's mouth, raises a soft branch to hit him. He doesn't hit him but pretends to. This is due to his immense affection for his son Thinnanar. Seeing this, the child Thinnanar becomes sad, and tears well up in his eyes. Then, the tears fall like pearls. His mother, Tattai, catches these pearl-like tears with her kiss. Kissing the mouth in this way is a stage called "mutthap paruvam" (kissing stage) among the ten stages of childhood. Next, Thinnanar plays. He plays with wooden toys shaped like drums. He rolls small wooden blocks and makes a small chariot to play with. This is the "siru ther paruvam" (small chariot stage) in Pillaitamil literature. Next, the hunter girls build small sand houses and play. Thinnanar plays by kicking these small sand houses with his sacred feet. This is the "sitril paruvam" (small house stage) mentioned in Pillaitamil literature. He plays with the dogs in the hunter settlements. He plays by holding, pulling, and cutting the ropes that tie the dogs. All these described events are from the child's life up to two and a half years of age. Similarly, boys make small drums and beat them. This child also plays by beating such small drums. Thinnanar, the child, grows up learning to walk and playing small games in every hunter's hut. Thus, playing many games, he grew up and now completed five years. He steps into his sixth year. Up to five years, children crawl, walk, and play inside the house, outside the house, and in nearby houses. After five years, when the sixth year begins, children go a little further and then run and play over long distances. Sekkizhar Peruman sings about this in the upcoming verses. Similarly, the child Thinnanar also plays by crossing fences made of elephant tusks. In the outskirts of the village, there will be small forests with sparse trees. Hunter boys, when they first come out of the village, practice dancing and play there. Fierce large animals like tigers and elephants do not live in these small forests. Since this area is adjacent to human settlements, small animals like rabbits and small pigs will be in this small forest area. Our young Thinnanar plays beautifully with the young rabbits, wild boar piglets, tiger cubs, and wild dog pups in this small forest area on the outskirts of the village. Didn't they already keep and raise watch animals like deer, rabbits, pigs, tigers, and wild dogs in the settlement area? So their young ones will be there. They play freely with them in that small forest area. An old hunter woman, a Kurathi, who is very old in the hunter community, has long experience in raising small children. So his parents entrust this boy to her care. The old Kurathi feeds the young Thinnanar in the evening. In the evening, she feeds him food after circling him with mustard smoke. Then, after nightfall, she gives him meat and then lets the boy play for a while before putting him to sleep. Thus, the growing boy Thinnanar reached the age for learning archery. Thus, father Naga was very happy to see his son Thinnanar reach the age of learning archery. Embracing his son Thinnanar with his strong shoulders, he decided to teach him archery and brought an old man skilled in that field. Naga performed penance to have this son. He was happy because he got the child through penance, wasn't he? He is happy to see Thinnanar grow up well. He is also happy to see Thinnanar grow up to be worthy of inheriting his chieftainship of the hunter tribe. Therefore, he expresses his joy by embracing Thinnanar so that their shoulders meet. When it was time for him to learn archery, Naga, the leader of the hunter tribe, also made arrangements for Thinnanar to learn archery. Let us continue to reflect. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 12 — Kannappa Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Periya Puranam, Part 135, Ilai Malinda Sarukkam, Kannappa Nayanar Puranam. Thinnanar is growing beautifully. He has reached the age of 12. Therefore, Naga, the hunter king and Thinnanar's father, decides that this is the right time and the right age to teach Thinnanar archery. It is a rule that everyone should learn their respective education at the appropriate age. The age at which one begins to learn varies depending on the skill being taught. Generally, educational training begins around the age of five, which is the tradition. The Chandeasar Puranam, "Aindhu varuda malarkkanaiya," also mentions this. But here, archery is being taught to the Nayanar. Archery is a physical art. Therefore, one must begin this archery training only after having attained the necessary physical development. That is why Naga, his father, decides to give Thinnanar archery training as soon as he reaches 12 years of age. Archery is the best skill for hunters. It is through the strength of this archery that one becomes qualified to lead the hunter tribe. If leadership comes to an unqualified person, they will struggle to bear it and may lose that leadership position. Furthermore, the inner meanings, such as the need to learn arts at the appropriate age, are all mentioned here. To teach Thinnanar archery, Naga appoints an old man who is very skilled and experienced in that field as his teacher. Naga makes this decision to begin teaching Thinnanar archery after consulting with other experienced elder hunters. He also sets an auspicious day. Then Naga, the king of the hunters, sends word to everyone about this news. He sends messengers to everyone, announcing that Thinnanar is going to take up the bow by the grace of Kandavel. To announce major events to the public, governments would beat drums and other instruments, and then announce the news to the public who gathered upon hearing the sound. Here, the hunters announce the news that Thinnanar is going to take up the bow by beating a "thudi" (a type of drum) in the hunter community. This news is also announced to the leaders of the respective communities in the neighboring mountains. Hearing this, all the kings of the hunter tribe ruling in the neighboring mountains, bring their mountain-produced goods such as gems, gold, pearls, tiger skins, elephant tusks, loads of peacock feathers, honey, various kinds of liquor, meat, fruits, and tubers, and come from all directions to Thinnanar's Uduppur. Numerous hunters skilled in archery also come. Sekkizhar Swamigal sings in many verses about how elaborately the ceremony to begin archery training is celebrated here. "Vil pidithal" (holding the bow) is the first practice taught when beginning archery training. Just as in education, one first learns to pronounce letters orally and write them by hand, in archery, one is first taught how to hold the bow. Thus, all the people from the hunter tribe in the neighboring countries arrive at Uduppur for Thinnanar's bow-holding ceremony. A huge crowd gathers, too large to be contained within the small village of Uduppur. All relatives, friendly kings, and people from neighboring countries and mountains come. Naga welcomes everyone and asks them to perform many "paravukkadan" (vows and offerings) to the deity and hold the archery festival. Naga honors all the relatives who came, entrusts them with responsibilities, and asks them to conduct this seven-day festival. The archery festival, from performing "kaappu" (protection ritual) for the student and the bow he will hold, to the act of teaching the student how to hold the bow, is celebrated as a seven-day festival. First, they perform "paravukkadan" to the deity. Here, the deity refers to Lord Muruga, the god of the Kurinji land. So, they perform pujas to Lord Muruga according to their custom, and perform "anangaadal," "veriyaadal," "kundrak kuravai," etc., to fulfill their "paravukkadan." The artisans who teach archery hand the bow to Thinnanar. A special bow was made by skilled experts specifically for Thinnanar's training. Sekkizhar Peruman says that the bow was made "paanmaiyil samaithuk kondu" (made according to its nature). "Paanmai" explicitly means "according to the method" or "according to the rule" of how the bow should be made. "Paanmai" also means "fate" or "destiny." Therefore, we must also see the inner meaning here that the Nayanar receives this bow to begin archery training according to the divine rule of fate. Why does the Nayanar begin archery training here due to fate? Because it is related to the Nayanar's previous birth. We will discuss this in detail at the appropriate time in the latter part of this Purana. Thus, the artisans make the bow according to the prescribed method and give it to Thinnanar's hands. Lord Shiva once held a bow, the Meru mountain bow. He held it to burn the three cities (Thiripuram). The Devas used that bow of Lord Shiva to churn the Milk Ocean as a churning rod. Although it was the Mandara mountain that churned the Milk Ocean, why is it called Meru mountain here? Because Meru mountain is the king of all mountains, so Sekkizhar Peruman refers to Mandara mountain as Meru as a respectful designation. The Devas churned the Milk Ocean using the Meru mountain, the bow held by Lord Shiva. By churning with that bow, instead of nectar, poison came out. Lord Shiva consumed that poison and became Thiruneelakanthar (the blue-throated one). So, Lord Shiva received poison from the bow he held. But when the same bow came into Thinnanar's hands, this bow would bring nectar to Lord Shiva. Because, holding this bow, with the help of its arrow, the Nayanar offers nectar to Lord Shiva in the latter part of the story. So, the Nayanar's bow brought nectar to Lord Shiva. But the Devas gave poison to Lord Shiva through the Meru mountain, the bow held by Lord Shiva. Therefore, the verse implies that this bow is more glorious than that bow. That Meru bow turned nectar into poison. But this bow in the Nayanar's sacred hand helped to turn undesirable, harmful, forbidden meat into nectar and offer it to Lord Shiva. Thus, Sekkizhar Swamigal composes this verse very artfully. Now, they tie "kaappu" (protection thread) to Thinnanar and the bow in his hand. They tie "kaappu" to both the bow and Thinnanar with the nerve of a single tiger. That is, the "kaappu" tied to the bow was made from the nerve of a certain tiger, and the "kaappu" for Thinnanar was also tied with the nerve of the same tiger. This seems to be a tradition of the hunters. They tie the "kaappu" on the beautiful forearm of Thinnanar, who incarnated to glorify the hunter tribe. All the mountain-dwelling hunters offered their traditional blessings to Thinnanar. Now, Sekkizhar Swamigal also sings about the types of food cooked and served to everyone who attended this festival. Rice cooked from mountain paddy, various kinds of millet rice, fine rice from thinai (foxtail millet) found in dense mountains, round rice from bamboo, thus, various kinds of rice made from different grains are cooked by the hunters. Along with this rice, tubers and meat mixed with rice are served there. The cooked food is piled up like a mountain in various places. All the hunters who came, and even the local people, eat this food heartily. Next, he describes what snacks they ate. They eat red millet flour mixed with honey. They eat roasted meat dipped in honey. They mix wood apple with honey, knead it into balls, and eat it. They eagerly eat dead flying ants. Thus, Sekkizhar Swamigal says they eat various kinds of food. The "senthinai maavu" (red millet flour) mentioned here is the finest millet flour. Just as "sennel" (red paddy) is considered superior among paddy, "senthinai" is superior among millets. They grind this "senthinai" into flour and eat it mixed with honey. Honey is a natural food that provides sweetness among the six tastes. It is abundant in the mountainous regions, which are "kurinji nilam" (mountainous landscape), and is very good for health. Honey is a food item that provides taste, fragrance, and medicinal properties all at once. Therefore, medical texts assert that there is no other food item as beneficial as honey. That is why the hunters here consume honey in various ways. They eat honey mixed with millet flour. Eating meat dipped in honey is considered a superior food among them. This is why, later, the Nayanar also offers meat mixed with honey to the Lord, saying, "Lord, this is a very delicious food." Similarly, they mix honey with wood apple and eat it in balls. We have already seen the properties of wood apple and the wood apple tree. Next, he says they ate flying ants. It is a tradition among mountain dwellers to collect large swarms of flying ants, prepare them like sundal (a dish of boiled legumes), and eat them. It is one of their food items. Flying ants emerge in large swarms from anthills, like "pulapulana kalakalana puttreesal pola" (like swarms of ants from an anthill), and die within a short time. They collect these dead flying ants in a basket, process them, and eat them mixed with honey. This verse states that they eat this with great relish. Thus, Sekkizhar Swamigal, like a mirror, shows us many aspects of the hunters' food types, their eating habits, food traditions, eating methods, and their enthusiasm for food, among other aspects of their lifestyle. The hunters ate more food than their usual capacity. This is because it was a celebration arranged by their leader with love. So, they ate until their stomachs were full and even more. Not only did the hunters eat food, but there is also a mention that they drank various kinds of liquor. Many kinds of liquor were consumed by them, causing them to become intoxicated and lie scattered here and there. Although these are various types of liquor, they were condemned by scholars. But in those days, in ancient times, it was customary for people of lower status to consume them during celebrations, which we can understand. Let us continue to reflect. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 13 — Kannappa Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Periya Puranam, Part 136, Ilai Malinda Sarukkam, Kannappa Nayanar Puranam. Sekkizhar Swamigal describes how the festival for Thinnanar to hold the bow is celebrated. The Eiyittiyar and Eiyinar, meaning the hunter women and hunter men, participate in this festival and bless Thinnanar. Although the archery festival is a ritual for men, women also participate with their partners and bless Thinnanar. From this, we can understand that even among ancient tribal people, there was a tradition of couples coming together to offer blessings. Furthermore, Sekkizhar Swamigal sings by placing women first, then men, i.e., Eiyittiyar, then Eiyinar, indicating the rights given to women by the ancient tribal people. The mountain dwellers wear ornaments made of leather, like a belt, crafted from various gems found in the mountains. This is generally tied around the waist to hold weapons like swords. Since it is made of leather, it is very strong and sturdy. Similarly, they wear garlands made of various green leaves found in the mountains. A large garland made of green leaves would cover the body to some extent. They also wear Vetchi flowers. Vetchi garland is characteristic of the Kurinji's Purathinai (external aspect), so they wear Vetchi flowers. They wear garlands made of Vetchi flowers. Furthermore, they offer Vetchi garlands made of Vetchi flowers to Lord Muruga, praying and blessing that Thinnanar's training may go well. To give training, to start the bow-holding ceremony, the good teacher is there, isn't he? The people gather around that place. They beat small Thondagam drums. They play horns. They sound Udukkai drums. They play flutes. Thus, a great sound filled everywhere. While making these sounds, everyone, dressed in various clothes, goes around the town in a procession. At that time, the hunters perform Varikuthu dance. The hunter women perform Thunangai Kuthu dance. Divine women dance there in a terrifying manner. In the archery festival, the teacher first holds the bow that will be given to the student, celebrates the festival, and at the end, gives it to the student's hand and begins to teach the first skill of archery, how to hold it. Therefore, this ritual begins at noon on the first day. The midday, when the sun is at its zenith, is considered an auspicious time for hunters according to their tradition. Therefore, this ritual begins at midday on the first day of the seven-day archery festival. Midday is also considered a very auspicious time by astrological texts. Astrologers call this "Abhijit Muhurtham." This Abhijit Muhurtham is about 15 Nazhigai (6 hours) from sunrise. It is important everywhere. It is important in every country. It is capable of removing all doshas (malefic influences). We can find this in astrological texts like Kalaprakasikai and Kalavidhanam. The time when "kaappu" was tied to the bow and to Thinnanar, the student, at the beginning of the archery festival, was this Abhijit Muhurtham, the midday. Similarly, the time when Thinnanar was made to hold the bow at the end of the archery festival on the seventh day was also the same time. Not only that, but at the end of this Purana, the time when the Nayanar ascended Thirukalathi mountain and had darshan of Kalathinathar was also this midday. This Abhijit Muhurtham is the time when the full heat of the sun impacts and provides its benefits to living beings, i.e., the time when the sun provides its full benefit. Similarly, astrologers say that the time of sunrise and sunset is called "Gothuli Lagnam" and this Muhurtham also has no doshas. This Gothuli Lagnam (time) is free from any doshas related to Thithi, Varam, Nakshatram, Yogam, and Karanam. It is stated in the astrological text Kalavidhana Paddhati that all actions performed during Gothuli Lagnam will be successful. The fact that the hunters also follow this tradition prevalent in the country, this tradition mentioned in astrological texts, serves as an example of how deeply Vedic culture and Vedic discipline permeated throughout Bharat Desam. All the aforementioned celebrations last for seven days. The seventh-day celebration is twice as grand as the usual celebration. Seven-day festivals are also mentioned in Agamas. The final festival performed on the seventh day is called "Thiruvizha Ganam." The concluding festival is celebrated with twice the grandeur of what happened for the six days. All the people who came bless with their mouths. Auspicious shouts are heard everywhere. The hunters teach Thinnanar to hold the bow by the hand of their ancient, traditional teacher. Antiquity and traditional lineage are very essential for the teaching profession. This is the firm decision of our ancestors. It is not our tradition to consider someone who has no tradition or lineage, but is new, as a great teacher. Our ancestors would not have done so. For isn't the proverb "Kulavithai kallamal paagam padum" (Family skill will be perfected without learning)? Therefore, it is very essential for the teaching profession to be hereditary. With a good teacher from that ancient teaching tradition, they made Thinnanar hold the bow that had been tied with "kaappu" and began his archery training. Furthermore, Sekkizhar Swamigal uses the plural "marabu vazhi vantha aasiyargal" (teachers who came through tradition) here. Therefore, it is implied here that there were many teachers skilled in teaching archery, and Thinnanar touched and held the bow from all of them and received their blessings. The phrase "aasill aasiyan" (flawless teacher) also indicates that among these many teachers, only one excellent and suitable teacher was accepted. Thus, the seven-day festival concluded. Thinnanar's archery training continued daily in a field on the mountain slopes outside Uduppur. Thinnanar, worshipping the bow, the teacher who taught it, and Lord Muruga, the presiding deity, practices archery every day. Thus, Thinnanar learns and masters archery completely. Isn't it a rule to learn any art or any education completely? What does it mean to learn completely? It means to fully acquire the benefits of learning. What is the benefit of learning? Thirukkural says, "Katradhanaal aaya payan enkol vaalarivan natraal thozhaar enin" (What is the use of learning if one does not worship the good feet of the truly wise?). The meaning of this Kural is that the benefit of education is to worship God. Sekkizhar Swamigal says that Thinnanar also learned this art of archery completely. By learning this archery completely, Thinnanar later uses this archery to offer food to God. Furthermore, he uses this bow to guard God at night. Isn't he going to worship the feet of God, the feet of Kudumithevar, using the archery he learned? That is why Sekkizhar Swamigal says that Thinnanar learned archery completely. Sekkizhar Swamigal says that Thinnanar, who rules him, learned archery completely. "Ennai aalum Thinnanar" (Thinnanar who rules me) is Sekkizhar Peruman's poetic expression. This Thinnanar, who has completely learned and mastered archery, at this point, Sekkizhar Peruman offers a salutation to the Nayanar. After offering salutations, he continues the story. If we divide the Nayanar's story into two parts, one related to worldly attachment and the other to Shiva's attachment, this is where the part related to worldly attachment ends. After this is completed, the Nayanar goes hunting. Then he is about to attain Shiva's attachment. That part begins. Therefore, since this part of the Nayanar's worldly attachment has ended and the part of the Nayanar's Shiva attachment is about to begin, Sekkizhar Swamigal offers a salutation to the Nayanar at this point and continues. It is our Agamic tradition and rule to perform "nyasams" (ritualistic placements) before performing Shiva Puja, i.e., Shiva worship. Because before performing Shiva Puja, we are in worldly attachment. We are engaged in worldly matters. Thus, before performing Shiva Puja, before entering the rituals of Shiva Puja, before beginning to perform the rituals, we purify ourselves with "nyasams" and then enter the Shiva Puja rituals. This is an Agamic rule. Similarly, Sekkizhar Peruman begins the next part by offering a salutation to the Nayanar, who is now about to attain Shiva's attachment. This is Sekkizhar Peruman's tradition. At this point, where the Nayanar's worldly history ends and the history of his Shiva attachment begins, we must recall the eighth Sutra of Sivagnanabodham, the primary text of all Saiva Siddhanta scriptures, composed by Meykandar Devar. "Aim pula vedarin ayarn thanai valarthu ena tham mudal guruvumaay thavathinil unartha vittu anniyam inmaiyum aran kazhal selume" (Having grown weary among the hunters of the five senses, the Lord, as the primal Guru, reveals through penance that there is no other, and leads to Shiva's feet). This explains how Shiva, as the Guru, takes possession of the soul. A king's son goes missing. That prince grows up in the forest with hunters. The king's son, growing up with hunters, forgets that he is a prince and that his father is the king ruling the country, and lives as a hunter for a long time. The king receives information about his son who had been missing for a long time. So the king wants to make his son realize that he is a prince, that he is not an ordinary hunter, and bring him back to the palace. But the king thinks that if he goes in royal attire, the prince, who has grown up as a hunter, might get scared and run away, and might not even realize the truth. So, how does he go there? He takes the form of a hunter, just like the hunters among whom his son lives. In the form of a hunter, he comes to the forest to search for his son. In the forest, he meets his son, who is a hunter in the hunter tribe. He advises his son, "You are not from the hunter community. You are the rightful ruler of this country. You are my son. Therefore, leave the company of these hunters and return to the palace." He makes him realize the truth. The king reveals that he is the king of this country and has come in the guise of a hunter. The king's son, who had grown up in the hunter community until then, realizes that he is a prince, that his leader is the king, and that his father is the king, and returns to the palace. He returns to the palace with the king. This is also how God takes possession of the soul. If God, to take possession of souls residing in human bodies, were to come in His true form, souls would not be able to recognize God. Therefore, God comes in a human form, just as the perfected soul resides in a human body. The God who comes in this way tells the soul, "You are growing up with the hunters of the five senses: body, mouth, eyes, nose, and ears. Because you have grown up with these five sensory hunters, you have reached this degraded state. You have become confused, not knowing who you are and where you came from." Thus, God comes as a Guru into the world and takes possession of the soul. Therefore, according to this Sutra, the part describing the growth of Thinnanar, the prince who actually grows up in the hunter's settlement, ends here. Now, according to the second part of the eighth Sutra of Sivagnanabodham, "thavathinil unartha vittu anniyam inmaiyin aran kazhal selume" (reveals through penance that there is no other, and leads to Shiva's feet), the story of the Nayanar's attainment of Shiva's attachment begins from here. Let us continue to reflect. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 14 — Kannappa Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Periya Puranam, Part 137, Ilai Malinda Sarukkam, Kannappa Nayanar Puranam. Archery training begins for Thinnanar, who is 12 years old. It was a seven-day festival. Thinnanar, who began his training, became highly skilled and proficient in archery. Not only archery, but he was also trained in other weapons and became very skilled in them. Thus, Thinnanar gradually grew and reached the age of 16. The moon has 16 phases, but a moon that has grown to 16 phases will then wane phase by phase, and then begin to wax again. But here, Thinnanar gradually grows and reaches the age of 16. However, he is an unwaxing moon. Upon reaching 16 years of age, by the grace of Kudumithevar, he attains the state of liberation, which knows no waxing or waning. Therefore, he is now like a full, undiminished moon of 16 years. The age of 16 is referred to in various ways: "manap paruvam" (marriageable age), "thirumana paruvam" (marriageable age), "kaalai paruvam" (youthful age). Thirugnanasambandar Puranam, song 1109, states "punniya 16 aandu perum pugali vendhar" (the great king of Pugali, of 16 meritorious years). Like Thirugnanasambandar Swamigal, the king of Pugali, Kannappa Nayanar also attained liberation at the age of 16. How does Thinnanar grow? Sekkizhar Swamigal says that he grows like ever-increasing Shiva-punyam (merit from devotion to Shiva). Sekkizhar Swamigal says that Thinnanar grows very beautifully, like the splendor of boundless Shiva-punyams gathering together and increasing further and further. Shiva-punyam is an imperishable merit. Ordinary worldly merit gives its fruit and then perishes. But Shiva-punyam, the Shiva-punyam we have earned, never perishes. Not only that, but that Shiva-punyam further causes us to perform more Shiva-punyams, and it continues to grow. All other "pasu-punyams" (merits related to worldly existence) give their respective pleasures and then lead to rebirth. But Shiva-punyam leads to liberation, from which one does not return to birth. The Nayanar grows to 16 years of age like such Shiva-punyam. At this same time, Thinnanar's father, Naga, becomes old. Until now, he had protected the people of this region under his rule through his archery and hunting skills. But now, Naga has become very old, so he can no longer protect his subjects by hunting as before. He reaches the stage of physical weakness due to old age. We have already seen in this Purana, haven't we, that Naga and Tattai were childless for a very long time? So everyone who saw them used to say, "Where will they get a child now?" We saw this at the beginning of this Purana. If we assume Naga was about 60 years old then, now Thinnanar is 16 years old. So, at this time, Naga could be around 76 to 80 years old. Nevertheless, until now, he was physically strong, hunting and raiding cattle, protecting the forest. But now he has become very old. At the same time, the menace of wild animals in the forest has increased, meaning Naga can no longer go hunting as before. Going hunting once a month and destroying fierce wild animals was their profession and custom. But now he cannot go hunting. So all the hunters come and complain to Naga. They all come and complain to Naga, their tribal chief, that wild animals are causing trouble because their monthly hunting activities have not taken place. They complain that they are greatly troubled by wild animals like wild boars, tigers, bears, wild cows, deer, and stags coming from the mountainous regions. The wild animals mentioned above come onto the cultivated fields of the hunters and destroy their crops, kill the domestic animals raised by the hunters, and also harm the hunters, causing them various kinds of suffering. Therefore, usually, the leader of the hunter tribe would go hunting once a month to curb the menace of these wild animals. Since this is not happening now, their troubles have greatly increased, they say. Sekkizhar has introduced a subtle point here. Among the wild animals listed by Sekkizhar, the wild boar is mentioned first. Although the boar is smaller than the tiger, the damage caused by the boar to crops is very high. Not only that, in this story, the wild boar leads the Nayanar to Thirukalathi mountain by making him follow it. Sekkizhar Swamigal has sung about this special significance by placing the wild boar first. A king has two indispensable duties. One is to ensure that all his subjects receive food and do not suffer from hunger. This is the first duty. The second duty is to protect his subjects from harm from anyone else. Sekkizhar Peruman, who was the Chief Minister, composed this song with these two duties as its underlying meaning. Naga, having heard all these requests from the hunters, tells them. "Due to old age, I cannot engage in hunting as before. Therefore, you all accept my son as your leader." Old age, when it comes to a person, gradually envelops them, suppressing all their energies. Just as night follows day, and then darkness gradually envelops every moment, so too old age slowly envelops people, making it impossible to pinpoint when it arrived. It suppresses the body's energy moment by moment. Thus, Naga openly states that old age has enveloped him and asks them to accept his son Thinnanar as their leader from now on. Naga was a hereditary ruler. Therefore, Thinnanar was the one who should be the king of those subjects after him. Nevertheless, Naga seeks the approval of the subjects there. Therefore, in ancient times, even if it was a monarchical rule, it happened only with the approval of the subjects in our Bharat Desam. It is surprising to see the same tradition even in the hunter community. Hearing Naga's words, the hunters first feel disturbed and pity Naga. They felt pity for their leader's old age and his consequent removal from leadership. Then, upon realizing that his son, their leader's son, was going to become a superior leader, a new leader for them, they felt joy. Then the hunters say to Naga, "Leader, for all this time, we have lived happily and without harm under your bow's rule. Now, is there any other way for us than to follow the path you have commanded and blessed? Moreover, you have given us Thinnan, who possesses true valor, from your lineage. Therefore, please call your son and bestow upon him your mountain kingdom's right." The hunters said this with joy. When all the hunters agreed, Naga sends for Thinnanar to be brought. As soon as the hunters agreed, he immediately asked for Thinnanar to be brought and made him their leader. Not only that, Naga immediately began making arrangements for Thinnanar's maiden hunt. This is an example of how a good leader should act swiftly upon hearing the grievances of his subjects. Naga says, "After crowning Thinnanar, he must go on his maiden hunt. Before that, sacrifices must be made to all the forest deities. Therefore, make the necessary arrangements." He also says, "Bring the 'Devaratti' (priestess) who comes from our tradition to perform the puja and sacrifices to the forest deities." In their tradition, there was a woman, an elder in the hunter tribe, appointed specifically for this purpose, to worship the forest deities for the well-being of the hunter community. The hunters would provide her with all her needs. This elderly woman, the Devaratti, would propitiate the forest deities, make them descend into her, and, possessed by the divine, would make the forest deities grant the wishes of the hunters through her. The forest deities would similarly possess the elderly Devaratti and grant the boons desired by the hunters. That elderly Devaratti arrives there. She wears a garland made of forest sprouts. She wears earrings made from the horn of a stag. She has a dot on her forehead made of "aridaram" (a type of red pigment) extracted from the stomach of a deer. She wears a necklace of conch beads, colored like a peacock's neck, as an ornament around her neck. The elderly Devaratti, wearing a tree bark garment tied around her waist, adorned with leaves and peacock feathers, slowly arrives there. She offers "akshatai" (rice mixed with flowers) and praises Naga, the leader of the hunters, who is skilled in battle. Sekkizhar Swamigal sings here that she praises him with "akshatai" made of mountain paddy and flowers. Therefore, the tradition of showering "akshatai" also existed in the hunter community. Generally, "akshatai" refers to whole rice grains that are unbroken, uncracked, and not soaked in water. In earlier days, for ceremonies like "kumabhishekam" (consecration), it was customary to collect good paddy from many houses and process it into rice for "akshatai." Even today, many who perform Shiva Puja regularly follow this custom. The blessing given with such unbroken whole rice is believed to last without breaking, which is its principle. Naga asks the elderly Devaratti who has arrived, "Mother, are you well? Do you have any poverty? Are you living well? You are living well, aren't you?" He asks with affection. The Devaratti also blesses Naga and says, "According to the provisions you made in the beginning, I receive all the necessary soft meat, flying ants, honey, mountain produce, and other necessities. Why have you called me?" Naga addresses the elderly woman as "Mother," doesn't he? She is an elderly woman, so he calls her "Mother." Moreover, she is a divine woman who lives by making the forest deities possess her, isn't she? That is why he calls her "Mother." Furthermore, isn't it a custom in our Tamil Nadu to address any woman who is not one's wife as "Mother"? Naga's affectionate inquiry reveals his concern and care that all the people living under his rule are living happily, free from poverty and other sufferings. In response, the Devaratti says, "According to the provisions you made earlier, we receive all the items," doesn't she?
