It was customary in our country in ancient times for kings to establish grants to ensure that their subordinates lived well and without poverty. Similarly, it is clear from this that Naga has made provisions for the Devaratti and provides her with her necessities. Let us continue to reflect. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 15 — Kannappa Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Periyapuranam, Part 138. Ilai Malinda Sarukkam, Kannappa Nayanar Puranam. Nagana, the leader of the hunter tribe, who had sent for the priestess, tells her: “I am bestowing the leadership of this tribe, which is my birthright, upon my son, Thinnan. Therefore, from now on, Thinnan will lead this tribe. Therefore, you must worship the deities of the forest and offer them forest sacrifices so that he may excel in hunting more than I do, and achieve victory in conquering foreign lands.” After Nagana, the right to rule would naturally pass to Thinnan. However, there is a tradition that Kannappa Nayanar should receive this leadership after Nagana, even though it would come naturally. Despite this tradition, Nagana voluntarily gives his leadership position to Thinnan. Few kings renounce their sovereignty during their lifetime and crown their heir apparent. This is a characteristic of a king with excellent qualities. Only a few individuals, endowed with excellent qualities and dedicated to their duties, will, in their lifetime, establish a successor and crown them. Nagana, here, is one such person. Furthermore, Nagana tells the priestess: “Worship the deities, wishing that my son may excel in hunting more than I do.” Is it not a Kural that “It is sweet to all living beings on earth when their children are wise?” Nagana is a wise man. He had performed penance in the past to have this son. Therefore, he wished for his son to be superior to himself. Nagana further states: “My son should rule better than I do. He should conquer not only our lands but also foreign lands and rule.” Foreign lands here mean neighboring countries. There is also an inner meaning to this. Nagana engaged in physical hunting. But Thinnanar is about to engage in spiritual hunting. Thinnanar is one who conquers the hunter tribes, which are the five senses, and seizes the supreme bliss that is distinct from them. Therefore, according to Nagana's wish, Thinnanar attained not only this land, that is, this hunter nation, but also the foreign land of supreme bliss, which is liberation. The priestess blesses Thinnanar, saying, "So be it." Nagana asks the priestess to offer forest sacrifices to the deities in the forest, with food items that they desire. Just as a large government has separate officials for each department to run it, there are many minor deities residing in the forest who protect it. Worshipping these deities is not contrary to worshipping the Supreme God. Worshipping these forest deities is also not polytheism. This is because Lord Shiva Himself, according to the level of maturity of each soul, places various mature souls in various positions, and Lord Shiva Himself rules this world with His grace. Therefore, worshipping these minor deities appointed by the Lord is not contrary to worshipping only the Lord. This is a system established to understand and realize the truth that Lord Shiva Himself bestows grace through these minor deities. Therefore, in this hunter tribe, they worship not only Lord Muruga, the Supreme God, but also various forest deities. It is one of the duties of a king who rules a country to ensure that all temples and all worship services are conducted without any shortcomings. Here, it is stated that Nagana worshipped the deities residing in the forest because his son was going on his maiden hunt. Nagana, who thus appointed his son as the next king and asked for the necessary rituals to be performed for his hunt, spoke without any worry. He spoke with joy. Nagana, who had a child after a long time, is now giving the kingship to his son Thinnanar at the appropriate age, isn't he? Therefore, he does this with great joy, free from worry. Then the priestess tells Nagana: "When I came here, I saw very good omens that I had never seen before. Very good signs appeared to me. Therefore, Nagana, your son Thinnan will achieve great victory, not just at your level, but even surpassing you," the priestess blesses. The good omens that appeared to the priestess are called nimittams. Nimittams are of two types: external omens that are visible, and internal omens that appear in our body. The chirping of certain birds is a good external omen. Omens like the twitching of the right eye in our body are internal omens. The priestess says why she saw better omens than she had ever seen before. Until now, Nagana and others had received the darshan of Kalathiyappar. But no one else had excelled like Thinnanar, having received the gracious gaze of Kalathiyappar. Therefore, the appearance of very good omens for Thinnanar, better than those that appeared to anyone else, is the inner meaning of this event. After the priestess blessed him thus and left, Thinnanar, who resembled a sapphire mountain, arrived there. He came holding a bow in his hand, bowed at his father's feet, and rose. Nagana, his father, embraced Thinnanar, fitting his two broad shoulders with Thinnanar's two broad shoulders. Then he seated him beside him on the tiger skin seat where he was sitting. Nagana looked at his son Thinnanar, who was seated before him, and said, looking at his face: "As old age has come upon me, I no longer have the desire to hunt with my own effort as before. Therefore, from now on, you shall undertake the protection of this hunter tribe, hunt, and forever bear the leadership of this hunter tribe, which is your traditional right." He thus blessed Thinnanar and declared him the next leader. As a symbol of this, Nagana immediately gave Thinnanar the waist-cloth and dagger he was wearing. The waist-cloth and dagger are the symbols of the hunters' government. These were borne by Nagana until this day. Now that Thinnanar is taking over the leadership, Nagana gives them to Thinnanar. All these happen immediately. Nagana's inability, his old age, his lack of interest in hunting, and the complaints of the citizens about the cruelty of wild animals, and the need to immediately alleviate the suffering of the citizens, all led to the immediate transfer of leadership. This clearly shows Nagana's concern and affection for his citizens. Thinnanar sees Nagana saying and doing this. He understands the situation. He thinks about his father's condition. He also thinks that their tribe needs a support for its leadership. Therefore, without refusing his father's words, he accepts his father's words, bows at his father's feet, and accepts the dagger and waist-cloth that his father gave him in due course.

Chapter 16 — Kannappa Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Periyapuranam, Part 139. Ilai Malinda Sarukkam, Kannappa Nayanar Puranam. Thinnanar, having assumed the leadership of the hunter tribe, sets out for his maiden hunt. Sekkizhar Peruman describes Thinnanar's hunting attire in about seven verses. Sekkizhar Swamigal describes Thinnanar's hunting attire from head to toe (Kesadi Padam). A skilled artisan, adept in making hunting attire, comes and adorns Thinnanar in this hunting attire. He straightens Thinnanar's curly hair, removes the curls, and ties it up so that it stands erect and voluminous. On this tied-up hair, he places a garland of tender leaves called Kanni. He adds peacock feathers to it. The artisan, who is making the hunting attire for Thinnanar, also adorns him with a fragrant jasmine garland, Kurinji flowers, and Vetci flowers. He has added peacock feathers to his hair, hasn't he? Below the peacock feather, on Thinnanar's forehead, he places Kundrimani beads and arranges twisted strands of hair to fit, thus decorating him. He places white conch shell earrings in his ears. These earrings, on both sides, resembled a large full moon. Thinnanar wears a necklace of various white beads around his neck. Then he wears a garland of victory called Sannaveeram. Kings wear a Sannaveeram, a garland of victory made of pearl necklaces. These are hunters, so they adorn Thinnanar with a Sannaveeram, a garland of victory made of tiger skin. This Sannaveeram, a garland of victory, is made of tiger skin, and many beads, boar's teeth, tiger's teeth, etc., are embedded in it in various shapes, like the crescent moon, flattened and intricately worked, and then placed on Thinnanar. Furthermore, a small beaded necklace made of elephant tusks hangs down on Thinnanar's chest. Armlets adorn his shoulders. A bracelet shines on his wrist. On his forearm, he wears an iron-like finger guard (kai kothai) to pluck the bowstring. Around his waist, he wears a tiger skin garment adorned with peacock feathers. Around his waist, he wears a long leather garment to carry weapons like a dagger. He wears heroic anklets on his feet. He wears large sandals on his sacred feet. Thus, Thinnanar, in his hunting attire, circumambulates his bow, takes it in his hands, bends it, and stands with the string drawn. Now, he plucks the string of the bow he holds, making a slight sound. Sekkizhar Swamigal sings that the sound of the bowstring he produced dispelled all the sorrows of these worlds. Sekkizhar Swamigal vividly portrays this hunting attire of the Nayanar, from head to toe, in a very beautiful poetic style. Why does he describe it in such detail? Because Thinnanar, also known as Kannappa Nayanar, is about to see Kalathiyappar in this hunting attire. It is in this same attire that he will see Kalathiyappar, then become Kannappar, unite with that Lord, and stand eternally on His right side. Furthermore, this very attire is the sacred form that great acharyas like Thirugnanasambandamurthigal and others saw, worshipped, and praised.

That is why Sekkizhar Swamigal describes this sacred form in such detail. We have already seen Sekkizhar Swamigal's detailed description and singing of Sundaramurthy Swamigal's wedding attire. This is because Lord Shiva, who came as an elderly Brahmin, saw Sundaramurthy Swamigal's wedding attire and stopped him, taking him into His service. Therefore, Sekkizhar Swamigal described that wedding attire in great detail. Similarly, Sekkizhar Peruman will describe in great detail the sacred form of Thirugnanasambanda Peruman on his wedding day, the day he attained liberation by reaching Thirunallur Perumanan. Similarly, he will describe the sacred form of Anaya Nayanar. We will see these in their respective Puranas. Therefore, through these seven verses, from 701 to 712, Sekkizhar Swamigal adorns Thinnanar from head to toe and presents him in the hunting attire with a bow, thus showing us the same sacred form he envisioned. That sacred form is one where the curly hair on the head is tied up, standing erect and voluminous. In it, a garland of tender leaves is placed. Peacock feathers are tucked into that topknot. Above the garland of tender leaves, a jasmine garland, Kurinji flowers, and Vetci flowers are tied. On Thinnanar's forehead, twisted strands of hair are arranged with Kundrimani beads at the base of the peacock feather, thus adorning his sacred form. He wears white conch shell earrings in his ears, shining like a full moon. Around his neck, a necklace of white beads, conch shells, and boar tusks resembling crescent moon fragments are interspersed, forming a flat Sannaveeram made of tiger skin. On his chest, there is a garland of small beads made of elephant tusks, arranged in an orderly fashion. Armlets are on his shoulders, bracelets on his forearms, and a finger guard on his fingers. Around his waist, a tiger skin garment adorned with peacock feathers is worn, and its edges are neatly bordered with many straps. Over it, a waist-cloth is worn. A dagger is tied with the waist-cloth. Heroic anklets are on his feet. Large sandals adorn his sacred feet. Thinnanar took a large bow, strung it, and produced a small twang. This is the sacred hunting attire of Thinnanar. Thinnanar orders the scouts to select and bring various types of arrows and place them in his quiver. He sets out for the hunt, taking his fellow hunters, that is, the tribesmen, with him. Many shouts of blessing are heard there. Small drums are beaten. Many hunters surround Thinnanar, ready for the hunt, and amidst the crowd, Thinnanar shines like a vast forest where blue water lilies bloom together. The priestess arrives there. She offers honey, good meat, liquor, rice, puffed rice, and other such items to the forest deities as forest sacrifices. Then the priestess places Akshathai on Thinnanar's sacred forehead and blesses him, saying, "Your father's father did not receive these benefits. You have received greater benefits than them. Your strength is also very great. This cannot be contained within our limits." She then blesses Thinnanar. After blessing the priestess with great honors, Thinnanar sets out with his bow in hand, shining like a monsoon cloud. This true radiance of Thinnanar, setting out for the hunt, is a radiance born of the intention to protect the hunter-citizens and do good for them, without seeking any personal gain. In a little while, Thinnanar will attain that state where he becomes the very embodiment of true love, clear in his forgotten self, bearing pure love. Just as the sun's light slowly emerges in the darkness before it dissipates, the true radiance of the great love that Thinnanar will later embrace begins to appear slowly at this time when he sets out for the hunt. Along with Thinnanar, a group of hunters, fearless, set out in their appropriate hunting attire. The hunters take their hunting dogs with them. The hunting dogs are chained. The hunters lead the hunting dogs by these chains. Hunting dogs are very aggressive. They harm people and animals. They should be unleashed at the right time under the supervision of their owner, but at other times, they should be kept chained. Hunting dogs should always be chained. Therefore, when hunting, these hunters will control the chained hunting dogs until they chase and catch animals. We have already seen in earlier verses that hunting dogs were chained in the hunters' settlements. Furthermore, hunting dogs have a nature of running against each other, without order. Therefore, these chained hunting dogs in the hands of the hunters also run against each other without order. They go with them. Dogs are not like cows or horses, which live in a group. Dogs do not have the nature of living in a pack. Dogs are referred to by a proverb, "Swajathi Vairi" (enemy of their own kind). Therefore, dogs do not go in an orderly fashion. The tongues of these hunting dogs are very red and hang long. Therefore, Sekkizhar Swamigal describes the hanging red tongues of these hunting dogs as being like the feet of the goddess of victory. They say there are eight types of Lakshmis, Ashtalakshmi. Among them, the goddess of victory is called Vijayalakshmi. The hunters who set out for the hunt have various types of nets with them. They spread these nets around and inside the forest and catch various types of animals in them. When going for the hunt, horns are blown. Small drums are played. The Pambai drum is beaten. The hunters go with great excitement, making various sounds. If horns, Aakuli, Pambai, and many other sounds are made, surrounding and besieging from all sides, the animals hiding in dens, bushes, and other hiding places will be frightened and run out. Then they will be hunted. This is the hunting tradition. Sekkizhar Swamigal compares the group of hunters setting out for the hunt with Thinnanar to the Kalindi River. The Kalindi River is the Yamuna River. It is a tradition in our country to refer to rivers as maidens. They say that the Yamuna River is black, and the Ganga River is white, like crystal, and when the Yamuna and Ganga meet, the confluence will appear black and white for a long distance. Cheraman Perumal Nayanar connects this appearance, that is, the Yamuna being black and the Ganga being crystalline white, both flowing together, with the Ardhanareeswarar form. The Lord's form, the Lord's color, is like white crystal, a crystalline color. Similarly, the Ganga is on one side. The color of Umaiyammaiyar is green, and that is the color on the left side. The Yamuna River is like the color of Umaiyammaiyar. Therefore, when the Ganga and Yamuna meet,

The appearance of black and white reminds Cheraman Perumal Nayanar of the Ardhanareeswarar form of the Lord. Here, Sekkizhar Swamigal sings that the group of hunters going for the hunt is like the flood of water in the Kalindi, also known as the Yamuna River. The black color of the hunters is compared to the black color of the Yamuna River, and the waves rising in the Yamuna River are compared to the rows of bows held by the hunters. The greatness of the Yamuna River is compared to the large stature of the hunters. Even in hunting, there are scouts. These scouts go ahead, find out the movements of animals, and report them to the hunters. They report that deer, boars, antelopes, and various other animal herds are abundant and close by in the mountain forests to the south. Therefore, the hunters, led by Thinnanar, go to hunt in the mountain forest to the south. Here, the mountain forest to the south, as reported by the scouts, can be understood as the forest area near and surrounding Kalathi Hill. This is because to the north of Kalathi Hill is Pothappi Nadu. Uduppur is a village in Pothappi Nadu. Thinnanar and his group of hunters set out from Uduppur for hunting. To their south is Kalathi Hill. Thinnanar hunts for many leagues towards this southern direction. Therefore, we can understand that what the scouts reported now is almost Kalathi Hill and the surrounding forests. Upon approaching the forest for hunting, the actions that the hunters, who are the advance party, perform before hunting include circling the forest, entering it from various directions, scouting, and setting up nets around the forest for direct guarding. Then, the group of hunters, led by Thinnanar, entered the forest, made various noises, and made all the hidden animals come out. Wild boars, deer, bears, strong herds of antelopes, wild buffaloes, elephants, tigers, and wild antelopes, many animals, rose up in anger, roared, sprang forward, and ran, leaping here and there. The group of hunters shot arrows at them. In these verses, Sekkizhar Peruman describes how the hunt takes place with beautiful rhythm and poetic grace. Let's continue to reflect. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 17 — Kannappa Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Periyapuranam, Part 140. Ilai Malinda Sarukkam, Kannappa Nayanar Puranam. Sekkizhar Swamigal describes how the hunting takes place in 11 verses, from verse 717 to 727. These verses are composed in a rhythmic style suitable for hunting, with a fast-paced sound. Now, Sekkizhar Swamigal has beautifully composed the description of how the hunting takes place, from verse 728 to verse 740, with a beautiful rhythmic grace. Let's look at some of these rhythmically composed verses: "Thaal aruvana vidai thuniyana thalai thumivana kalai maavaligalodu kudal sorithara marivana sila marai maaneeludal vidu saramuruvida nimirvana midai kadamaa meeligal kanai padumudalazha vizhuvana pala uzhaiye venkanai padu pidarkizhipada visai uruviya kayavaai senganal pada vathanodu kanai seriyumuniru karumaa vengalasiramuruvia pozhuthadalaiyirura vathanai pongiya sinamodugavarvana puraivana sila puligal." These verses are composed with rhythmic grace. Now let's look at their meaning. Let's look at the meaning of these verses. Deer are hunted. Some of the deer die with arrows piercing their bodies. Kadama animals are hunted. Large boars are also hunted. Tigers are hunted. In some places, the hunters shoot arrows from behind the animals and hunt them. These are hunters who hide behind trees. Therefore, they attack from behind. This is not righteous. Even if it is unrighteous, the hunters do not know about unrighteousness, do they? So, some of them shoot arrows from behind in this manner. But our Thinnanar only hunts animals that come in front of him. He hunts animals from in front of them. Other hunters hunt small rabbits and deer. Our Thinnanar hunts elephants, tigers, and lions. Here, Thinnanar is engaging in a heroic battle. Now Sekkizhar sings a beautiful scene. The hunters have spread nets in those areas for hunting. Due to the sound they made, deer get caught in those nets. Then, thinking that they are caught in the nets, the deer try to escape. In front of the deer trying to escape, the hunters' hunting dogs stand. The hunting dogs stand in front of the deer, preventing them from escaping. This is the scene described by Sekkizhar Swamigal. Sekkizhar Swamigal sings a beautiful metaphorical comparison with a profound spiritual meaning for this. Just like the deer caught in the net, we, as souls, are caught in the nets of good and bad karma. Thinking that we are caught in these karmas, souls wish to escape from these nets of karma. Thus, wishing to escape, the souls' hearts seek to attain true knowledge. This is the state where the deer try to escape and survive. But there, the hunting dogs stand in front of the deer and do not let them escape, don't they? Similarly, when souls seek true knowledge to be freed from these two karmas, the five senses, called jnanendriyas and karmendriyas, stand in their way, opposing them. This is the metaphor shown by Sekkizhar Peruman. It is because Sekkizhar Swamigal sings metaphors with spiritual meaning even in a hunting profession that he earned the title "Deivasekkizhar" (Divine Sekkizhar). Good and bad karma, called hitam and ahitam, are the two karmas that subject living beings to birth and death, causing them to be born again and again in this world. Bad karma leads to sin and causes the suffering of hell. Similarly, good karma leads to merit and gives pleasures like heaven. Therefore, the merit resulting from good karma and the sin resulting from bad karma give birth again and again to living beings. As a result, living beings are born again and again, die again and again, and are caught in this cycle. This is what he compares to the deer caught in the net and suffering, isn't it? The deer are caught in the net and suffer, unable to get out of it. Souls, on the other hand, suffer in the cycle of birth and death again and again due to good and bad karma, unable to escape from this cycle. Merit is a chain made of gold that binds us. Sin is a chain made of iron that binds us. Therefore, both merit and sin bind us. This is what Manikkavasagar Peruman says: "Bound by the strong ropes of righteousness and sin." That is why Thiruvalluvar calls these two karmas "irulser vinai" (dark karmas) in Thirukkural. "Irulser iruvinaiyum sera Iraivan porulser pugazhpurindhar maattu" is the fifth Kural of the chapter "God's Praise." Even yagas and other rituals yield only perishable benefits (kamya). They do not give spiritual knowledge. Therefore, merit not only fails to give knowledge but also acts as a hindrance to the occurrence of knowledge, just like bad karma, that is, sin. Thus, if a soul wants to be freed from the cycle of good and bad karma, that is, merit and sin, it must attain the state of "iruvinai oppu" (equanimity towards two karmas). Iruvinai oppu occurs in souls only through the grace of God, through penances like chariyai and kiriyai. Thus, souls suffer like deer caught in the net of good and bad karma. The deer wishing to be freed from that net is a metaphor for the souls' attempt to attain true knowledge and escape from these nets of karma. But there, dogs stop the deer. Here, the karmendriyas and jnanendriyas of the souls, who wish to attain true knowledge, stop them. Therefore, our five senses are compared to dogs here. Let's see why the senses are compared to dogs here. Dogs keep biting a dry bone. This causes a wound in the dog's mouth. As the dog continues to bite the bone with this wound, blood oozes from the wound. The dog tastes this oozing blood and foolishly thinks it is coming from the bone, and bites the bone more and more. This causes more and more blood to ooze from the wound in the dog's mouth. It keeps tasting it more and more. It even considers it very joyful. While there are many good things to eat, the dog eats feces, which are not edible. It also eats dirt. Furthermore, the dog eats its own vomited food again. Just like this dog, living beings experience the harms that come from the five senses. They mistakenly consider these sufferings that come from the five senses as pleasures and experience them with joy. Furthermore, while there is the experience of Shiva, which is a good experience, they do not experience it, but instead repeatedly experience worldly pleasures and wallow in them. They repeatedly experience the same dirty experiences they have already experienced. "Undathe undu, udutthathae uduthu, adutthaduthu uraithathe uraithum, kandathe kandum, kettathe kettum kazhinthana kadavul naalellam" is a verse by Pattinathadigal. Therefore, this verse by Pattinathadigal states that souls waste all their days by eating what they have already eaten through the five senses, wearing what they have already worn, speaking what they have already spoken, seeing what they have already seen again, and hearing what they have already heard and are accustomed to. Based on these ideas, elders compare themselves to dogs, saying "nayen" (I, the dog) and "lower than a dog." Even in a hunting forest filled with blood and killing, finding and showing such spiritual truths is the nature of Sekkizhar Peruman's divinely fragrant poetry. Similarly, in Periyapuranam, every verse is full of devotion and spiritual meanings. Let's continue to reflect on the verses. We saw earlier that some hunters shot arrows from behind the deer. There are some such hunters. But generally, Sekkizhar Peruman says that hunters hunted according to righteous principles. As mentioned above, those who hunt unrighteously are a minority. But generally, here, Sekkizhar Peruman explains that hunters hunt according to righteous principles. Generally, the method of warfare in our Bharata Desam is based on dharma. Before starting a war, they would announce to cows, Brahmins, ascetics, children, the sick, women, childless people, and those who do not fight against them to go to a safe place. After making such announcements and securing these categories of people in safe places, war would take place according to our tradition. But in Western countries and other countries, there is no such justice at all. For the sake of food, out of greed for wealth, and out of a frenzy to conquer countries, they mercilessly slaughter innocent civilians, just as sheep and cattle are slaughtered in slaughterhouses. We still see thousands of civilians suffering from such wars. By the great compassion of our teacher Sekkizhar Peruman, for the welfare of the world, these principles exemplified here should be followed by our people not only in war but in all fields. Not only in war, but in all fields, people should not use their strength to seize others' property or to gain benefits. They should act according to righteous principles, without deviating from justice. Hunters would not hunt female animals during their hunt. They would not hunt young animals. They would not harm pregnant female animals that walk with difficulty. Thus, these hunters followed righteous principles even in hunting. Generally, hunters are described as having a very harsh appearance and a very harsh mind, using words like "kill," "throw," and "stab." They steal the belongings of travelers passing through their forests. These cruel hunters are uneducated. Even they followed a principle in hunting. They hunted based on a dharma. But in this era, which is said to be very civilized, is it not the cruellest of cruelties that those who are called educated and civilized cause suffering to this world without any justice in their professions? Now, even in the cruel-minded hunters, the mind that wants to hunt according to dharma is the grace of God. God is present everywhere, as the life of all living beings. Therefore, He is found even within these cruel hunters, and this hunting with dharma is an event that manifested without their knowledge. Moreover, the hunters who go for hunting do not completely destroy all animals. Their leaving female animals, pregnant animals, and young animals unhunted is also based on the good intention that these animal species should not be completely wiped out. As the hunting was going on in the forest, a large wild boar came running. The wild boar, making a loud thunderous sound like a huge black cloud, shaking the entire forest, even frightening elephants, broke through all the nets, and ran with great speed, emitting fire from its eyes. While the other hunters were busy with their hunting, unaware of this, only Thinnanar ran after the wild boar. Two hunter friends, accompanying Thinnanar, ran with him. Thinnanar is chasing that large wild boar, isn't he? This becomes the turning point in Thinnanar's story. It runs as if the same boar that caused a fight between God and Thinnanar in Thinnanar's previous birth has now appeared in front of Thinnanar in this birth and is running. Thinnanar also follows it very quickly. Let's continue to reflect. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 18 — Kannappa Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Periyapuranam, Part 141. Ilai Malinda Sarukkam, Kannappa Nayanar Puranam. A very large wild boar, breaking through all the nets, ran very fast, and Thinnanar ran after it. Following Thinnanar, two hunters, Nanan and Kadan, ran with him as companions. The boar, which had run for many leagues, stopped among a cluster of trees at the foot of a small hill. Thinnanar, who had already known that the boar, having run a long distance, would stop at such a place, followed it quickly and stood in front of it. Generally, animals that sense danger to their lives, out of fear, try to escape and run away. But this boar did not run away in fear; instead, it stood its ground. Therefore, Thinnanar, instead of trying to kill the boar with his arrows from a distance, stood in front of it, as if to engage in a heroic hand-to-hand combat. The boar also stood ready to pounce on him. Therefore, Thinnanar, standing in front of the boar as if to engage in a wrestling match, drew his sword in front of it and stabbed it so that its body was cut in two. The hunter chief, Thinnanar, stabbed it so quickly that the boar fell to the ground in two pieces. Nanan saw this and told Kadan, who was with him, in wonder. Nanan was one of Thinnanar's inseparable bodyguards, and Kadan was another. But here, Nanan was very close to Thinnanar and served him intimately. He spoke to Kadan in wonder, seeing Thinnanar's bravery and skill. Then Nanan and Kadan told Thinnanar: "We are very hungry and thirsty from running for such a long distance. So, let's roast this boar. You also eat a little. We will eat this to satisfy our hunger and drink some water, and then we will continue." Both of them said this. They could not find high-quality meat like deer meat or honey to give to their chief Thinnanar. But this boar, which is of the lowest quality among meats, is what is available now. So, without any other option, they both said they would prepare and eat the boar's meat. Hearing Nanan and Kadan say this, Thinnanar asked: "Is there even water in this forest? Where is the water in this forest?" This is because they had run a long distance chasing this boar, hadn't they? While running, Thinnanar had observed all the forest areas. He had not seen any springs or water bodies along the way. That is why he asked this question. He asked where there was water in the dense forest. Nanan answered Thinnanar's question. Nanan said: "Don't you see a teak forest nearby? If you go past that teak forest, the Ponmugali River flows beside a long hill." Although Thinnanar generally asked the question, Nanan answered. Among Nanan and Kadan, Nanan was the more knowledgeable and experienced one. Nanan was the one who nurtured Thinnanar's love and interest in Kalathi Hill, saying that if he went to Kalathi Hill, he would see a good sight. Nanan was Thinnanar's guide until Thinnanar climbed Kalathi Hill and saw Kudumithevar. When he climbed to the top of Kalathi Hill, Thinnanar heard the sound of divine drums and asked Nanan what that sound was. Nanan replied that it was the sound made by bees circling the flowers around the hill. Nanan also explained to Thinnanar that the flowers and water found on Kudumithevar's sacred body were the result of a Brahmin's worship. Therefore, Nanan was responsible for the worship that Thinnanar later performed for the Lord.

Not only that, but Nanan was also a strong and trusted companion to Thinnanar's father, Nagana, in hunting, and he had come to Kalathi Hill with Nagana. It is this Nanan who now introduces the Ponmugali River and Kalathi Hill to Thinnanar. He received that great fortune. He says that if you cross this large teak forest, there is a mountain there, a continuation of long hills. Generally, the Eastern Ghats, unlike the Western Ghats, are not continuous, high, and dense, but rather a series of disconnected hills. Kalathi Hill forest is one such hilly region in the Eastern Ghats. He says that there is one such hill in a long line of hills. He also says that the Ponmugali River flows beside that hill. Nanan says that the Ponmugali River is there, implying that it is cool and capable of relieving our fatigue, hunger, and thirst. Not only does drinking the cool water of the Ponmugali River bring coolness, but there is also the meaning that merely thinking of or speaking of the Ponmugali River brings coolness to us. This river, Ponmugali, is called Suvarnamukhi in Sanskrit. It has been referred to as Ponmugali in the Thevarams. Mugaram means light, and Mugari means radiant. Since the water in this river flows making sounds, it is called Ponmugali with the suffix Mugari. Since 'la' and 'ra' are interchangeable, it can also be called Ponmugari or Ponmugali. In the Thevarams, it is referred to as Ponmugali River. We have already reflected on the sacred properties of the Ponmugali River in the Thirukalathi Puranam. Thus, Nanan speaks about the Ponmugali River and Kalathi Hill. Hearing this, Thinnanar says, "Let's go there. Bring that boar." As he said, he goes towards the Ponmugali River. After going half a league, he reaches the grove on the slopes of Thirukalathi Hill, where Lord Shiva resides. The verse says, "He himself went towards it, and after half a league, he saw the grove on the slopes of the sacred mountain where the Lord with the red eye and bull resides." Sekkizhar Peruman refers to Kalathi Hill as Thirumalai (sacred mountain). In Periyapuranam, we first saw the section called Thirumalai Sarukkam. There, Thirumalai referred to Thirukailaya Malai (Mount Kailash). This Kalathi Hill is also called Thenkailai (Southern Kailash), isn't it? Therefore, Thirumalai here refers to Kalathi Hill. The Anthadi composed by Nakkiradevar, "Kailai Paathi Kalathi Paathi," is also a work based on this idea. Thirunavukkarasu Peruman, after seeing Kalathi Hill here, began his pilgrimage to Thirukailayam with a desire to see the magnificent form of the Lord residing in Thirukailayam. Thirugnanasambanda Peruman and Sundaramurthy Swamigal also sang about Thirukailaya Malai from here. Therefore, just as Thirukailaya Malai is called Thirumalai, Thirukalathi Malai is also called Thirumalai. After going half a league, Thinnanar sees the sight of Thirukalathi Hill for the first time. As soon as he saw that sight, all of Thinnanar's hunger, thirst, and fatigue vanished. Thirukalathi Hill captivated his heart. Thinnanar immediately tells Nanan, looking at him, "Nanan, let's go to that hill that appears in front." Thinnanar says, "Nanan, let's go to this hill that appears in front." Then Nanan tells Thinnanar: "If you go there, Thinnanar, you will see a good sight. On this high and sacred Kalathi Hill, Kudumithevar, who makes everything righteous and without crookedness, resides. We can worship that Kudumithevar," he said. This is a verse composed by Sekkizhar Peruman. Nanan says: "Thinnanar, if you go there, you will see a good sight. On this high and sacred Kalathi Hill, Kudumithevar, who makes everything righteous and without crookedness, resides. We can worship that Kudumithevar," he said. Those who go to such sacred mountains and sacred places generally travel there. They go and come back. They go and come back without seeing all the special features there. But here Nanan says: "If you go to that Kalathi Hill, you will see a special sight." He says, "You will see a good sight." This is because the sight that Thinnanar will see will not be an ordinary sight like that of others. The sight that Thinnanar will see is a sight that the Lord shows him, having received the gracious gaze of the Lord. That is why it is called a good sight in the verse here. What is a good sight? It is seeing the sight that scriptures say one should see with internal and external instruments, and that elders say one should see. Thirunavukkarasar Swamigal sings in the Thiruangamalai, a Thiruppathikam, "Kankal kanminkalo kadal nanjunda kandan thannai en thol veesi nindraadum piran thannai kankal kanminkalo" (Oh eyes, behold the Lord who drank the poison of the ocean, the Lord who dances, swinging my shoulders, oh eyes, behold Him!). Thirunavukkarasar Swamigal sings that we should see only Lord Shiva with our eyes. Such a sight is called a good sight. Nanan had previously said that if you cross the teak forest, you can see the hill. That was something the Nayanar knew only by hearing. Now, the Nayanar directly sees Thirukalathi Hill with his sacred eyes. Furthermore, Nanan says that Kudumithevar is at the top of this hill, and we can worship him. Kudumithevar is the deity who sprouted from the hill, rooted in it. He is the one who removes all faults from souls. The name of the Lord, the Sivalinga Peruman, at the top of Thirukalathi Hill is Kudumithevar. Kudumi means the top. He is the one who resides at the top of the hill, the Kudumi. Therefore, he is named Kudumithevar. The hunters have worshipped Thirukalathiappar, the Lord of Thirukalathi, by the name Kudumithevar. Does Kudumithevar reside only at the top of this hill? Is he not also at the pinnacle of Vedanta, which is praised as the pinnacle of the Vedas? Is he not the essence of the Vedantas, called Veda Siras? At the end, that is, at the pinnacle, of the four Vedas stands the Atharvana Veda. Its head is Atharva Siras, and its crest is Atharva Sigai. What does that Atharva Sigai say? It ultimately states that one should abandon everything and meditate only on Shiva, who is auspicious. Shiva alone is to be meditated upon. Therefore, Lord Shiva is the ultimate reality that shines at that pinnacle, at the culmination of the Vedas and Vedantas. He is the one who shines here at the top of Kalathi Hill, bearing the name Kudumithevar. Therefore, the sight of Kudumithevar at Kalathi Hill is the vision of knowledge that shines at the pinnacle of the Upanishads. After seeing Thirukalathi Hill, Thinnanar says, looking at Nanan, "What is happening to me now? As I see this hill and go towards it, I feel as if all my burdens are decreasing. My desire swells, and my desire for this Thirukalathi Hill increases more and more, my eagerness grows. I have no other desire in my mind. My mind only thinks of going quickly towards Thirukalathi Hill. No other thought arises in my mind. Where is Kudumithevar's abode on this hill? Take me there quickly."

Thinnanar asks Nanan. With every step Thinnanar takes towards Thirukalathi Hill, he experiences a feeling as if his burden is decreasing. The desire to go to that Thirukalathi Hill swells. That desire rises and swells more and more, without subsiding. His mind goes to the hill much faster than his body. Thinnanar has no other desire or wish except to see that Thirukalathi Hill. This desire and eagerness come to Thinnanar because of the Shiva penance he performed in all his previous births, as a result of Shiva's merit, he now has such an experience. A similar passage also appears in the story of Chandeesa Nayanar. Sekkizhar states that Chandeesa Nayanar's Shiva worship occurs as a continuation of his previous knowledge. The Thirukkural, "One's learning in one birth is a safeguard for seven births," is also relevant here for our reflection. Thus, due to the continuation of his previous birth, Thinnanar's desire to see this Thirukalathi Hill swells more and more. The desire to go up the Thirukalathi Hill that he saw and see Kudumithevar also swells more and more. As he goes towards that Thirukalathi Hill, he feels as if all his burdens are decreasing. He has an experience. The burdens of souls are the 36 Tattvas and 60 Tatvikas, totaling 96 entities. Until the grace of God falls upon the soul, the soul clings to these 96 entities, believing them to be pleasure, and remains bound within them, entangled in many births. But in reality, these 96 entities are a burden to the soul. However, the soul will wander through births, believing these 96 to be pleasure, not realizing they are a burden. This continues until the grace of God falls upon that soul. After the soul has matured and the grace of God has fallen upon it, the soul begins to perceive these 36 Tattvas and 60 Tatvikas, these 96 entities, as distinct from itself. It begins to realize that it is separate from these 96 Tattvas. As the grace of God falls more and more upon the soul, the soul begins to realize that these 96 Tattvas are a burden to it. As the grace of God falls even more deeply upon the soul, these 96 Tattvas, these 96 burdens, begin to detach from the soul one by one. They fall away. It is then that the soul's burden, its true burden, begins to decrease. This experience, where the 96 Tattvas detach one by one, is what Thinnanar experiences now as he goes towards this Thirukalathi Hill. As these 96 Tattvas detach one by one, the soul experiences joy. Furthermore, as this burden decreases, the desire to attain God increases greatly. Until then, the soul, standing united with ego and devoid of knowledge, will become disgusted with ego, despise it, and then strive to unite with true knowledge. Sekkizhar Peruman explains this state in the verses that follow. Appar Peruman has also composed a Thevaram to the effect that if living beings, lamenting that these 96 Tattvas cause them suffering, develop love for God, God Himself, being a lover, helps those souls. This is the fourth Thirumurai, Thiruvathikai Veerattanam Thiruppathikam, Pathikam number 25, verse number 4. The verse is as follows: "Thuruththayam kurambai thannil thonnootru angaruvarnindru viruththi than tharuga endru vedanai palavum seyya varuthiyall vallavaaru vanthu vanthadaiya nindra aruthiyarkku anbar polum athigai veerattanare." In that verse, Thirunavukkarasu Peruman also states that 96 entities cause suffering to souls. Saiva Siddhanta Gnana Shastras also state the same. Sekkizhar Swamigal has composed this experience of the 96 Tattvas slowly detaching one by one, and that burden falling away one by one, in this Thiruppuranam of Kannappa Nayanar, as the 97th verse, after 96 verses. This arrangement is also a divine arrangement. It is also a historical fact that Manikkavasaga Swamigal attained a similar state when he reached Thirupperunthurai. Among those 96, there are 36 Tattvas and 60 Tatvikas. Sekkizhar sings that when Kannappa Nayanar began to climb Thirukalathi Hill, he climbed and crossed the 36 Tattvas as steps. Therefore, the feeling of Thinnanar's burden decreasing here suggests that it is the 60 Tatvikas among those 96 Tattvas. We can list only the names of these 96 Tatvikas here. Then, when we see Kannappa Nayanar climbing Thirukalathi Hill, crossing 36 Tattvas, we can see the summaries of the 36 Tattvas there. Now, we can see only the names of the 36 Tattvas and 60 Tatvikas here. First, the 36 Tattvas. God creates these 36 Tattvas from Maya. These 36 are divided into three: Suddha Maya, Asuddha Maya, and Prakriti Maya. In Suddha Maya, there are five Tattvas; in Asuddha Maya, there are seven Tattvas; in Prakriti Maya, there are 24 Tattvas. First, the five Tattvas in Suddha Maya: Shiva Tattva, Shakti Tattva, Sadashiva Tattva, Ishvara Tattva, Suddha Vidya Tattva. Next, the seven Tattvas in Asuddha Maya: Six Asuddha Maya Tattva, Seven Kala Tattva, Eight Niyati Tattva, Nine Kala, Ten Vidya, Eleven Aragam, Twelve Purusha. Next, the 24 Tattvas of Prakriti Maya. These are: 13 Moolappakuthi or Moola Prakriti, 14 Buddhi, 15 Ahankaram, 16 Manam, 17 Sevi (ear), 18 Thol (skin), 19 Kan (eye), 20 Naakku (tongue), 21 Mookku (nose), 22 Vaai (mouth), 23 Kaal (leg), 24 Kai (hand), 25 Payu, 26 Upastham. 27 Sabdam (sound), 28 Sparsam (touch), 29 Roopam (form), 30 Rasam (taste), 31 Gandham (smell), 32 Aakaayam (ether), 33 Vaayu (air), 34 Theyu (fire), 35 Neer (water), 36 Prithvi (earth). These are the 36 Tattvas. The external instruments that arose from these 36 Tattvas are called Tatvikas. There are 60 of these Tatvikas. They are as follows. Among the 60 Tatvikas, the five components of Prithvi are: hair, bone, skin, nerve, muscle. The five components of Appu (water) are: flowing water, blood, semen, brain, marrow. The five components of fire are: food, sleep, fear, sex, laziness. The five components of Vayu (air) are: running, sitting, lying down, walking, standing. The five components of Aakaayam (ether) are: lust, anger, greed, delusion, pride. The five functions of the five Karmendriyas are: speech, movement, giving, excretion, joy. The ten Vayus (airs) arising from Vayu are: Pranan, Apanan, Udanan, Vyanan, Samanan, Nagan, Kurman, Krikaran, Devadattan, Dhananjayan. The ten Nadis (channels) arising from Prithvi are: Idai, Pingalai, Suzhumunai, Gandhari, Athi, Singuvai, Alambudai, Purudan, Kugu, Sangini. The five arising from Aakaayam are: Muthirasayam, Malasayam, Amasayam, Pakkuvasayam, Karppasayam. Next are the impure expanded worlds of Sabda, Sparsa, Rupa, Rasa, Gandha. All these together make 60. These are the Tatvikas. Therefore, there are 60 Tatvikas. The causal Tattvas for these are 36, making a total of

It will mention the six impermanent Tattvas. Let's continue to reflect. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 19 — Kannappa Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Periya Puranam, Part 142, Ilaimazhindha Sarukkam, Kannappa Nayanar Puranam. Thinnanar, Nanan, and Kadan reach the banks of the Ponmukali River. This Ponmukali River, with the water it carries, beautifully flows, gathering bamboo pearls, agarwood, sandalwood, other gems from the mountains, gold dust, diamonds, and other priceless items with its sand dunes and waves. It is stated in Kailai Paathi Kaalathi Paathi Anthathi that if one bathes in the Ponmukali River, bowing to Kalathinathar, all evil deeds, whether done unknowingly or knowingly, will be removed. As stated, for those who come and bathe in it to perform Shiva Puja, and for those who come and bathe in it to attain liberation through Shiva Puja, this Ponmukali River collects all the necessary items for Shiva Puja, such as agarwood for incense, sandalwood for the Lord's anointing, and gems and gold to be placed in the Lord's holy water and arghya water. It is said in the Ponmukali Sarukkam of the Thirukalathi Puranam that those who bathe in it will attain liberation. This Ponmukali River is capable of bestowing worldly benefits like gold, pearls, and gems, and the ultimate benefit of liberation, to those who come and bathe in it. They reached the banks of this Ponmukali River. There, under the shade of a tree, Nanan and Kadan placed the pig they had carried. Thinnanar then said to Kadan, "Make fire from wood by friction. We will climb this mountain and see it." Hunters know that dry wood, when rubbed, produces fire, as the fire element is abundant in it. They have also used it. Such wood used to produce fire is called Arani wood. Arani is a Sanskrit name. Is it not a philosophical truth that fire is hidden in wood and will emerge if rubbed quickly? Therefore, hunters also produce fire by rubbing these woods, which have a high fire content, when they need it. "Fire is in the firewood" is from Appar Swamigal's Thevaram. Thus, after telling Kadan to make fire and prepare the pig, Thinnanar and Nanan reached the Ponmukali River. They descended into the cool, sacred waters of the Ponmukali River and bathed. As a result, their minds became clear. Thinnanar felt more and more joy. Generally, it is believed that bathing in cool river water clears the mind and removes confusion. This is the sacred Ponmukali River, obtained through penance by the holy Agastya. Therefore, it removed Thinnanar's worldly attachments, such as his lineage, and brought him the clarity of Shiva-gnana-ananda. Kannappa Nayanar attained liberation in the month of Thai, under the Mrigasirisha star. So, this event takes place in the month of Thai. We can understand that clear water flows in the Ponmukali River during the month of Thai. Thus, Thinnanar and Nanan crossed the Ponmukali River and ascended the other bank. Thinnanar looked only at the Kalathi mountain, not at anything else. Great joy arose in his heart. At that time, the sun reached its zenith. It was high noon. They were at the foot of the Thirukalathi mountain, on the other side of the river. At that moment, divine drums (Deva Thundubhi) resounded on top of the Thirukalathi mountain. The sound of the five divine drums resounded there like the roar of the ocean. Why was it so?

Why were the five sounds of the divine musical instrument, Deva Thundubhi, heard at noon on the mountain? Because the Devas come to this mountain and worship the Lord at noon, sounding the Deva Thundubhis. This Thirukalathi mountain is the Southern Kailash. Therefore, like Kailash, Brahma and other Devas come here to worship. Hearing the sound of these Deva Thundubhis, Thinnanar asked Nanan, "What is this, Nana?" This is an expression that the worldly realm is leaving Thinnanar, and the realm of liberation is welcoming him. Thinnanar asks, "What is this, Nana?" These words are the last words Thinnanar spoke in connection with the world. After some time, by the grace of the Lord's gaze, he became the embodiment of love and lost himself. With that, Thinnanar's conversation with the world ceased. His talk about the world ceased. His talk about worldly connections ceased. Therefore, these very words, "What is this, Nana?", were Thinnanar's last words spoken with a sense of worldly connection. However, Nanan was not virtuous enough to hear the sound of the Deva Thundubhis on this mountain. He did not hear any of those divine drum sounds. Therefore, Nanan replied, "There are many flowers on this mountain. It might be the sound of bees buzzing around those flowers." Nanan also spoke with doubt. Only Thinnanar, who was at the pinnacle of great Shiva Punya, could hear the sound of the Deva Thundubhis. But Nanan's reply, unknowingly, further propelled Thinnanar towards the Lord. This becomes Nanan's third instructive word. The Ponmukali River is there. One can bathe there. One can drink water, he said about the river, which was the first instruction. The Thirukalathi mountain is there. You will get a good view there. Kudumit Thevar is there, Nanan said, which was the second instructive word. And now, Nanan's statement that bees are eating honey around the flowers on the mountaintop becomes the third instructive word. Although Nanan generally and very casually said that it was the sound of bees buzzing for honey, it created a desire in Thinnanar to go up and see Kudumit Thevar. Therefore, Shivakavimani says that this too is an instructive word, revealing its inner meaning. Nanan said that bees surrounding the honey in the flowers are making sounds, didn't he? The Lord, who is the great form of bliss enshrined in this mountain, is the great honey. The bees that buzz around this honey are the mature souls whose minds seek and approach the Lord. The sound of the buzzing bees is the sound of praise sung by the mature souls who praise the Lord. It is the tradition of great people to praise the Lord as great honey. "Shall we pluck the flower-creeper, praising the golden pot where the honey of bliss resided in Thiru Idaimaruthu?" is from Thiruvachagam, Thiruppoovalli. "Having drunk the great honey from the glorious Idaimaruthu" is the third verse of Thiruvidaimaruthur Mummanikkovai. In many such sacred verses, great people have sung of the Lord as great honey. Therefore, great people find the inner meaning in Nanan's simple reply, that it is the sound of praise sung by mature souls, like bees, surrounding the great honey that is the Lord. The next three verses are about Kannappa Nayanar climbing the mountain and seeing Kalathiappar, Kudumit Thevar. These are verses 102, 103, and 104, which describe until he sees Kalathi Thevar. Let's look at the verses and then reflect on their deep meanings. Thinnanar followed Nanan, climbing the Kalathi mountain. Now, the verses. The accumulation of past penance,

To demonstrate endless blissful love, boundless enthusiasm surged, and great love for the earth was expressed. Towards the mountain of the generous Lord, with bones melting and a great yearning rising in his heart, Nanan and Anbu ascended the cool mountain first. As they ascended the tall mountain, like those who climb the ever-increasing steps of cherished Tattvas to reach the Lord, who is Shiva, the Lord, before they could see the Lord with the moon-adorned matted hair, the compassionate gaze of the Lord of beautiful eyes fell upon them. The old attachments of birth, which had lingered, left and moved away. He became the embodiment of incomparable love, in the shade of surging light. These are verses 102, 103, and 104. Now, let's reflect on the meaning of each verse. Verse 102: "The accumulation of past penance, to demonstrate endless blissful love, boundless enthusiasm surged, and great love for the earth was expressed. Towards the mountain of the generous Lord, with bones melting and a great yearning rising in his heart." This is the sacred verse. Its meaning is that as Thinnanar looked towards the mountain of the generous Lord, a great desire and love arose in his heart, causing even his bones to melt. Boundless desire surged, blossoming into enduring great love. This is because the accumulation of penance performed by the Nayanar in his past lives manifested as that endless blissful love. That is why such events are happening to the Nayanar. It is in this verse that references to Kannappa Nayanar's previous birth appear. When Sekkizhar Swamigal says "the accumulation of past penance," it refers to the Nayanar's previous birth. Firstly, as Thinnanar looked towards the mountain of the generous Lord, a desire arose in his heart, causing his bones to melt. "Looking" here means to gaze intently. Thinnanar is coming, continuously looking at Kalathi mountain. A desire for Kudumit Thevar arose in him. It is common in the world for someone who intensely desires something to focus on it and move towards it, isn't it? Therefore, Thinnanar comes, gazing with great desire at Kalathi mountain, the mountain of the generous Lord. When such great love arose in his heart, even the bones, which are the foundation of this body, melted there. So, if the bones melted, it means the entire body was ecstatic and melted. Bones are the hardest part of our body, aren't they? Thus, even hard bones become soft and pliable. Such great love arose in the Nayanar. Such great love arose that even his bones melted, meaning his entire body was ecstatic and melted. Manikkavasagar also sang that the Lord ruled him in such a way that all his bones melted. "Entering my body, which is a temple of flesh, making every pore ripen, you entered as a golden temple, melting all my bones, and ruled me as a humble one, O Lord, O flawless gem, cutting off all attachments to sorrow, birth, death, and delusion, O pure light of bliss, I have held you firmly, where will you now arise?" This is the tenth verse of Thiruvachagam, Piditha Pathu. Manikkavasagar says, "O Lord, who made this humble body of flesh, even its hair follicles, experience events, making this body of flesh a great golden temple, residing in it, melting all my bones, and ruling me, a humble one."

We should consider that state here in relation to Thinnanar. Why did such endless love and great desire for the Lord arise in Thinnanar? It is the accumulation of penance performed by Thinnanar in his past lives. Therefore, we must see how Thinnanar performed penance in his previous births and what penance he performed. This story of Thinnanar's previous birth is briefly mentioned by Sekkizhar in the Periya Puranam. However, it is extensively described in the Thirukalathi Puranam. Furthermore, Sekkizhar gives a hint that Thinnanar, due to the continuation of his past lives, became a very skilled archer, quickly mastering archery and becoming perfect in it. Also, a wild boar ran and came up to the foot of this Thirukalathi mountain. Thinnanar came chasing this boar. Therefore, it seems as if the same boar, which caused a fight between Thinnanar and the Lord in Thinnanar's previous life, has now come to unite Thinnanar with the Lord, as that large wild boar brought Thinnanar from Uduppur to Thirukalathi. All these are references to Thinnanar's previous birth, mentioned here and there. So, after reflecting on Thinnanar's previous birth, how he performed penance, and what boons the Lord granted him, through the Thirukalathi Puranam, we will continue from this point. Let's continue our reflection. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 20 — Kannappa Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Periya Puranam, Part 143. As Thinnanar approached the foot of Thirukalathi mountain and began to ascend it, his bones melted, and he developed an unceasing, great love for the Lord. This great love and affection that came to Thinnanar stands here as a manifestation of the endless bliss of the accumulated penance he performed in his past lives. Thinnanar performed numerous penances in his previous births. What is penance? According to Saiva Siddhanta, Shiva Puja itself is penance. Righteous conduct (chariya), ritualistic worship (kriya), and yogic practices (yoga) are penance, according to Saiva Siddhanta. Performing penance directed at other deities does not become a penance that demonstrates endless blissful love. Only Shiva Puja performed towards Lord Shiva is penance. Thinnanar performed this penance, Shiva Puja and Shiva devotion, in his previous births. Who was he in his previous birth, according to the Thirukalathi Puranam? In his previous birth, Thinnanar was Arjuna and performed numerous Shiva Pujas and penances. It is due to that great penance that he has now come as Thinnanar. This is the history of Thinnanar's previous birth. Arjuna, the central figure among the Pancha Pandavas in the Mahabharata, has come as Thinnanar in this birth. When he was Arjuna, he performed numerous penances. He also performed numerous penances in births prior to that. Only after all these penances were completed and perfected did he incarnate as Thinnanar, a hunter, here. Arjuna's penance is found in three important places in the Mahabharata. Not only Arjuna, but all five Pandavas were Shiva devotees. The glories of Arjuna's penance are found in the Mahabharata in sections like Arjuna's Pilgrimage and Arjuna's Penance. Furthermore, the event that led to Arjuna's birth as Thinnanar occurred in the Kirata Parva of the Mahabharata's Vana Parva. Villiputhur Azhwar translated the Mahabharata into Tamil. His work is Villibharatham. It contains the section on Arjuna's Pilgrimage. Then, in the Vana Parva, there is Arjuna's penance. Later, during the Mahabharata war, in the 13th day of battle, there are also references to Arjuna's penance. Generally, there are three epics: Ramayana, Mahabharata, and Sivaragasyam. Among these, the Ramayana and Mahabharata are epics that subtly indicate the supremacy of Shiva. Sivaragasyam is an epic that explicitly describes the supremacy of Shiva. Therefore, all the characters in the Ramayana and Mahabharata have a history of performing Shiva Puja. The Pancha Pandavas in the Mahabharata were Shiva devotees. Among them, Arjuna's devotion to Shiva was particularly superior. In the Mahabharata, there is a section called Arjuna's Pilgrimage. On one occasion, Arjuna happened to see his elder brother Dharma and Draupadi alone. Therefore, it was a rule that he had to undertake a pilgrimage as atonement. So, Arjuna undertook a pilgrimage, bathed in various sacred rivers, and visited various Shiva and Vishnu temples. This history is described in the Adi Parva of Villibharatham, in the section on Arjuna's Pilgrimage. Arjuna, who set out on a pilgrimage from Indraprastha, bathed in sacred rivers like the Ganga and Yamuna. He traveled east and visited all the sacred places there. Then he came to Dakshina Bhumi, South India.

He bathed in rivers like Godavari and Krishnaveni and had darshan of Srisailam. In the south, he had darshan of Thiruvengadam and Thirukalathi, which are on the border of Tamil Nadu. He reached Kanchipuram. In Kanchipuram, one of the seven cities that grant liberation, he had darshan of Ekambaranathar, Kamakshi Amman, and Kumarakottam, where Lord Muruga is enshrined. In Hastigiri, he had darshan of Mahavishnu. Then he had darshan of Thiruvannamalai, where the world-renowned Annamalaiyar resides. He bathed in the Pennai River, reached Thirukkovalur, and had darshan of Veeratannar and Perumal there. He had darshan of Lord Shiva in the Shiva temples of Thiruvennainallur and Thiruvathigai. In Thiruvahindrapuram, he praised and had darshan of Lord Vishnu. Then he came to Chidambaram and had darshan of Lord Nataraja. He came to Srirangam and worshipped the feet of Namberumal. Similarly, he bathed in many sacred waters and came to Madurai. There, he worshipped Lord Shiva and Angayarkanni Amman and married Chitrangada. Then, in Thirumaliruncholai, he had darshan of Sundararaja Perumal, also known as Azhagar or Kallazhagar. Then he reached Sethu and bathed in the sacred waters of Rameswaram. He bathed in Kumari Thurai, also known as Kanni Theertham or Kanyakumari. Then he had darshan of all the temples established by Parasurama. He had darshan of Thirukokarna Kshetra and then reached Dwaraka. Thus, in Arjuna's pilgrimage, he had darshan of numerous Shiva and Vishnu temples. These are the events that occur in the Adi Parva of the Mahabharata. Similarly, during the Mahabharata war, on the 13th day, there are accounts of Arjuna's Shiva Puja and Shiva penance. To defeat Jayadratha, a great devotee of Shiva, Krishna took Arjuna to Mount Kailash to seek boons and weapons from Lord Shiva. On the way, Arjuna felt hungry. He asked, "I will not eat without performing Shiva Puja. How can I perform Shiva Puja now?" Then, there are references in Villibharatham that he performed Shiva Puja according to the Agamic tradition. He reached Mount Kailash and performed penance on its slopes. Lord Shiva was pleased with his penance and granted him the desired boons and weapons. Now, the third and important part is the event that occurs in the Vana Parva. This event forms the basis for Arjuna's birth as Thinnanar. This is found in the Thirukalathi Puranam. "Thus, Arjuna, as a hunter, performed the puja of the foremost Lord, and then, by the grace of Kalathinathar, who shares half with the Goddess, attained high liberation," states the Thirukalathi Puranam. Among the five Pandavas, Arjuna performed penance to obtain the unparalleled Pasupatha Astra from Lord Shiva, to use against his enemies, Duryodhana and others, and defeat them. He performed penance on the Indraneela Parvat. He bathed in the sacred river, wore a clean cloth, smeared sacred ash all over his body according to his vows, wore a rudraksha garland, chanted the Shiva Panchakshara Mantra, sat in a noble posture, directed his breath through the Sushumna instead of Ida and Pingala, transcended the six chakras, and performed intense penance to achieve mental absorption in the form of light in the Brahmarandhra. While Arjuna was performing such penance, Mookasura, sent by Duryodhana to kill Arjuna, took the form of a boar and went to the forest where Arjuna was performing penance, causing trouble to the sages and other great people there. At the same time, seeing Arjuna performing penance on Mount Kailash, Ambika's heart softened. Seeing Arjuna performing severe penance on one leg, the Mother's heart softened. Therefore, Parvati Devi, the Universal Mother, appealed to Lord Shiva, "O Lord, please bestow your grace upon Arjuna, who is performing severe penance." After Uma Devi, the grace-power of Lord Shiva, appealed to bestow grace upon Arjuna, the Lord immediately decided to bestow grace upon Arjuna. Lord Shiva resolved that Arjuna's penance should not be disturbed by Mookasura, who was sent by Duryodhana in the form of a boar. Therefore, Lord Shiva assumed the form of a Kirata (hunter) to bestow grace upon Arjuna. The Lord came as a hunter. Lord Shiva, as a hunter, wore sandals on his feet, tied a tiger skin, tucked a sword, adorned himself with various beads, Kuntri mani, conch shells, Athi flowers, and Konrai flowers, carried a quiver, and held a bow and arrow, coming as Kirata Murthy, in the form of a hunter. Uma Devi also took the form of a huntress, befitting the hunter's guise assumed by Lord Shiva, and came with Lord Shiva. Because, "In whatever form the Lord stands, in that form she also stands," is from Sivagnana Siddhiyar, isn't it? Therefore, whatever guise the Lord assumes, the Mother, as the power of grace, will assume a similar guise. The scriptures speak of the nine forms of the Lord as "Navam Tharum Bhedam." In all these nine forms of the Lord, the Goddess also appears. When the Lord is Parasiva, the Mother is Parasakti. When the Lord is Shiva, the Mother is Sakti. When the Lord takes the form of Sadashiva, the Mother comes as Manonmani. When the Lord is Maheswara, the Mother is Maheswari, and when the Lord is Rudra, the Mother is Uma. When the Lord is Thirumal, the Mother is Lakshmi, and when the Lord is Brahma, the Mother is Vani. This is the form that Shiva-Saktis take at the highest level of Tattvas. Not only that, as in this story, if the Lord comes as a hunter, the Mother comes as a huntress, befitting the hunter's guise assumed by the Lord. In the Purana of Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, when the Lord worked in the field disguised as a farmer, the Mother also stayed with the Lord as a farmer's wife, a woman from the farmer's community. As the Lord and Goddess came as a hunter and huntress, Mookasura, the boar, came roaring before the Lord. Lord Shiva, without any anger, playfully shot a sharp arrow. It struck the boar. The boar squealed and ran. The Lord followed behind. The boar ran across the hermitage of the sages. All the sages were frightened by this. Seeing this, Arjuna, who was performing penance there, angrily shot an arrow at the boar. The boar fell and died. At that moment, Lord Shiva, who had come in the form of a hunter, also arrived there. "I killed this boar," said Lord Shiva in the hunter's form. Arjuna, however, said, "I killed this boar." This led to a fight between Arjuna and Lord Shiva. Both exchanged arrows. In this fight, the string of Arjuna's bow broke. Then, an angry Arjuna struck Lord Shiva on his sacred head with the bow. Lord Shiva accepted that blow, as if it struck all the worlds. Lord Shiva bore Arjuna's blow on his sacred head, which even Brahma could not see. He did so to show that he is easily accessible to his devotees. Then, Arjuna and Lord Shiva wrestled for a long time. Lord Shiva considered Arjuna's fighting as a game played with a child and affectionately touched and played with him. Finally, Lord Shiva revealed his true form. Lord Shiva appeared to Arjuna as Nilakantha, with four arms, accompanied by Uma, mounted on a bull.

Arjuna, realizing that it was Lord Shiva, trembled, sweated, and his mind weakened. His love overflowed, pouring out as tears of joy. He prostrated himself on the ground many times, becoming bewildered. Then, with a somewhat clear mind, he said, "O Lord, please forgive the faults I have committed." Hearing this, Lord Shiva graciously said to Arjuna, "Do not fear. Ask for the boon you desire. We will grant that boon." Arjuna, bowing respectfully, stood up and appealed to Lord Shiva, "I have suffered in the ocean of birth. Please grant me liberation so that I may be saved." To this, Lord Shiva replied, "Arjuna, you performed penance to obtain the Pasupatha Astra to kill all your enemies, didn't you? Therefore, first receive that Pasupatha Astra." He continued, "You performed penance for the Pasupatha Astra, so we have given it to you now. Now you are asking for liberation. Therefore, be born in the next life in Southern Kailash. Then we will grant you liberation." He added, "You spoke disparagingly of me as a hunter just now, didn't you? Therefore, in your next birth, you will be the king of hunters, reach Southern Kailash, and there, with great devotion and joy, worship the Shiva Lingam, which is the embodiment of boundless love, and feed me with the flesh and ambrosia of animals like boars. When you do so, we will grant you liberation." Lord Shiva then blessed him and disappeared. It is for this reason that Arjuna, in this birth, incarnated as Thinnanar, was born as the chief of the hunter tribe on Kalathi mountain, and even though he did not receive education or knowledge, he is destined to worship Lord Shiva and attain liberation according to the grace bestowed by Lord Shiva. These are the details about Kannappa Nayanar's previous birth found in the Thirukalathi Puranam. When he performed penance as Arjuna, a boar came, and a fight ensued between them, didn't it? Similarly, a boar here also served as an instrument, by the grace of God, to connect the Lord and Thinnanar, the reincarnation of Arjuna. This is the penance Arjuna performed in his previous lives. It is due to the accumulation of that penance that such great love and desire for the Lord now overflows in Thinnanar. Thinnanar did not perform penance only in his previous birth as Arjuna. He performed numerous penances in births prior to Arjuna as well. Because in Arjuna's previous births, he was Nara, one of the Nara-Narayanas. These very Nara-Narayanas came as Arjuna and Krishna during the Mahabharata period. Arjuna also received Shiva Diksha and performed numerous Shiva Pujas. He received the Pasupatha Astra. In his next birth, he was blessed to incarnate as Thinnanar. Sri Krishna also received Shiva Diksha from Upamanyu Muni. In the Anushasanika Parva of Sri Mahabharata, Chapter 17, there are details of Upamanyu Muni performing Shiva Diksha for Sri Krishna, the birth of children for Sri Krishna by the grace of Lord Shiva, and Upamanyu Muni teaching Sri Shiva Sahasranamam to Sri Krishna. Therefore, Arjuna was a severe ascetic not only in his birth as Arjuna but also in his previous births. All these penances combined have now led to his incarnation as Thinnanar, and Thinnanar now stands as an embodiment of love. This is due to the fruit of his previous births, because such a great state of love is unlikely to arise from penance performed in just one birth. Furthermore, Thiruvalluvar also states in the chapter on penance, "Penance is for those who have penance; it is folly for those who do not have it to undertake it." Thiruvalluvar beautifully states that one must have performed penance even to perform penance. Only those who have desired to perform penance and have performed it get the opportunity to do so. Therefore, the penance performed by Arjuna as Nara among the Nara-Narayanas in those times, and the Shiva penance performed in many births, and the Shiva Punya by which Lord Shiva appeared as Kirata Murthy and blessed Arjuna's penance when he was Arjuna—all these combined, "the accumulation of past penance," have come to Thinnanar in this birth, and as a result, Sekkizhar Swamigal sings that he stands as an embodiment of love. This history of Arjuna receiving the Pasupatham also appears in many places in the Thirumurais. In many Shiva temples in South India, there are still historical records of Shiva Lingams established and Shiva Pujas performed by the Pancha Pandavas and Arjuna. However, here in the Periya Puranam, Sekkizhar Swamigal has only briefly mentioned the history of Thinnanar's previous birth, without elaborating. Generally, Sekkizhar Swamigal refers to it as "the accumulation of past penance" in a general sense. This is because it is Sekkizhar Swamigal's tradition. Similarly, in the Purana of Thirunavukkarasu Swamigal and others, Sekkizhar Swamigal only hints at the previous births of those Nayanars. This is because those histories should be narrated in their respective Puranas. It is a rule that we should learn those histories from their respective Puranas. Therefore, Sekkizhar Swamigal only provides hints and arranged for us to read the related history in the Puranas associated with them. However, we might wonder why he narrated the past and future history of Sundaramoorthy Swamigal in the Thirumalai Sarukkam and Vellanaich Sarukkam. This is because Sundaramoorthy Swamigal is the central figure of this Periya Puranam epic. Therefore, it was necessary to narrate the history of the origin of the Thiruthondathogai Thiruppathigam, which is the source of this Purana, and the history of the Purana itself. Since it was indispensable, Sekkizhar Swamigal sang it that way. Similarly, Kochengatchozha Nayanar is a slight exception to this tradition of Sekkizhar. To explain the reason for the history of Kochengatchozha Nayanar, who, by the grace of Adimoorthy, was born with prior knowledge, ruled, and built all the Shiva temples as Maadakkoils (temples inaccessible to elephants), Sekkizhar Swamigal extensively narrated the history of Kochengatchozha Nayanar's previous birth there. Therefore, Sekkizhar Swamigal has only extensively sung about the previous and future history of Sundaramoorthy Swamigal and the previous birth of Kochengatchozha Nayanar. This is because they were indispensable for the Purana. For other Nayanars like Thirugnanasambandha Peruman, Thirunavukkarasu Peruman, and Kannappa Nayanar, whom we are currently studying, Sekkizhar Swamigal has only hinted at their previous births. This is Sekkizhar Swamigal's tradition. This history of the Nayanar's previous birth is found in the Thirukalathi Puranam. It is also found in the 11th Thirumurai, in Nakkirar Deva Nayanar's work "Kailai Paathi Kalathi Paathi" and in "Pottri Thirukkali Venba." Therefore, as the Lord willed, Arjuna was born as a hunter, Thinnanar, and reached Thirukalathi. The slopes of Mount Kailash where he previously performed penance are now the slopes of Thirukalathi mountain where he performs penance. When he was Arjuna, a boar served as an aid to see the Lord in that state of penance. Similarly, here, a boar came as an aid to see Kalathiappar and led Thinnanar. Thirukalathi mountain is Kailash mountain, according to the Thirukalathi Puranam. "He who is on the peak of Kailash is Kalathian, he is within my eyes," is from Thirumurai. Therefore, understanding all these connections, Thinnanar's Shiva penance and Shiva Punya from all his previous births combined, he has now become an embodiment of love, has great love for the Lord, and is now going to attain liberation. These are the histories of Thinnanar's previous births. Let's continue our reflection. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 21 — Kannappa Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Periya Puranam, Part 144. Ilaimazhindha Sarukkam, Kannappa Nayanar Puranam. We saw that Sekkizhar Swamigal composed three verses describing Thinnanar's ascent of Thirukalathi mountain. We have already examined the meanings of the first verse. Now, the second verse: "Nanan and love went ahead, ascending the cool mountain. He, the Lord, ascended the tall, stable mountain, as if climbing the ever-increasing steps of cherished Tattvas, to reach Shiva, who is the command." This is the sacred verse. Nanan and love go before Thinnanar. Love, which grows as enthusiasm, passion, and yearning, is the activity of Iccha Shakti (will-power). With this Iccha Shakti, this love, going ahead, and Nanan also going ahead, Thinnanar climbs the mountain. His ascent of Thirukalathi mountain, described by the phrase "climbing the ever-increasing steps of cherished Tattvas," implies that just as Shivayogis arrange the 36 Tattvas, from earth to Shiva, as 36 steps, and ascend these Tattvas, transcending them to reach Shiva, similarly, Kannappa Nayanar's ascent of the mountain, his ascent of Thirukalathi mountain, signifies Thinnanar progressively transcending the 36 Tattvas to reach Lord Shiva. Thinnanar's ascent of Thirukalathi mountain is a symbol of transcending the 36 Tattvas. Why does Sekkizhar Swamigal call these Tattvas "cherished Tattvas" (Peṇu Tattvas)? "Peṇudal" means to nourish or foster. Tattvas, in the Sakala state, serve as instruments for the growth of knowledge, will, and action of souls, fostering the knowledge, will, and action of souls. Therefore, they are called "cherished Tattvas." "Peṇudal" also means to worship. Worshipping Tattvas means worshipping Tattvas in the Padmasana Puja of Shiva Puja, and in the Adhva Sodhana of Nirvaana Diksha. These methods are prescribed in the Agamas. Devotees can learn and understand these through initiation and instruction from qualified Shivacharyas. Here, Sekkizhar Swamigal says that Thinnanar ascends the steps of Tattvas, doesn't he? These Tattvas are the 36 Tattvas from Prithvi Tattva to Shiva Tattva. Therefore, ascending each Tattva from Prithvi, transcending all Tattvas, and reaching the Supreme Being who is beyond Tattvas, is what is being described here. Now, let's briefly reflect on these 36 Tattvas, which are an important aspect of Saiva Siddhanta. We saw that the impurities of souls, called Malas, are three: Anava, Karma, and Maya. To experience the fruits of actions, the Lord creates and provides souls with Tanu (body), Karana (organs), Bhuvana (worlds), and Bhoga (experiences) from Maya. Therefore, the Lord creates these 36 Tattvas from Moola Maya. Each religion has Tattvas according to its philosophical principles. In Saiva Siddhanta, these are stated to be 36 Tattvas. What is Maya in Saiva Siddhanta? Maya is the primordial cause of the universe, eternal, insentient, the parigraha shakti (instrumental power) of the First Cause, pervading the First Cause, the ultimate reality, and formless. "Ma" means to dissolve, "Ya" means to arise. Therefore, that which is the cause for all effects to dissolve into it and for all effects to arise from it,

That is called Maya. This Maya is generally of two types: Suddha Maya and Asuddha Maya. Suddha Maya has other names like Maamaya, Kudilai, and Kundalini. Asuddha Maya has many names like Adhomaya, Maya, and Mohini. Suddha Maya is the cause of the pure universe, and Asuddha Maya is the cause of the impure universe. This second type, Asuddha Maya, is further divided into Suddhasuddha Maya and Asuddha Maya. Therefore, Tattvas are of three types: Suddha Tattvas, Suddhasuddha or Misra Tattvas, and Asuddha Tattvas. The third type, Asuddha Tattvas, are also called Prakriti Tattvas. Among these, the first, Suddha Tattvas, consist of five Tattvas.
