Suddhasuddha Tattvas are seven, and Asuddha Tattvas or Prakriti Tattvas are 24, making a total of 36 Tattvas. The five Suddha Tattvas are Shiva, Shakti, Sadashiva, Ishwara, and Suddhavidya. These are also called Shiva Tattvas. Now, the Suddhasuddha Tattvas or Vidya Tattvas are seven. They are Asuddha Maya, Kala (time), Niyati (order), Kala (art/knowledge), Vidya (pure knowledge), Raga (attachment), and Purusha (soul). Next comes the third type of Tattvas, the Prakriti Tattvas, which are 24. They are Moola Tattva or Guna, Buddhi (intellect), Ahankara (ego), Manas (mind), Mei (skin), Vaai (mouth), Kan (eye), Mookku (nose), Sevi (ear), Vaak (speech), Paadam (feet), Paani (hands), Paayu (anus), Upastham (genitals), Sabda (sound), Sparsa (touch), Roopa (form), Rasa (taste), Gandha (smell), Akasha (ether), Vayu (air), Agni (fire), Neer (water), Nilam (earth). Thus, the Shiva Tattvas, which are Suddha Maya Tattvas, are five. The Vidya Tattvas, which are Suddhasuddha Tattvas, are seven. The Atma Tattvas, which are Prakriti Tattvas, are 24, making a total of 36 Tattvas. When explaining these Tattvas, explaining from Suddha Tattvas to Prithvi Tattva is the creative order (Srishti Krama). Explaining from Prithvi Tattva to Suddha Tattvas is the dissolution order (Odukkam Krama). When the Lord creates, He creates one by one from the Suddha Tattvas above. But when souls living in the Prithvi Tattva transcend the Tattvas, they must ascend from the Prithvi Tattva below to the Shiva Tattva above. Therefore, when great people explain these Tattvas, there is a method of explaining them in the Srishti Krama. According to that, we will look at their explanations. We will briefly look at these 36 Tattvas. This brief explanation is taken from an article titled "Tattvas and Taattvikas" written by Thuthukudi Ramachangu Pandian Ayya. Devotees who wish to read their detailed explanations can learn them systematically from Saiva Siddhanta Gnana Shastras like Sivagnana Bodham, Sivagnana Siddhiyar, Sivaprakasam, Thiruvarutpayan, and Unmai Vilakkam. First, the first Tattva in Suddha Maya is Shiva Tattva. This is the first vritti (modification) of Suddha Maya, activated by the general power of knowledge (Gnana Shakti). It is presided over by Shiva, who stands as pure knowledge. It is the locus of the subtle (Sukshma) Para Vak. It is also named Laya, Nishkala, Shaktiman, and Nada. Souls with only subtle mala impressions reside in the Bhuvanas and Bhogas (worlds and experiences) within this Shiva Tattva. Next, the second Tattva is Shakti Tattva. This is presided over by the activity of Kriya Shakti (power of action). It is the locus of the Pashyanti Vak. It is also named Thoola Laya, Thoola Nishkala, and Bindu. It is the locus for Muktas (liberated souls) who have only gross Laya impressions, and for the Bhuvanas and Bhogas where they reside. Now, the third Tattva is Sadashiva Tattva. This Sadashiva Tattva is activated by the special type of Gnana Shakti and Kriya Shakti. It is presided over by Shiva, who is called Sadashiva. It is the locus of the Madhyama Vak. It also has names like Udyuttam, Bhogam, and Sakala Nishkala. It is the locus for Sadashiva Murthy, for the Shiva Agamas that stand as the form of Madhyama Vak, and for Pranavas and others who are the cause for the classification of those Shiva Agamas, and for Anusadashivas and others, and for their Tanu, Karana, Bhuvana, and Bhogas. Now, the fourth Tattva is Ishwara Tattva. This Ishwara Tattva is the fourth vritti of Suddha Maya, subtly activated by greatly exerting Kriya Shakti. It is presided over by Shiva, who stands as Ishwara. It is the locus of Sukshma Vaikari. It has names like Sukshma Adhikara, Sukshma Pravritti, and Sukshma Sakala. This Ishwara Tattva is the locus for the eight Vidyaswaras, such as Ananta Devar, and for the Rudras who have attained that state, and for their Tanu, Karana, Bhuvana, and Bhogas. Next, the fifth Tattva is Suddha Vidya Tattva. This is the fifth vritti of Suddha Maya, grossly activated by greatly exerting Gnana Shakti. It is presided over by Shiva, who stands as Ishwara, the cause of Vidya. It is the locus of Thoola Vaikari Vak. It has names like Thoola Ishwara, Thoola Adhikara, Thoola Pravritti, and Thoola Sakala. This Tattva is the locus for Ishwara, for the Shiva Agamas that stand as the form of Thoola Vaikari, for the seven crore Mantras, for the Ganas like Nandi, for the nine Shaktis like Vama and Jyeshta, and for Rudra, Mal, Ayan, Indra, and others who have attained liberation and have only mala impressions, and who activate the lower Rudra, Mal, Ayan, Indra, and others, and for their Tanu, Karanas, etc. Thus, we have seen the five Suddha Vidya Tattvas. Now, let's look at the seven Suddhasuddha Maya or Vidya Tattvas. The sixth is Asuddha Maya Tattva. This generally deludes souls bound by karma. Therefore, it is also called Mohini. Now, the seventh Tattva is Kala Tattva (Time). This Kala Tattva defines the experiences for souls who have gone to experience Bhogas, by various divisions of time such as Kanam, Lavasam, Thudi, etc. This Kala Tattva expands from the small unit of time called Kanam to the lifespan of Brahma, Vishnu, and Rudra. Next, the eighth Tattva is Niyati Tattva (Order). This Niyati Tattva ordains that one must experience the fruits of one's actions. The ninth Tattva is Kala (Art/Knowledge). This Tattva removes a little of the mala shakti and manifests kriya shakti for the experience of bhoga for souls that were obscured by the primordial impurity of Anava Mala, lying as if void. These three Tattvas—Kala, Niyati, and Kala—arise from Asuddha Maya Tattvas. Next, the tenth Tattva is Vidya (Pure Knowledge). This arises from Kala. It illuminates the knowledge power of souls. This Vidya then combines with the subsequent Tattva, Buddhi Tattva, and functions. The eleventh Tattva is Raga (Attachment). This arises from Vidya. It illuminates and functions the Iccha Shakti (will-power) of souls. The twelfth Tattva is Purusha (Soul). The state of the soul, which is connected to these five, from Kala to Raga, and is inwardly directed towards experience, is called Purusha. Thus, we have seen the seven Suddhasuddha Tattvas. Including the first five Suddha Tattvas, we have seen 12 Tattvas. Now, the 24 Prakriti Maya or Atma Tattvas. Let's look at them. The thirteenth Tattva is Moolaprakriti. This arises from Kala. It is also called Prakriti, Pradhana, Maan, and Avyakta. It is the cause of the five kleshas (afflictions) like Avichai (ignorance). The three Gunas—Sattva, Rajas, and Tamas—stand in equilibrium here in Moolaprakriti. From this, Guna Tattva arises. The state where the three Gunas, which are modifications of Moolaprakriti, stand in equilibrium, is the Guna Tattva. The fourteenth Tattva is Buddhi Tattva (Intellect). This Buddhi is produced from Guna Tattva. The state where Sattva predominates among the three Gunas—Sattva, Rajas, and Tamas—and Rajas and Tamas are diminished, is called Buddhi. The function of Buddhi is to ascertain. This Buddhi is what helps us to ascertain any matter. Therefore, this Buddhi Tattva is the support for the merits and demerits, which are the fruits of good and bad deeds performed by the soul. Next, the fifteenth Tattva is Ahankara (Ego). This Ahankara arises when Rajas predominates in Buddhi Tattva, and Sattva and Tamas are diminished. Its function is to stimulate the soul with the surge of "I will ascertain this and that in such and such a way." This Ahankara is of three types: Thaisatha Ahankara, Vaikarika Ahankara, and Bhootadi Ahankara. Thaisatha Ahankara arises when Sattva predominates and the other two are diminished. Vaikarika Ahankara arises when Rajas predominates and the other two are diminished. Bhootadi Ahankara arises when Tamas predominates and the other two are diminished. From Thaisatha Ahankara, Manas (mind) and the five Gnana Indriyas (sense organs of knowledge) arise. From Vaikarika Ahankara, the five Karma Indriyas (organs of action) arise. Next, the sixteenth Tattva is Manas (Mind). This arises from Sattvic Ahankara and has two functions: Sankalpa (conception) and Vikalpa (doubt). After the mind arises, the five Gnana Indriyas, which were mentioned earlier, arise from Sattvic Ahankara. Now let's look at those Gnana Indriyas. The seventeenth Tattva is Sevi (Ear). This, having Akasha (ether) as its locus, perceives the Tanmatra called Sabda (sound). The eighteenth Tattva is Thol (Skin). This, having Vayu (air) as its locus, perceives Sparsa (touch). The nineteenth Tattva is Kan (Eye). This, having Agni (fire) as its locus, perceives the Tanmatra called Roopa (form). The twentieth Tattva is Naakku (Tongue). This, having Appu (water) as its locus, perceives the Tanmatra called Rasa (taste). The twenty-first Tattva is Mookku (Nose). This, having Prithvi (earth) as its locus, perceives the Tanmatra called Gandha (smell). Now, from the Rajasic Ahankara mentioned earlier, the five Karma Indriyas—Vaak (speech), Paadam (feet), Paani (hands), Paayu (anus), and Upastham (genitals)—arise in order. These function, presided over by the soul's Kriya Shakti. They are as follows: The twenty-second Tattva is Vaai (Mouth). Its function is speaking, speech. The twenty-third Tattva is Kaal (Foot). Its function is moving. This is called Gamana. The twenty-fourth Tattva is Kai (Hand). Its functions are giving and receiving donations. The twenty-fifth Tattva is Paayuru (Anus). Its function is excretion. The twenty-sixth Tattva is Upastham (Genitals). Its function is pleasure. Now, the five Tanmatras and five elements that arise from Bhootadi Ahankara. The twenty-seventh Tattva is Sabda (Sound). This is called sound. The twenty-eighth Tattva is Sparsa (Touch). This is the sensation of touch. The twenty-ninth Tattva is Roopa (Form). This is the Tattva of light. The thirtieth Tattva is Rasa (Taste). This is the Tattva, the Tanmatra, called taste. The thirty-first Tattva is Gandha (Smell). This is the Tanmatra called smell. From these Tanmatras, the five elements arise in order. The thirty-second is Akasha Bhutam (Ether element). This arises from the Tanmatra called Sabda. The thirty-third Tattva is Vayu (Air). This arises from the Tanmatra called Sparsa. The thirty-fourth Tattva is Tejas or Theyu (Fire). This arises from the Tanmatra called Roopa. The thirty-fifth Tattva is Appu or Jalam (Water). This arises from the Tanmatra called Rasa. The thirty-sixth Tattva is Prithvi (Earth). This arises from the Tanmatra called Gandha. Thus, there are a total of 36 Tattvas. The order we have just seen is the order of creation. In the order of dissolution, the Tattvas dissolve into their causal Tattvas in the same order as they arose from them. Thus, the creation of these 36 Tattvas from Maya, the primordial substance, in a subtle to gross order, is called Srishti. Maintaining this is called Sthiti. Just as they arose, the effect Tattvas dissolve into their respective causal Tattvas, and finally, the gross Tattvas all dissolve into Moolamaya. This is called Samhara. Therefore, the Lord performs the three functions of Srishti, Sthiti, and Samhara within Maya. The soul, born in the worlds appropriate to these Maya Tattvas, experiences karmas, undergoes experiences, and then, by the grace of the Lord, following the path of the Shiva Agamas, removes its karmas (i.e., removes the Karma Mala), transcends these 36 Tattvas, starting from Prithvi up to Shiva Tattva, and by doing so, removes the Karma Mala and Maya Mala, thereby completely weakening the Anava Mala, and finally attaining the Supreme Being. This is what Saiva Siddhanta calls liberation. Sekkizhar Swamigal states that Thinnanar's ascent of Kalathi mountain, symbolizing the 36 Tattvas as 36 steps, is like transcending these 36 Tattvas as 36 steps. Therefore, Thinnanar's ascent of Kalathi mountain is a symbol of his transcending the 36 Tattvas and attaining the Supreme Being. This is what Saiva Siddhanta Shastras and Thirumurais explain. Shivayogis transcend these 36 Tattvas, from Prithvi to Shiva, as steps, remove these Tattvas, and unite with Shiva, attaining Shiva. "Is there a day when you and I will stand in the space beyond the elements, senses, five sense organs, the three qualities, and the sound?" is a sacred verse by Pattinatthadigal. "Is there a way for this humble servant to attain the state beyond the six sixes?" is the 47th verse of Kandar Anubhuti. Arunagirinathar Peruman sings here, "beyond the six sixes," doesn't he? Six sixes mean 36. Therefore, Arunagirinathar Peruman sings, "When will this humble servant attain the state beyond these 36?" Thinnanar ascends these 36 Tattvas as steps when he climbs Thirukalathi mountain. Let's continue to reflect on the meaning of this second verse. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 22 — Kannappa Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Periya Puranam, Part 145, Ilai Malindha Sarukkam, Kannappa Nayanar Puranam. We are contemplating the meaning of the three verses describing Kannappa Nayanar ascending the Thirukalathi mountain. We are currently contemplating the second verse. We have already pondered the meaning up to "Gnananum Anbum munbu nalirvarai era thaamum penu thathuvangal enum perugu sopaanam eri." We saw that Thinnanar ascending the Thirukalathi mountain is a symbol of him transcending the 36 Tattvas. Now, in the next two lines of this verse, Sekkizhar Peruman describes what Thinnanar does after ascending these 36 Tattvas. He says, "Penu thathuvangal enum perugu sopaanam eri aanaiyaam Sivathai saara anaibavar pola Aiyar neelnilai malai malaiyai eri nerpada sellu pothil." The Nayanar ascends the steps of the Tattvas, approaching Shiva, who is the embodiment of grace. "Anaithal" means to unite with Shiva. "Saara Anaithal" means that the Nayanar, leaving behind his previous attachments and the state of being one with ego, now unites with Shiva, who is true knowledge. Thus, he approaches Shiva. The verse states, "Aanaiyaam Sivanai saara anaithal." "Aanaiyaam Sivam" means Shiva who is grace. Here, "aanai" means grace. Shiva is the pure consciousness that stands without regard for external objects. This Shiva removes the "pasutvam" (animalistic nature) from souls and creates "Sivatvam" (Shiva-nature), doesn't it? That compassion, that mercy, is called grace. Therefore, he said "Aanaiyaam Sivam," meaning Shiva who is grace. When Shiva comes to bestow grace upon souls, it is out of compassion, based on compassion. That is why Sekkizhar Peruman uses the epithet "Aanaiyaam" here. Grace itself is the power of God. We worship the divine grace of God as Ambika. When God is in His true form (Swaroopa Nilai), He is Shiva Himself. When He has compassion for souls and comes to bestow grace upon them, He descends through the stages of formless (Aruvam), semi-form (Aruvuruvam), and form (Uruvam) to bless the souls. Thus, the God who is in His true form, out of compassion for souls, descends from that state in three stages – formless, semi-form, and form – and that compassion is what we celebrate as Shakti. The divine grace of God is Shakti. Therefore, these three forms that God takes are not like the bodies we have. He takes His divine grace as His form. Why does He take His divine grace as His form? Because if He did not come in this way, if God did not come with these forms from His true state, souls would not attain salvation. Therefore, for the sake of souls, for their salvation, God descends in these three stages. When He does so, He bestows the Vedas and Agamas upon souls. This is also solely due to His compassion for souls. The 66th verse of Sivagnana Siddhiyar states this: "Aaranam Agamangal Arulinaal Uruvu Kondu Kaaranan Arulaan Agil Kathippavar Illai Aagum. Naaranan Muthalaai Ulla Surar Narar Naagarkku Ellam Seerani Guru Santhana Cheythiyum Sendridaave." Therefore, we see in many places in Periya Puranam that whenever God comes to bestow grace upon souls, He comes with Shakti. The 68th verse of Sivagnana Siddhiyar also states this: "Mun Arul Sakthi Thanpaal Mugilgum Thaan Mulaiyaan." The commentary on this is that God always appears through Shakti and does not appear independently. Therefore, when asked who the God of Saivism is, the wise answer, "Shiva with Shakti." Therefore, anyone who transcends the stages of the Tattvas and attains Shiva does so only through the divine grace of God, through His great compassion, and attains that state of liberation. That is what Sekkizhar Swamigal states in this verse. "Gnananum Anbum munbu nalir varai era thaamum penu thathuvangal enum perugu sopaanam eri aanaiyaam Sivathai saara anaibavar pola" – we have understood the meaning up to this point. Now, Sekkizhar Swamigal continues, "Aiyar neelnilai malaiyai eri nerpada sellum pothil." He says, "Aiyar neelnilai malaiyai eri." Sekkizhar Swamigal refers to the Kalathi mountain as Aiyar Malai. "Aiyar" means a person of greatness. It also means a sage who has conquered the five senses. Therefore, "Aiyar Malai" means the mountain where sages live. Sages live and perform penance on this Thirukalathi mountain. This can be seen in many places, including the Sthala Puranas. Therefore, this Thirukalathi mountain is the mountain where such ascetic sages live. Hence, he calls it Aiyar Malai. Furthermore, "Aiyar" can also mean God, and thus it can mean the mountain where God resides. He refers to this mountain as "Neelnilai Malai." "Neelnilai" means extending for a long distance in form. The Thirukalathi Puranam, Soota Samhita, and other texts state that this place is five yojanas wide and ten yojanas long. Moreover, it is Mount Kailash itself. Therefore, it also means that it has an imperishable, permanent nature. He says that Thinnanar approaches this mountain "nerpada." "Nerpada" means to encounter, to meet directly, in the sense of attaining the liberation for which Thinnanar was born. Thus, Sekkizhar describes what happens when Thinnanar ascends the Aiyar's mountain "nerpada" in the third verse. This is the 104th verse. He concludes this verse by saying, "Nerpada sellum pothil Thingal ser sadaiyar thammai sendravar kaanaa munne Anganar karunai koorndha arul thiru nokkam eytha Thangiya pavathin munnai saarbu vittu agala neengi Pongiya oliyin neezhal poruvil anbu uruvamaanar." As Thinnanar ascends this Thirukalathi mountain, and as he goes to see Shiva there, Shiva's merciful and gracious gaze falls upon him. It falls upon Thinnanar. Because of this, all of Thinnanar's previous attachments leave him. Thinnanar, having abandoned all worldly and birth-related attachments, became the embodiment of love in the shadow of God's grace. Thinnanar ascends the Thirukalathi mountain and runs, goes, to see God there. Even before he goes to see God, God's gracious gaze falls upon Thinnanar. Even before he sees God on the mountaintop, the gracious divine gaze of God, who is on the mountaintop, falls upon Thinnanar. Didn't we see in the first verse that love goes first, and Thinnanar follows? As an echo of the love that went ahead, the grace that came from God above falls upon Thinnanar. Therefore, Sekkizhar Peruman refers to God here with the epithet "Anganar." "Anganar" means one with beautiful eyes. The beauty of the eye is its compassion. "Kannottam" means compassion. The true beauty of the eye is indeed compassion. Therefore, "Anganar" means one with eyes full of compassion. Thus, the compassionate and gracious gaze falls upon Thinnanar. "Karunai koorndha arul thiru nokkam" means that the "marakkarunai" (unconscious compassion) that had hitherto been feeding the fruits of the two karmas and standing as a form of "thirodhanam" (concealment) changes into "arakkarunai" (righteous compassion) and becomes an excellent gaze. "Arul thiru nokkam" means "chakshu deekshai" (initiation through gaze). The initiation by which the guru gazes upon the disciple to remove his worldly attachments is called "chakshu deekshai." Through this "chakshu deekshai," this divine gaze, the worldly attachments of souls are removed. Therefore, the "thirodhana shakti" (power of concealment) that had hitherto stood now transforms into "arul shakti" (power of grace) and falls upon souls. God bestows such a gracious gaze, a compassionate and gracious gaze, upon Thinnanar. This divine gaze is a continuation of Thinnanar's past life. When Thinnanar was Arjuna in his past life, he was performing penance to obtain the Pasupatha Astra. When he saw God, didn't he ask for liberation? What did God say then? "In this birth, you will receive the Pasupatha Astra. In your next birth, on the Kalathi mountain, I will grant you liberation." Didn't He bless him thus? The continuation of that gracious gaze bestowed by God then is what is happening now upon Thinnanar. Therefore, the reason for God's gracious gaze to fall is Thinnanar's past penance. We should contemplate the verse from Nenju Vidu Thoodu at this point: "Thaan ennai paarthaan pazhaiya vinai pancha malak koththai ellam neethaan ninaiyu veru aakkinaan eththariya thonnootru aruvar payil thokkile thuvakku aruthan kannooru then amudham kaattinaan." The reason why this experience does not happen to everyone who goes to Thirukalathi mountain is their lack of penance. All the penances performed by Thinnanar in his past lives have now converged, and because of that, God's gracious gaze falls upon him. Because of that, he stood there as the embodiment of love. Due to this, the "pavam" (birth) that had hitherto remained with him leaves him. "Pavam" means birth. "Thangiya pavam" means the remaining birth. This means that this birth of Thinnanar was the only one remaining for him. The inner meaning of this is that he will have no more births. Due to this, the "mala," "maya," and "kanmam" (impurities, illusion, karma) that had continuously clung to the soul from eternity have all left Thinnanar. Therefore, this remaining birth for him now becomes a fulfilling birth, and he will now revel in liberation, where there are no more births. This is its meaning. Through God's gracious gaze, the soul's previous attachments will leave it. Thus, as all of Thinnanar's previous attachments left him due to God's grace, he became the embodiment of love in the shadow of the surging light. He mentions "pongiya oliyin neezhal" (shadow of the surging light), doesn't he? Here, "oli" (light) refers to Kalathinathar. "Neezhal" (shadow) refers to His divine feet. Therefore, it means that Thinnanar stood as the embodiment of love under the divine feet of Thirukalathinathar. Sundaramoorthy Swamigal also addressed and sang about Thirukalathinathar as "Oliye" (Oh Light). In the ninth verse of Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's Thevaram, Thirukalathi Thirupathikam, it says, "Kaliyar vandu uraiyum Thirukalathi ullirundha oliye unnaiyallal ini ondrum unarenen." Meditating on God as a great light is prescribed in the Siva Agamas. "Aayiram Gnayiru polum Aayiram neen mudiyanum" is from Appar Swamigal's Thiruvarur Thevaram. This is the eighth verse of Thirunavukkarasu Swamigal's Fourth Thirumurai, Fourth Thirupathikam, Thiruvarur Thirupathikam. The verse is: "Aayiram thamarai polum Aayiram sevadiyanum Aayiram pon varai polum Aayiram tholudaiyanum Aayiram Gnayiru polum Aayiram neen mudiyanum Aayiram per ugandhanum Aroor amarndha ammanee." Here, Thirunavukkarasu Swamigal says, "Aayiram Gnayiru polum." This means that God shines like countless suns. When Thirumurugatrupadai begins, how does it describe Lord Muruga? It describes him as a mass of light. It describes him as the sun. Thirumurugatrupadai begins with "Ulagam uvappa valan erpu thiridharu palar pugazh Gnayiru kadal kandaangu."

How does the sun rise from the sea? The sun rises crimson from the blue sea. Similarly, Lord Muruga, on his blue peacock, with his reddish divine form, shines like the brilliance of a thousand suns. This is the meaning of the opening lines of Thirumurugatrupadai. God has been described as light and the sun in numerous texts. Lights are of three types: Akanda Jyothi, Kandakanda Jyothi, and Kanda Jyothi. Kanda Jyothi refers to the ordinary lamps and other light-emitting objects we see within this world. Kandakanda Jyothi refers to the sun, moon, and stars we see in the sky. Akanda Jyothi is called Shiva Jyothi. Among these, the Shiva Jyothi, which is Akanda Jyothi, is the greatest Jyothi that gives light to all other Jyothis. Shiva Jyothi is called Swayam Jyothi, Param Jyothi, Akilam Jyothi, and so on. Just as soul knowledge is the source for knowledge perceived by the senses, so too is Shiva knowledge the source for soul knowledge. The meaning is that God operates as the life of life, and Shiva, as Shiva knowledge, illuminates soul knowledge within the soul's knowledge. "Jyothiye Sudare Soozh Oli Vilakke" is from Thiruvasagam. "Oliyaai oli adhan oliya oli adhan oliyum thani tharum oliyaagi" is from the Perur Puranam, Niruthappadalam song. Thus, Thinnanar stands as the embodiment of love in the shadow of Kalathinathar, who is the source light, the supreme light, for all lights, in the shadow of His divine feet. They refer to God's divine feet as a shadow, don't they? God's divine feet signify "Thiruvadi Gnanam" (knowledge of the divine feet). God is the primal being who cannot be realized by "pasu gnanam" (animalistic knowledge) or "pasa gnanam" (knowledge of attachments). To realize Him, one can only do so when soul knowledge is immersed in the knowledge of His divine feet. Through that knowledge of the divine feet, all the aggregates of attachments, from earth to sound, will leave the soul. Then, that "pathi gnanam" (knowledge of the Lord), which is the knowledge of the divine feet, will manifest as a cool shadow to the heat of the suffering of birth. That is why Sekkizhar Peruman here refers to God's divine feet as a cool shadow. For Thinnanar too, since the 36 Tattvas were removed and all worldly attachments were removed from him, Thinnanar is now in the cool shadow of Kalathinathar, who is light, having abandoned "pasa gnanam" and "pasu gnanam," and is in the shadow of "pathi gnanam," which is the knowledge of God's divine feet. Therefore, he is the embodiment of love in the shadow of God's divine feet. He has taken love itself as his form. The accumulation of past penance manifested endless, blissful love. Then, through God's gracious gaze, previous attachments left Thinnanar. As all such previous attachments left Thinnanar, he saw the shadow of God's surging light. Because God's gracious gaze fell upon Thinnanar, and because Thinnanar is under God's divine shadow, Thinnanar attained boundless love for God as his form. This is its meaning. The soul has the nature of taking on the color of what it attaches to. Now, the soul is attached to Shiva. Therefore, Thinnanar has taken on the form of love, which is Shiva's form. In the first verse of the "Anbudaimai" (Possession of Love) section of the First Tantra of Thirumandiram, composed by Thirumoola Deva Nayanar, it says, "Anbum Sivanum irandu enbar arivilar. Anbe Sivamavathu aarum arikilar. Anbe Sivamavathu aarum arindha pin Anbe Sivamaai amarndhirundhare." Thinnanar now stands as an embodiment of the grammar of this verse. We will continue to contemplate the inner meanings of the next verses. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 23 — Kannappa Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Periya Puranam, Part 146, Ilai Malindha Sarukkam, Kannappa Nayanar Puranam. The accumulation of Thinnanar's past penance manifested endless, blissful love. Through God's gracious gaze, all his previous attachments left him. Thinnanar was in the shadow of God's surging light. On that Thirukalathi mountain, he became the embodiment of love, taking love as his form. He saw the "Eka Nayakar" (Sole Lord), who was a rising shoot on the vast Thirukalathi mountain. The surging intensity of the great joy and love that arose within him propelled him forward with great speed, and he ran towards Him with intense longing, embraced Him, and stood there smelling Him. This is the next verse. Thinnanar saw the "Eka Nayakar," the Sole Lord, who was a rising shoot on the Thirukalathi mountain, which stood tall and vast, touching the sky. Why does God reside on the peaks of mountains that reach the sky? It is to bestow grace upon the Devas who descend from the heavens to perform worship. Thus, on the Thirukalathi mountain, which stood tall and vast, reaching the sky, God is like a rising shoot on the mountain. He is like a shoot that rises and shines on the mountain peak. "Kozhundhu" (shoot) is the part of plants from which new leaves emerge. In the Thirukalathi Thevaram, Aazhudaiya Nambigal praises Him as "Kozhundhe en gunakkadale" (Oh Shoot, my ocean of virtues). The Thevaram also says "Kottur Kozhundhe." "Saiva Kozhundhe Thavakkadale" is from Kandar Kali Venba. Therefore, the way of addressing God as "Kozhundhu" is found in various Thirumurais and various stotras. Here, the one who is the rising shoot on the mountain is Kudumithevar. He is a Swayambhu Lingam on the mountain peak. Kudumithevar is there as a Sadashiva Murtham, an unmanifested-manifested divine form that arose as a Swayambhu. Shivalingams are of two types: Ishta Lingams and Parartha Lingams. Ishta Lingam is for those who have received Shiva Diksha to perform Aatmartha Puja. Parartha Lingams are Lingams created for Parartha Puja. The book "Saiva Samaya Neri" states that Parartha Lingams are of five types. Among these five types of Parartha Lingams, Swayambhu Lingam is the first. The second is Daivika Lingam, established by Devas. The third is Aarusha Lingam, established by Rishis. The fourth is Gaana Lingam, worshipped by Ganas, meaning established by Ganas like Asuras, Yakshas, Siddhas, etc. The fifth is Manuda Lingam, established by humans. Thus, Parartha Lingams are of five types. Among them, the first is Swayambhu Lingam. Swayambhu Lingam is further of three types. How did Swayambhu Lingam originate? From the countless movements of Anantheshwara's heads, three sparks arose from the fiery anger of Kaalagni Rudra. These three sparks became three Lingams with names like Nirmoolam and Sammoolam. Among them, Nirmoola Lingam is in water. Sammoola Lingam is on earth, based on a mountain. The other Lingam originated from earth and other elements. Thus, the book "Saiva Bhooshanam" states that Swayambhu Lingams are of three types. Here, the one on top of Thirukalathi mountain is Kudumithevar, who is a Swayambhu Murthi. He is there as a rising shoot on the mountain. Thus, Kudumithevar is the rising shoot on the Thirukalathi mountain, which stood tall to the sky. Sekkizhar Peruman sings about this Kudumithevar as "Eka Nayakar" in this verse. Thinnanar saw the "Eka Nayakar," meaning he saw Kudumithevar. "Eka Nayakar" means the unique primal God. "Oruvane Potri Oruvan Enum Oruvan Kaanaka. Eka Nayakanai Imaiayavarkku Arasai" – don't the Tamil Vedas praise Him thus? "Ekan Anekan Iraivan Adi Vaazhga" is from Sivapuranam, Thiruvasagam. Here, Sekkizhar Peruman refers to God as "Eka Nayakar." In Thiruvasagam, Manikkavasagar says, "Ekan Anekan Iraivan Adi Vaazhga." In the book "Thiruvasaga Vishaya Soosanam," the following explanations are given for the term "Ekan" here. "Ekastatha Sarvabhootha Antharaatma" is from Kathopanishad. There are two types of universes: Chit Prapancham (conscious universe) and Jada Prapancham (insentient universe). This Upanishad states that the "Ekan" (One) who is the indwelling spirit of all these Chit Prapanchams and Jada Prapanchams is the only God. The meaning of this is that the Lord, who pervades both the conscious and insentient universes like the sky, is one. To the question of who this "Ekan" (One) God is, the Atharva Siras Upanishad answers. It says, "Eko Rudro Na Dwiteeyaka." This means that Rudra is one, and there is no second Lord besides Him. "Devanam Pathir Ekaka" is from Chandogya Upanishad. This means the One who is the Lord of the Devas. "Tam Aadi Madhya Anta Viheenam Ekam" is from Kaivalya Upanishad. This means the One who has no beginning, middle, or end. "The one God is hidden in all beings" is from Brahma Upanishad. "You are the one Brahman" is from Atharva Siras Upanishad. "Hara, the one God, guides the eternal souls" is from Svetasvatara Upanishad. Thus, from all these scriptural proofs and sayings, it is established that Shiva is the one who exists through the intermingling of all conscious and insentient universes, and that unity (Ekathvam) resides in Him alone. The name "Ekan" is one of Shiva's names, as stated in the Atharva Siras Upanishad. This is stated in the mantra beginning with "Atha Asma." Why is Shiva called "Ekan"? Because He absorbs all worlds into Himself. He then creates, destroys, and protects what He has absorbed. That is why God is called "Ekan." So says the Atharva Siras Upanishad. There are two types of universes, aren't there? Shabda Prapancham (world of sound) and Artha Prapancham (world of meaning). This world of words and world of objects are established with Shiva as their prime cause. They shine by Shiva's very nature. Therefore, the word "Shivamayam" (full of Shiva) is heard in the Vedas and Vedanta. Furthermore, the Sanatkumara Samhita says, "Sarvamayam Shivamayam Proktham Jagadethat Saraasaram," stating that this entire moving and unmoving world is full of Shiva. Therefore, according to the scriptural proofs, it is established that God, Brahman, is one, and that Brahman is Shiva. Thus, we have seen the scriptural proofs for Shiva being the "Eka Nayakan." The twelve Thirumurais, which are the Tamil Vedas, also praise this. "Oruvan enum Oruvan Kaanaka" is from Thiruvasagam, Thiru Andappaguthi. "Oruvane Potri" is from Thiruvasagam, Thirusathakam. "Eka Nayakanai Imaiayavarkku Arasai" is from the Ninth Thirumurai, composed by Thirumaligai Devar. Thirugnanasambandar Peruman says, "Eeraai Mudhal Ondraai endrum Oru Uru Aayine." In the Tenth Thirumurai, Thirumandiram, composed by Thirumoola Deva Nayanar, it says, "Ondru Kandeer Ulagukku Oru Deivamum Ondru Kandeer Ulagukku Uyir Aavathu Nandru Kandeer Ini Namasivaya Pazham Thinru Kandeerkku Ithu Thithiththavaare." Thus, the twelve Thirumurais, which are the Tamil Vedas, conclusively state that God is one, and that God is Shiva. We have seen this stated in the scriptural proofs and in the twelve Thirumurais, which are the Tamil Vedas. Now, Saiva Siddhanta,

We can see that Sivagnana Bodham, which is the essence of the Gnana Pada of the Agamas of Saiva Siddhanta, also states this. Sivagnana Bodham, Pramana Iyal, Second Sutra. In this, the illustrative Venpa number seven is as follows: "Ondru endrathu ondre kaan ondre pathi." In its commentary, the elders state: In the Chandogya Upanishad, it says "Ekamevadvitiyam Brahma," doesn't it? The word "advitiyam" in the phrase "Brahma advitam" here means "not two." Therefore, the meaning of "Ekamevadvitiyam Brahma" is that God is one. It conveys the meaning that there is no second God. Furthermore, it refutes the Abheda Vada, which states that God and the world are one and not two. This is because the Veda does not merely state "Brahma advitam" but also states "Brahma ekam" and "Brahma advaitam." Here, "Brahma ekam" should be understood to mean that Brahman is one. And for "there is no second thing apart from it," it should be understood to mean that there is no second God, no second Brahman, apart from that Brahman. This is what the Venpa of this Sutra of Sivagnana Bodham also states: "Ondru endrathu ondre kaan ondre pathi." Only one Brahman exists. There is no second Brahman apart from it. Only one entity called God exists. There is no second entity as God. This is its meaning. Sivagnana Swamigal also states the same idea as the meaning of the illustrative Venpa five, which begins with "Ondralaa Ondraal." All living beings, united with a body given by the unique primal being, attain birth, exist, and then attain their end through that unique primal being. That destroyer, who brings about the end, becomes the primal being. He becomes the unique primal being. No one else can be the primal being. This is its meaning. Thus, the scriptural proofs, the proofs from the twelve Thirumurais which are the Tamil Vedas, and the Agamic proofs explain the term "Eka Nayakar" used by Sekkizhar Peruman. Thinnanar saw Kudumithevar, who is such an "Eka Nayakar" and a rising shoot on the Thirukalathi mountain. Thinnanar, who had come with boundless eagerness and great longing due to love, wondering where God was, was seen by Kudumithevar's gracious eyes even before he saw Kudumithevar. Thus, as God's gracious gaze fell upon Thinnanar and God saw Thinnanar, Thinnanar's soul and senses shone with Shiva-consciousness. With such Shiva-conscious eyes, Thinnanar now sees Kudumithevar. Therefore, this sight seen by Thinnanar is not a sight seen with ordinary eyes like ours. It is a sight seen by Thinnanar, shown by God, with God's grace as his eyes. When Thinnanar, with Shiva-conscious eyes, saw Kudumithevar, the "Eka Nayakar," the surging intensity of the great joy and love that arose within him increased. The joy that was within him has now risen and become great joy. Therefore, that love is blissful love. Therefore, the intensity of this great joy and love increases. Ramanatha Chettiyar says that "vegum" (intensity) here refers to the speed of love that leads to Shiva-ananda (bliss of Shiva). The "vegum" mentioned here is not the "vegum" of ordinary human beings, which is subject to afflictions like hunger, thirst, sleep, etc. It is the "vegum" of love for Shiva, the desire to attain Shiva. Only when the ordinary "vegums" like hunger, thirst, sleep, etc., are overcome will the "vegum" of love to attain Shiva arise and increase.

This is what Thiruvasagam says: "Vegam keduthu aanda vendhan adi potri" (Praise to the feet of the King who subdued the speed and ruled). Thus, that desire to attain Shiva, without being contained, rose up and captivated Thinnanar, drawing him in. Thinnanar lost control of himself and completely forgot himself. He became completely devoted to God. Thus, having become devoted to God, and having seen Kudumithevar with Shiva-conscious eyes through God's grace, he ran towards Him with speed and embraced Him with longing. He embraced Kudumithevar with longing and stood there smelling Him. All these actions happened due to the abundance of love. Thirunavukkarasu Peruman has sung in Thiruththadakam: "Munnam avanudaiya naamam kettal murthi avan irukkum vannam kettal pinnai avanudaiya Aroor kettal peyarthum avanukke pichchiyanal annaiyaiyum aththanaiyum andre neeththal agandral agalidaththar aachcharaththai thannai marandhal than naamam kettal thalaippattal nangai thalaivan thale." Kannappa Nayanar is now an embodiment of the grammar of this verse. Thirunavukkarasu Peruman explains the state of a woman who has fallen in love with Shiva in this verse. First, the woman who has fallen in love with Shiva hears Shiva's divine name. She hears about Shiva's golden complexion. She hears about Thiruvarur, Shiva's abode. She becomes deeply in love with Shiva. She mentally abandoned her mother and father that very day. She gave up all the customs prescribed by worldly people. In her devotion to her Lord, she ceased all her actions. She forgot herself and even her own name. The meaning of this verse is that the woman united with the feet of Shiva, her Lord, and became His servant, having nothing for herself. This is the state of those who are "Shakti Nipadathu Utthamarkal" (those of the highest grace of Shakti). For those who have received Shakti Nipada, the stages in the Gnana Pada are Sravanam, Mananam, Nidhidhyasanam, and Nishtai. In Tamil, these are called Kettal, Sindhithal, Thelithal, and Nishtai Kooduthal. Here, in this verse, the first four stages are respectively called Kettal, Sindhithal, Thelithal, and Nishtai Kooduthal. The elders speak of them in terms of knowledge. "Munnam avanudaiya naamam kettal" (She heard His name first) is the first stage of knowledge, Sravanam, which is hearing. "Murthi avan irukkum vannam kettal" (She heard about the form in which He resides) is the second stage of knowledge, Sindhithal, which is contemplation. Name is generally perceived, that is the first stage. To specially analyze and understand the nature of that perceived name is the act of contemplation. This is the second stage of knowledge. Now, the third stage is Thelithal (clarity). "Munnam avanudaiya naamam kettal murthi avan irukkum vannam kettal pinnai avanudaiya Aroor kettal." This is the third stage of knowledge, clarity. Now, "peyarthum avanukke pichchiyanal" (and then she became mad for Him), doesn't it come? "Pichchi" means madness. To be madly in love with the Lord, Shiva, like this, is the fourth stage, Nishtai Kooduthal (attaining absorption), which is deeply immersing oneself in that love. "Annaiyaiyum aththanaiyum andre neeththal" (She abandoned her mother and father that very day), doesn't it come next? Here, the elders interpret "mother" as the power of concealment (Thirodhana Shakti) and "father" as Shiva in His neutral state (Thadastha Nilai), and thus it means the state beyond Tattvas, having abandoned these two. "Agalidaththar aachcharaththai andre neeththal" (She abandoned the customs of the worldly people that very day), doesn't it come? Here, Navukkarasu Peruman specifically indicates that the worldly people's customs are these actions of accumulating and experiencing karmas through self-assertion, and thus revolving in births and deaths.

They forget. Now, "Thannai marandhal" (She forgot herself), doesn't it come? This is the state of forgetting the truth that "I exist," that there is such a thing as "self," and knowing and abiding only in the primal Lord. Then, finally, he says, "Thalaippattal nangai thalaivan thale" (The maiden reached the feet of her Lord). "Thaal" (feet) refers to the divine feet of God, which is God's true nature, and it is in the form of bliss. Thus, the soul revels in the state of liberation. Thus, the elders explain the spiritual meaning of this Thiruththadakam of Navukkarasar Peruman. Thinnanar now attains the same state. According to "Munnam avanudaiya naamam kettal," Thinnanar first heard His name, Kudumithevar. Nanan said, "Kudumithevar is there," didn't he? That is the meaning of "Munnam avanudaiya naamam kettal." "Murthi avan irukkum vannam kettal" (She heard about the form in which He resides), isn't it there? Nanan says, "There will be a faultless God there." "There will be a good sight." That is the state in which Kudumithevar resides. "Pinnai avanudaiya Aroor kettal" (Then she heard about His Aroor), isn't it there? This is Nanan saying, "Kudumithevar is there, Thirukalathi mountain is there, and Kudumithevar is on that Thirukalathi mountain." Therefore, "Pinnai avanudaiya Aroor kettal" means hearing about the Thiruvarur where God resides, and this meaning comes in this verse. Here, it should be understood as hearing about Thirukalathi, the place where God resides. Thus, Thinnanar first heard His name, Kudumithevar. He heard about the state in which Kudumithevar resides. He heard about the Thirukalathi mountain where Kudumithevar resides. Because of this, he ran quickly, became devoted to Him, and embraced Him. In accordance with the lines "Annaiyaiyum aththanaiyum andre neeththal," he embraced Kudumithevar, forgetting his mother and father, and forgetting that he was the leader of the hunter tribe. In accordance with "Agandral agalidaththar aachcharaththai," Thinnanar abandoned all worldly customs and previous attachments that very day. In accordance with "Thannai marandhal than naamam kettal," Thinnanar forgot himself. In accordance with "Thalaippattal nangai thalaivan thale," Thinnanar stood in the shadow of Kudumithevar's divine feet, completely forgetting himself, captivated by His love and grace. He lost himself to Thirukalathi Embiruman. Sekkizhar Peruman now describes the state of Thinnanar, who became devoted to God, in the next two verses. We will continue to contemplate. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 24 — Kannappa Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Periya Puranam, Part 147, Ilai Malindha Sarukkam, Kannappa Nayanar Puranam. Sekkizhar Peruman, having described the event of Thinnanar becoming devoted to God, saying, "He saw the Eka Nayakar, who was a rising shoot on the vast Thirukalathi mountain; the surging intensity of the great joy and love that arose within him propelled him forward with great speed, and he ran towards Him with intense longing, embraced Him, and stood there smelling Him," continues to describe the state of Thinnanar, who became devoted to God. "For a long time, he sighed and stood, with goosebumps rising on every hair follicle of his body, tears flowing like a waterfall from his lotus-like eyes. 'Oh, I have found Him here!' he exclaimed, as if unparalleled compassion itself had taken a form. To Kudumithevar, he said, 'In this forest where elephants, bears, tigers, and lions roam, there is no one to accompany you. I am lost without anyone here. That you are alone on this mountain, how can this be?'" Sekkizhar Swamigal writes thus about Thinnanar's state. Thinnanar, having embraced God and smelled Him for a long time, stood there for a long time, with goosebumps rising all over his body due to love, and tears flowing like a waterfall from his lotus-like eyes. "Oh, I have found Him here!" he exclaimed in wonder, as if unparalleled compassion itself had taken a form. He then addressed Kudumithevar, saying, "In this forest where elephants, bears, tigers, and lions roam, there is no one to accompany you. You are alone on this mountain. How can this be?" Sekkizhar Swamigal writes thus. Thinnanar, driven by the great joy and love that arose within him, quickly ran, embraced, and smelled Him. Therefore, he stood smelling Him for a long time until that speed and longing subsided. All his hair follicles surged with the goosebumps of love. "Pulagam ponguthal" (goosebumps rising) means the hair follicles rising and showing prominently. This happens due to immense joy. Due to physical and mental speed, blood, heated by the body's speed, rushes to those active areas, causing sweat and tears. Here, as the speed of love was immense, his heart melted. Blood flowed with great force throughout his body, which was controlled by his heart. This caused goosebumps and a flood of tears. Then Thinnanar says, "Adiyerkku ivartham inge agappattar" (He has been found by me, a humble servant), exclaiming in wonder. "Agappaduthal" means to be caught within oneself. It was his wonder that this supremely exalted being had been found by him, a most humble servant. We should contemplate the Thiruvasagam here: "Yaavarkkum melaam alavilaa seerudaiyaan yaavarkkum keezhaam adiyenai yaavarum petrariyaa inbaththul vaiththaai ken emperumaan mattrariyaen seyyum vagai." (Oh my Lord, you who are beyond all, of immeasurable glory, you placed me, who is below all, in a joy that no one has ever known. I know not what to do.) "Enna punniyam seythanai nenjame" (What good deeds have you done, oh heart?) is from Thirugnanasambandar Thevaram. This state of Thinnanar, exclaiming "Adiyerkku ivartham inge agappattar achcho," should also be contemplated in the Thiruvasagam's "Achcho Pathikam": "Mukthi neri ariyaatha moorkkarodu muyalvenai bakthi neri ariviththu pazhavinaigal paarum vannam siththamalam ariviththu sivamaakki enaiyaanda aththan enakku aruliyavaaru aar peruvaar achchove." (Oh, who can receive the grace that my Father, who ruled me, bestowed upon me, who was struggling with fools ignorant of the path of liberation, by revealing the path of devotion, removing ancient karmas, cleansing the mind's impurities, and making me Shiva?) "Achcho" is an emphatic word, an exclamation of joyful surprise. Thus, Thinnanar speaks there, revealing the nature of love itself having taken a form. "Kannappan oppathor anbinmai" (There is no love like Kannappan's) is from Thiruvasagam. Thinnanar says to Kudumithevar, "You are alone in this forest. There is no one to accompany you. Only hunters have the strength and courage to be alone in this forest. Those hunters, by birth and profession, are engaged in hunting. They are merciless, living a life of 'kill, throw, stab.'

Such people alone would be in this forest, where fierce animals live. But you, who are the embodiment of love and compassion, are alone here. Is this appropriate?" Thinnanar's heart melts with sorrow. He laments, "If fierce animals come into the forest, there is no one to protect you from them." The knowledge that hunters would be alone in the forest and that fierce animals would cause trouble is a residual memory from Thinnanar's past life experiences. Although Thinnanar had now forgotten himself and who he was, and was the embodiment of love, devoted to God, he still had these thoughts due to the residual memory of past experiences. Later in this story, when he sees blood flowing from Kudumithevar's eye, he wonders, "Who did this? Which animal?" That too is a residual memory of Thinnanar's experiences in this life. Thinnanar, having attained the state of a Jivanmukta, where he had completely forgotten himself and had no worldly thoughts of hunger, fear, pleasure, pain, etc., nevertheless had worldly thoughts of hunger, taste, fear, pleasure, and pain for the sake of Kudumithevar, for whom he had become the embodiment of love. Due to his love for Kudumithevar, and through the residual memory of his worldly experiences, he remembers animals and their nature to harm people, and the nature of the dense forest as unfit for human habitation, and other such things. All these thoughts come to him solely because of his love for Kudumithevar. Therefore, we must see that Thinnanar's past memories of this life and his memories of likes and dislikes all occurred only for the sake of Kudumithevar. For his own sake, Thinnanar was completely devoid of these memories. But for the sake of God, solely for God, he was full of memories, and that is why these events happened during these six days. These are the states of Thinnanar's Jivanmukti. Due to this state of love, this state of being devoted to God, the bow in Thinnanar's hand falls. He doesn't even notice it. He looks intently at Kudumithevar. There are fresh leaves, flowers, and water on Kudumithevar. So Thinnanar asks, speaking in a state of self-forgetfulness, "Who is it that has done this good deed of plucking these fresh leaves and flowers, and pouring water?" Offering leaves, flowers, and water is prescribed in the Agamas as part of Shiva Puja. "Poovodu neer sumandhu ethi" (carrying flowers and water and praising) and "Salam poovodu thoopam" (water, flowers, and incense) are the words of the Tamil Vedas. Thus, Thinnanar asks, "Who did this, as there are fresh leaves, flowers, and water on God's divine form?" But Thinnanar is not asking Nanan. This is because Thinnanar has completely forgotten his worldly existence. Only Kudumithevar is visible to his eyes. His eyes do not see Nanan, who is standing nearby. But Nanan does not understand Thinnanar's state. He also does not know that Thinnanar is asking in a state of self-forgetfulness. Therefore, Nanan, thinking that Thinnanar's question, "Who did this act?" was directed at him, answers. "I know who did this." But when Nanan answers, only the words related to Kudumithevar fall on Thinnanar's ears. Other words do not fall on his ears. Therefore, Thinnanar's question was one of doubt, and he asked himself, not addressing anyone. But Nanan, who was nearby, hears the question. Nanan has the responsibility to clear his master's doubt. So he eagerly comes forward and begins to say, "I know this, I know."

Nanan tells Thinnanar, "I know who offered these fresh leaves, flowers, and water here." "Once upon a time, we came to this hill with your father to hunt animals. At that time, a Brahmin bathed Him with cold water, adorned Him with leaves and flowers, offered Him food, and spoke some words. So, he must have done this today as well," Nanan said. The Agamic rule is that God should be bathed with cold water, offered flowers and leaves according to the season, offered food, and these should be done with mantra words, and this Parartha Shiva Puja should be performed by Shiva Brahmins. Here, even Kudumithevar, who is enshrined on the mountain, is worshipped by a Shiva Brahmin according to Agamic rules. Nanan had seen this once before when he came hunting. He now describes it in his own words. The Agamic rule describes the bathing of God. It states that in the early morning, fresh cold water should be filtered through a new cloth, placed in a new pot, and offered with sacred grass and flowers. Only after anointing with ghee should the bathing be done with lukewarm water. Nanan, who saw this, says that a Brahmin came and poured water on the Lord, the Swami, with cold water. Now, regarding the leaves and flowers, he said that the Brahmin placed leaves and flowers and performed some actions, uttering some words. Thus, offering worship with leaves and flowers is according to the Agamic rules prescribed for each deity and season. Leaves, flowers, and water are indispensable for puja. "Punniyam seyvaarkku poovundu neerundu" (For those who do good deeds, there are flowers and water) is from Thirumandiram. Nanan, though ignorant of these Agamic rules, had seen and understood the Shiva Puja performed by the Shiva Brahmin, Sivagocchariyar, according to Agamic rules. Therefore, he speaks thus. Thinnanar, who heard Nanan's words, also, without knowing these rules, only followed the action, deciding that he should offer water, flowers, and leaves to God. For those in an exalted state, like Thinnanar, their actions transcend rules, yet God accepts them. He saw Sivagocchariyar, the Shiva Brahmin, perform the puja with leaves, flowers, and water. Similarly, he also saw Sivagocchariyar offer food to God. But he did not see what kind of food was offered. It is a rule that no one should see God's offerings. Therefore, Nanan did not say what kind of food was offered. But Nanan says that the Brahmin offered the food to the Swami, uttering some words. When offering food, just as a mother lovingly feeds a child small portions of food, one should lovingly take a small portion of the offering, show it to God's divine mouth, pray, "Please partake of this," and then offer it five times with the appropriate mantras ending in "Swaha." Furthermore, the Agamic rule states that when an Adisaiva Shivachariyar invokes God, he should consider himself as the Guru; when performing ablutions and adornments, as the Mother; when offering food, incense, lamps, and worship, as the mantra form; and when performing praises and circumambulations, as a devotee. This hint is present in Nanan's words.

We must see that all the flowers are there. Nanan refers to Sivagocchariyar, who performs these actions, as a Brahmin. "Brahmin" means one who sees the ultimate reality. Nanan had seen all these actions performed by Sivagocchariyar, the Shiva Brahmin, once before when he came hunting with Thinnanar's father. Now too, seeing the flowers, water, and leaves on Shiva's divine form, he inferred that the same Shiva Brahmin must have done this, and thus gave these answers to Thinnanar. Therefore, only those words related to God, spoken by Nanan, fell on Thinnanar's ears, and Thinnanar, thinking that God liked all these things, also proceeds to do the same. We will continue to contemplate. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 25 — Kannappa Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Periya Puranam, Part 148. Ilai Malindha Sarukkam, Kannappa Nayanar Puranam. Thinnanar, having learned that Kudumithevar liked flowers, water, leaves, and food very much, desired to perform those actions that God liked. But he was unwilling to leave Kudumithevar. He yearned, "He is alone here, and there is no one to offer Him meat to eat." Thinnanar sets out, saying, "I will go and bring meat." But he grieves, thinking, "The Swami will be alone here." He cannot bear to leave the Swami. If he leaves Him, he can bring the meat He needs. Thinnanar wonders, "What shall I do?" Thinnanar wants to offer meat to God. Didn't Nanan say that a Brahmin had already offered food to God? But Nanan could not see, did not see, what the offering was. Therefore, he did not say what it was. But Thinnanar, as a continuation of his past life's residual impressions, desires to offer meat to God. Devotees, killing and eating meat are sins prescribed in the Vedas given by God. Killing and eating meat are condemned in all scriptures and by all wise people. Not killing and not eating meat are not human-made restrictions. These are according to the rules prescribed by God. Humans should not arbitrarily add or relax these restrictions. The Veda says, "Ma himsiyaat" (Do not kill) and "Dharmam chara" (Practice righteousness). "Ara vinai yaathu enil kollaamai" (What is righteous action? It is non-killing) is from Thirukkural. "Nalla ninaippu ozhiya naalgalil or uyirai kolla ninaippathum kutram matrozhiya" (Even thinking of killing a life on days other than good thoughts is a sin) is from the Tamil Veda, Aazhudaiya Nambigal Thevaram, Thiruvarur Thirupathikam. Considering such non-killing and abstaining from meat as mere restrictions is a great mistake. These are not mere restrictions; they are prescribed by God Himself. If all people follow these rules prescribed by God and perform penance, their love will ripen. As love for God ripens, Shiva's grace will flourish. Finally, the soul, becoming the embodiment of love, will shed all worldly bonds and attain the state of uniting with Shiva. At that time, there will be no exceptions to rules, and actions beyond rules will also occur. When such actions beyond rules occur, they are not subject to the rules of merit and demerit. This is the conviction of the wise. There are two ways for souls to attain God: the path of rules and the path beyond rules. Both arise from love. When all souls, following the Vedas and Agamas prescribed by God, practice them and perform penance, and their love ripens and flourishes in Shiva's grace, then worldly thoughts and worldly rules will automatically fall away from those souls in that state of grace. Worldly thoughts will automatically slip away like a betel nut in the hand of a sleeping person. If a sleeping person holds an object in his hand, he will hold it as long as he is awake. Once he goes to sleep, the object he was holding will slip from his hand without his knowledge. Therefore, for souls in that exalted state, captivated by love and God's grace, all the restrictions prescribed for living in the world will automatically disappear. Thus, both the disciplined conduct prescribed by the scriptures, followed out of love, and the conduct beyond rules, forgetting the world, are bestowed by God according to the maturity of the souls. Therefore, both the state of rules and the state beyond rules are desired by God and bestowed upon souls according to their respective maturities.

In this story, we must observe and understand that God embraced both Sivagocchariyar, the Shiva Brahmin who worshipped by following the rules out of love, and Thinnanar, who worshipped by transcending the rules out of the same love. Without this understanding, those who cling to worldly life and wallow in it, thinking themselves to be like those who have forgotten the world and are under the influence of grace, and either unknowingly or knowingly transgress the rules and exceptions given to them, offering their own food to God, is not a state of love but a great sin. Therefore, devotees should deeply understand the essence of this Purana by observing Thinnanar's exalted state of love and God's bestowed grace, and praise and extol Thinnanar's devotion and love. They should sing and spread the glory of Thinnanar's divine feet. Sivakavimani Ayya writes this very extensively and clearly. Thinnanar thinks that he must offer food to God and sets out for it. But then he thinks he has to leave God and comes back. Again, he thinks he must offer food to God and must go to bring it, so he goes. Then, thinking that he has to leave God, he runs back and embraces Him. Again, he goes, and out of love, he looks back at God. Thinnanar is like a cow leaving its calf. We still see a cow that has just given birth, when it goes to graze, going a short distance and then returning without grazing, due to the abundance of love for its calf, don't we? Therefore, the elders give the example of a mother cow and its calf for immense love. In Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's divine history, when the old Brahmin who came to Thiruvannainallur temple disappeared and God appeared as Parvati and Parameshwara on Rishabam (bull) to Sundaramoorthy Swamigal, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal's state was like that of a calf seeing its mother cow, didn't we see in the Thaduththaatkontta Puranam? Just as a calf separated from its mother cow cries out in love when it sees its mother cow again, Sundaramoorthy Swamigal cried out in love when he saw God, didn't Sekkizhar Peruman say there? God is the one who gives everything to all beings and protects them like a mother, isn't He? "Thaaye aagi valarthanai potri" (Praise to You who became the Mother and nurtured) and countless other Tamil Vedic verses sing thus. We should also remember the divine plays where God Himself became the Mother and gave milk to piglet. But in other places, great people have sung, depicting God as the mother cow and the devotees as the calves. However, there are also devotees who have loved God as the calf and themselves as the mother cow that nurtures that calf. Here, Sekkizhar Peruman also uses Kudumithevar as the calf and Thinnanar as the mother cow who cannot bear to be separated from this calf, as an example. He uses it as a simile. Similarly, in the Karaikkal Ammaiyar Puranam, God Himself says to Karaikkal Ammaiyar, "Matru ival nammai penum ammai kaan" (See, she is the mother who cares for us), and God Himself calls Karaikkal Ammaiyar "Ammaiye" (Oh Mother). A cow, unable to be separated from its calf, will turn back again and again, look at the calf, and melt with compassion. "Kattraavin manam pola kasindhu uruga venduvane" (May I melt with compassion like the heart of a cow) is from Thiruvasagam. "Utraarai yaan vendan oor vendan per vendan kattraarai yaan vendan karpanavum ini amaiyum kutraalaththu amarndhu uraiyum kooththa un kurai kazharkke kattraavin manam pola kasindhu uruga venduvane" (I desire no relatives, no town, no name; I desire no learned ones, enough of learning now. Oh Dancer who resides in Kutraalam, may I melt with compassion like the heart of a cow at your beautiful feet) is from Thiruvasagam, Thiruppulambal. Thiruppulambal.

Similarly, the Thiruvathavoorar Puranam also describes the state of Manikkavasagar Swamigal. The verse goes, "Kanru agal ponittra venna kasindhu iru kannir vaara nindru nindru irainji" (Standing and standing, praying, with two tears flowing, melting like a calf separated from its mother). Thinnanar, who was thus distressed, then somehow composed himself and said, "Oh Lord, I will myself choose and prepare good, tender meat for you to eat and bring it here." He says, "I will not leave you. I cannot bear your hunger either." With tears in his eyes, somehow determined to go and return, Thinnanar took his large bow in his hand, bowed to God with his lotus-like hands, and set out. Thus, Thinnanar left Kudumithevar's presence and descended the mountain. Nanan followed Thinnanar down the mountain. When ascending the mountain, Nanan walked ahead and guided the way. Now, when descending, there is no need for guidance. Moreover, Thinnanar descends very quickly. Nanan, and no one else, could match his speed, so Nanan follows him. Furthermore, Nanan, fearing Thinnanar's state, descends the mountain behind him. The inner meaning of Nanan following Thinnanar is that after Thinnanar attained this state of grace, the world should follow Thinnanar. Didn't Thinnanar and Nanan come after telling Kaadan to prepare the pig below? Now Thinnanar descends and reaches the place where Kaadan is. There, Kaadan shows Thinnanar the prepared parts of the pig. Kaadan also asks, "Why did you come so late? What is the reason?" Nanan answers him. "Thinnan saw Kudumithevar on the mountain and embraced Him, clinging to Him like a monitor lizard to a tree hollow, unwilling to leave the Swami. Even now, he has come here only to take meat for that Swami. Thinnan has abandoned our tribal leadership. He has become a servant of that Swami," Nanan says. Here, Sekkizhar Peruman beautifully explains Thinnanar's state using a simile from the hunters' language. Hunters know only forests, trees, and animals, don't they? So Nanan says, "Just as a monitor lizard clings tightly to a tree hollow and does not leave it, Thinnan clung to Kudumithevar like a monitor lizard." He uses that simile in their language. If a monitor lizard clings to something, it holds on to it strongly and does not let go. A monitor lizard has the nature of holding on strongly even on a flat surface. If it is a curved hollow, it will be even more convenient for it to hold on. So it will hold on very well. According to this simile, Thinnanar clung to Kudumithevar like a monitor lizard. Kudumithevar, like a tree hollow, bestowed His gracious gaze, making it convenient for the monitor lizard to cling to Him. We should also remember here that in Thiruvasagam, God is referred to as a sprout and a tree hollow in "Idaimaruthil aanandhath thenirundha pondhaip paravi" (Praising the hollow where the honey of bliss resided in Idaimaruthu). Just as a monitor lizard goes and clings to a tree hollow, God stands with compassion for all souls to cling to Him. Thus, this simile used by Nanan has a beautiful inner meaning. We should be happy to see that this simile, like the words of advice Nanan gave before, also possesses that true inner beauty and exalted quality. Furthermore, Nanan says, "Thinnan has abandoned our hunter leadership. He has become devoted to Kudumithevar."

He also says. The soul always clings to something. If it leaves worldly attachments, the soul truly clings to God. Souls will not exist without any attachment. They cling to the attachment of impurities. If that attachment of impurities is removed, they will cling to the attachment of God. This is the conclusion of the Gnana Sastras. Nanan conveys this idea by saying that Thinnanar has abandoned the leadership of the hunter tribe and has become devoted to Kudumithevar. Nanan's words were born out of his distress that they had found a good leader but were now about to lose him. Nanan refers to Kudumithevar as God. Shiva alone is the true God. Others are called gods only as a matter of courtesy. Shiva is the God of all those who are called gods. "Aththevar thevar avar thevar endru ingan poiththevu pesip pulambugindra poothalathe patrethum illaathu en pattrara naan pattri nindra meiththevar thevarkke sendru oothaai kooththumbee" (Oh bee, go and buzz to the true God of gods, whom I clung to without any attachment, in this world where people speak of false gods and lament). This is from Thiruvasagam. Kaadan, hearing all these words spoken by Nanan, asks Thinnanar, "Thinna, what have you done? You are our leader. Why have you become so deluded?" But Thinnanar did not hear Nanan's words. Thinnanar did not hear Kaadan's question either. He tested the pig's flesh that Kaadan had already prepared, using his arrow, selected the good flesh, and to further test its taste, he put it in his mouth, chewed it with his teeth, and tasted it. He took the good-tasting flesh from these meats, folded and stitched some dried teak leaves nearby to form a container, and filled it with this meat. Nanan and Kaadan watch all these actions of Thinnanar with shock. Kaadan, even more shocked, says to Nanan, "What is Thinnan doing? He is boiling this rare pig's meat, chewing it with his teeth, and then spitting it out. Even though he is very hungry, he refuses to eat it. He also doesn't think of giving that meat to us. He throws away all the tasteless meats." Thus, Kaadan says. So Nanan and Kaadan watch Thinnanar's actions, which are like a divine delusion. They do not know how to cure this. Therefore, they decide that they should go to the village, bring the Devaratti (priestess) and Nagan to cure Thinnanar of this divine delusion, and also gather the other hunters in the hunting ground and go to the village. So Nanan and Kaadan set out from there. Thinnanar did not hear any of what Nanan and Kaadan said. Their words did not fall on Thinnanar's ears. Thinnanar remained unaware that they had both left. He took the pig's meat, which he had tasted, that meat offering, and tied it in a teak leaf. Then, to bathe God, he filled his pure mouth with the water from the great river, the Ponmugali river. Kudumithevar likes flowers and leaves, doesn't He? So he plucked flowers and leaves and tucked them into his hair. Thinnanar tucked in many of them. Thus, carrying the arrow and bow in one hand, and the meat, that divine offering, in the other hand, filled with the water of the Ponmugali river in his mouth, and with flowers and fresh leaves on his head to offer to God because He likes them, thinking, "My Lord must be very hungry," with a melting heart, he yearned and very quickly reached God's mountain. We will continue to contemplate. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 26 — Kannappa Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Periya Puranam, Part 149, Ilai Malinda Sarukkam, Kannappa Nayanar Puranam. Thinnanar, holding meat in one hand and a bow and arrow in the other, with his mouth full of water from the Ponmugali river, and flowers and green leaves tucked into his hair for the Lord, rushes swiftly towards Kudumi Thevar. He hurries, thinking that the Lord might be hungry. Upon arriving, he laments, "My Lord has become weak," and approaches Kudumi Thevar very closely. Thinnanar removes the old flowers from Kudumi Thevar's sacred head with his beautiful sandal-clad foot. The beginning of the daily puja is the removal of the previous day's Nirmalyam (withered flowers and offerings). Therefore, according to that rule, Kannappa Nayanar first removed the flowers from the Lord's head with his sandal-clad foot. Nannan did not mention removing old flowers. What did Nannan say? Nannan only said that a Brahmin would come, place flowers and leaves on the Lord, perform Abhishekam with water, offer food, and then leave. Nannan did not mention removing old flowers. But Thinnanar has the fragrance of the pujas he performed in his previous births, doesn't he? Because of that fragrance, he had the inner urge that old flowers should be removed and Abhishekam performed. So, he removes the old flowers that were already on the Lord's sacred head. Since he held the bow and arrow in one hand and the food for the Lord in the other, he used his foot to remove the old flowers from the Lord's sacred head. After removing the old flowers, it is customary to perform Abhishekam for the Lord. Therefore, he performs Abhishekam for the Lord with the water he brought in his mouth from the Ponmugali river. He spat the sacred water from his mouth onto the Lord. Thinnanar used his mouth as a vessel, filling it with water. He spat the water onto the Lord as if spitting out the love that arose within him. Sekkizhar Swamigal uses love itself as a metaphor for this act, which was born of love. He says that spitting the sacred water from his mouth was like spitting out the love he held. Because, as we saw in the previous verses, Thinnanar is the embodiment of love. Therefore, Sekkizhar Swamigal sings that even what Thinnanar, who was the embodiment of love, spat out, seemed like he was spitting out love itself. Kacchiyappa Sivachariyar later expresses the same idea in the sixth verse of the Aatruppadalam in the Kandapuranam. Kacchiyappa Sivachariyar says in that verse that the dark cloud raining on the mountain was like Thinnanar spitting the sacred water he held in his mouth onto the Lord. Here, Sekkizhar Swamigal sings, "He spat the sacred water from his mouth onto the head of the pure Lord, as if spitting out the love he had produced." In this verse, Sekkizhar refers to the Lord as Vimalanar. The Lord is naturally free from imperfections. Therefore, he is called Amalan, Nirmalan, and Vimalan. So, the Lord is Vimalanar. Therefore, purifying him by performing Abhishekam is not necessary. But the Lord prefers to be immersed in love. That is why he lovingly accepted Thinnanar's sacred mouth-water Abhishekam. Is it inappropriate for Thinnanar to perform Abhishekam for the Lord with his sacred mouth-water? No, it is not inappropriate.

Because the Lord is Vimalan, naturally free from attachments. Thinnanar is the embodiment of love. Therefore, it is not inappropriate. But when the later Sivachariyar, Sivakochariyar, saw all these actions of Thinnanar and worried that it was inappropriate, the Lord appeared in his dream and told him, "All his actions are love towards us," explaining to Sivakochariyar that it was a pure act, not an inappropriate one. Then Thinnanar, with reverence, placed the sacred flowers and leaves (Thiruppallithamam) he had carried on his head, on the Lord's sacred head. The head is the highest part of the body. Above that is the crown. And on the crown is the hair tuft. Therefore, Thinnanar placed the Thiruppallithamam, meant for Kudumi Thevar, even higher than that. Sekkizhar Peruman sings, "He placed the Thiruppallithamam, carried on his head, on the head of the Lord, our Master, on the great Kalatthi mountain, with reverence." Thinnanar held the bow and arrow in one hand and the meat-nectar in the other. Therefore, it was not possible to place them with his hands. So, he bowed, bringing his head close to the Lord's sacred head, and let the Thiruppallithamam (flowers and leaves) he carried on his head fall onto the Lord's sacred head. Sekkizhar Swamigal calls this "Saatthi" (placing). He also says "Vanangi" (bowing). Thinnanar bowed so that the flowers he held on his head would fall onto the Lord's sacred head. This also indicates the method of worship where one should bow when offering flowers to the Lord. After doing this, Thinnanar offers the meat-nectar he brought to the Lord. Thinnanar says to the Lord, "My Lord, for you, I have selected all the good flesh, strung it on my arrow, cooked it over a fire until it reached the right consistency, then tasted it with my teeth, and brought delicious meat. This is very beautiful. Therefore, you must partake of it." Saying this, he offers the meat-nectar to the Lord. A great desire arises in Thinnanar's mind to offer more nectar to the Lord in the same way. At that moment, the sun set below the western mountain. Evening came, followed by night. The sun, knowing Thinnanar's desire to offer more nectar to the Lord, set in the western mountain with the desire to help Thinnanar. It set in the western mountain now, with the desire to rise again, dispel the night, and show Thinnanar the nectar needed for the Lord with its rays. The evening twilight arrives. Thinnanar knows that fierce animals roam here at night. Due to his great love for the Lord, Thinnanar stands like a large dark mountain close to the Lord, so that no harm comes to the Lord from such fierce animals. It is common for fierce animals to roam in the forest at night. Therefore, Thinnanar stands guard close to the Lord, not leaving him for even a moment, with his bow and arrow ready in his hand to prevent any fierce animals from harming the Lord. Not only that, Thinnanar stands guard with the desire for the next day to dawn so that he can offer nectar to the Lord again. Sekkizhar Swamigal sings about how he stands guard: "He stood, looking directly, having seen with overflowing love, with boundless affection, the one whom even sages and celestials, who perform difficult austerities and reach the dark mountain forest, find hard to see."

He sings, "He stood, dispelling the darkness." Who is Kudumi Thevar, the Lord enshrined here? He is Lord Shiva, whom even sages and celestials find difficult to see. Sages (Munivargal) means those who are contemplative. Celestials (Amarargal) means those who prevent death by consuming the elixir of immortality and so on. Thus, Lord Shiva is difficult for both sages and celestials to see. What do these sages do? They perform all kinds of austerities. Sages are those who go to forests and mountains and perform difficult austerities. Austerities (Thavangal) include vows, yogas, etc. Thavam means to burn, to torment. The name Thavam came to refer to these practices, which are performed to torment the body and control the senses. Austerities are performed in many ways, such as staying amidst fires in summer and in water in winter. Pattinatthu Adigal describes how sages perform austerities in Thiruvidaimarudur Mummanikkovai: "Leaving behind the many people, wives, and relatives of this wide world, abandoning homes and other things, entering unimaginable forests and mountains, raising their hands above their heads in summer, bending one leg, standing amidst five types of fire, and then in the rainy season and snowy season, immersing themselves in water for long periods, wearing matted hair, plucking their hair, abandoning clothes, starving themselves, eating fruits, roots, wind-blown leaves, and drinking air and water, sleeping on barren land and stones, they torment their weakening bodies, all to attain liberation." This is from Thiruvidaimarudur Mummanikkovai. Sages perform austerities in these ways. But even with such austerities, our Lord Shiva is difficult to see. Because these austerities are performed with the ego of self-awareness. Therefore, the Lord is rare for austerities performed with self-awareness. When self-awareness is subsumed in Shiva-awareness due to abundant love, the Lord appears easily and bestows grace. Therefore, Lord Shiva, who is difficult for sages to see through their austerities performed with self-awareness, and difficult for celestials to know, was easily found by Thinnanar here. Because Thinnanar's love was boundless love, ever-growing and overflowing. Manifesting this love, Thinnanar stands guarding the Lord, constantly looking at him. He stands guarding, looking directly at the Lord. "Looking directly" means to gaze with absorption, where the seer, the seen, and the act of seeing do not appear as separate but as one. This is explained as Nishthai (meditative absorption). In Sanskrit, this is called the state of Thirupputti, where the distinctions of Gnatru (knower), Gnanam (knowledge), and Gneyam (known) do not arise. In any experience, these three elements exist: the knower, his knowledge, and the object to be known. Here, in the Shiva-experience, where Shiva and the soul are deeply merged without any difference, these three also exist. However, the distinct nature of these three does not appear. That is, the soul, as the knower, is immersed in knowing only Shiva, without knowing itself or its knowledge. In this state, the soul, as Gnatru, will be in the state of Gnana, knowing only Shiva, the Gneya object. In that state,

without separating itself and its knowledge by thinking, "I know this," it remains immersed in the Shiva experience. This clarifies that Shiva-experience is the state where the soul, as Gnatru, without knowing itself and its knowledge, remains immersed in knowing only Shiva, the Gneya. This state of forgetting oneself, being immersed in Shiva, and reveling in Shiva-bliss, while still in this body in this world, is called Nishthai. It is in that state that Thinnanar now stands near the Lord. He is constantly looking at Kudumi Thevar, the Lord. Thinnanar has forgotten that he exists.
