He has also forgotten that he is looking at the Lord. He is immersed in the Shiva-experience, thinking only of the Lord. How is Thinnanar? How long does he remain? Sekkizhar Peruman sings, "He stood until the long darkness was dispelled." He stood like that until the long night passed. This darkness must be dispelled. Dawn must break. He stood for the long night to pass, with the thought that when dawn breaks, he must offer nectar to the Lord again. Furthermore, in Saiva Siddhanta, darkness (irul) is referred to as the Anava Malam (ego impurity). Therefore, "He stood until the long darkness was dispelled" implies that the long-standing Anava Malam, which has been eternally bound to the soul, was dispelled. Because the Anava Malam was dispelled, and because the soul attained the merciful gaze of Lord Shiva, the previous bond of birth was removed, and thus, with the long darkness dispelled, he remained united with Shiva. This is the implication. Furthermore, another inner meaning for "He stood until the long darkness was dispelled" can be that he stood not only for himself but also for the darkness of other souls to be dispelled. It implies that Thinnanar stood there so that the Anava Malam, which binds other souls, would be dispelled, and other souls in this world would also see the state of love, have their Anava darkness dispelled, and attain salvation. Sekkizhar Swamigal continues to describe how the Kalatthi mountain was during that night when Thinnanar stood guard. Even in that intense night, the moonlight filled the Kalatthi mountain. In the caves of the Kalatthi mountain, the red glow of the rubies emitted by snakes shone on one side. Furthermore, various gems and jewels on the Kalatthi mountain also shone even in the dead of night. On that Kalatthi mountain, there were trees that glowed at night, called Jyoti Vrikshas. These are considered divine trees. It is said that these Jyoti Vrikshas are found in miraculous sacred mountains. They are considered one of the Kayasiddhi herbs. Similarly, it is said that Jyoti Grass (Jyoti Pullu) and Jyoti Creeper (Jyoti Valli) also exist. Thus, the Jyoti Vrikshas on the Kalatthi mountain also glow at night. Pearls and gems kept by female monkeys in their homes to provide light at night also glitter, creating light there. Weaver birds in their nests also have the habit of keeping fireflies and other luminous insects to provide light at night. So, such lights also exist there. Above all these, the ascetic light of the sages who perform austerities, controlling their five senses, glows on that Kalatthi mountain. This is the light of wisdom emanating from those sages. We read in scriptures that even those who see these sages from afar, due to the power of their austerities, perceive their bodies as effulgent. Thus, with various kinds of lights appearing on the Kalatthi mountain in the dead of night, it seemed as if it were not night at all, and the Kalatthi mountain shone like a beacon. Within Thinnanar's heart, there was no inner darkness at all. Similarly, outside, on the Kalatthi mountain, there was no darkness either. Thus, as the entire night passed and the hours dwindled, birds began to chirp and fly. Thinnanar, the brave warrior who stood like a dark sea without sleep, thinking that it was now time to offer the meat-nectar to his Lord, sets out for hunting on the Kalatthi mountain. We will continue to reflect. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 27 — Kannappa Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Periya Puranam, Part 150, Ilai Malinda Sarukkam, Kannappa Nayanar Puranam. Thinnanar, who had guarded Kudumi Thevar throughout the night, at the break of dawn, thinking it was the right time for hunting, went hunting. He intended to bring meat-nectar and offer it to the Lord. Thinnanar bowed to Kudumi Thevar and set out for the hunt. At this early hour, as the sun slowly rose, spreading its rays, a sage named Sivakochariyar arrived there. He was a Shiva Brahmin. "He brought flowers, water, and other items according to the puja prescribed in the revered Agamas, to worship the mountain medicine (Lord Shiva) with the blue throat, a sage of great penance, Sivakochariyar." This is a verse by Sekkizhar. Sivakochariyar, a sage of penance, who regularly worshiped Lord Kalatthi, the medicine arising on the mountain, according to the Shiva Agamas, arrived there with flowers, water, and other puja items suitable for the Shiva puja rituals mentioned in the Shiva Agamas. Sivakochariyar, a Shiva Brahmin, regularly performed Parartha Puja (worship for the benefit of others) for Kudumi Thevar, who is enshrined on the Kalatthi mountain, according to the Agamic tradition. He was a devotee of great penance. The name Sivakochariyar means one who always contemplates Shiva. "Kochariththal" means to be the object of knowledge, to meditate. Shiva is beyond speech and mind, yet Sivakochariyar knows that Shiva through Shiva-knowledge, making Shiva an object of his awareness. Sekkizhar refers to him as "the sage Sivakochariyar," adding an epithet. His penance is the penance of worship. It is the penance that serves as the cause for Shiva worship. Worship itself is called Shiva Puja, and Shiva Puja is considered penance. To perform Shiva Puja, one needs prior penance. Thus, Sivakochariyar, a Shiva Brahmin, is a great ascetic who has performed both past and present penance. He performs the daily Parartha Puja for the Lord enshrined on the Kalatthi mountain according to the Shiva Agamas. Sekkizhar Peruman refers to Kudumi Thevar as "the medicine enshrined on the mountain." The medical texts state that medicines found on mountains are excellent and special. Here, the Lord, the medicine enshrined on the Kalatthi mountain, is the medicine that cures the terrible disease of birth, which is the most dreadful of all diseases. The Lord is both the medicine and the physician who uses that medicine to cure the ailment. "He is the mantra, the tantra, and the medicine, capable of curing incurable diseases," says Thiruppullirukkuvelur Thiruthandakam. Thus, Sivakochariyar worships Kudumi Thevar, the medicine on the mountain, according to the Shiva Agamas, as the Lord desires. In the Puranam of Thirukurippuththondar Nayanar, it is said that "the truth desired by the Lord, who spoke countless Agamas, is worship." Accordingly, Sivakochariyar performs the Parartha Puja, which is done according to the Shiva Agamas and is greatly pleasing to the Lord. Generally, the term "Agamas" refers to Shiva Agamas. There are Saiva Agamas, Vaishnava Agamas, and Shakta Agamas. However, the term "Agamas" by convention refers to Shiva Agamas. For Vaishnavites, there are two Agamas: Pancharatra and Vaikhanasa. However,

it is the Vaishnava tradition to refer to Vaikhanasa Agamas as Vaikhanasa Sutras and Pancharatra Agamas as Pancharatra Samhitas. Similarly, it is the Shakta tradition to refer to Shakta Agamas as Tantras. Therefore, "Agamas" generally refers to Shiva Agamas. The Saiva Siddhanta principle is that the Vedas are general, and the Agamas are specific. Saiva Siddhanta states that the Vedas are for all people in the world, and the Agamas are for those who have attained Shakti Nipada (descent of divine grace). The Agamas describe Pati (Lord), Pasu (soul), and Pasam (bondage), and outline the paths for souls, entangled in bondage, to be freed from them, know the Lord, attain liberation (moksha), and experience bliss. Thus, the Agamas are poetic works that explain the three entities of Pati, Pasu, and Pasam, enabling souls to realize the truth and live righteously to attain salvation. In the word "Agama," "A" stands for Shiva Gnanam (divine knowledge), "Ka" for Mukti (liberation), and "Ma" for Mala Nasam (destruction of impurities). Therefore, Agama is a scripture that destroys impurities in souls, produces Shiva Gnanam, and is a means to Mukti. There are a total of 28 Shiva Agamas. Thirumoolar's Thirumandiram, in the 57th verse of the first Tantra, "Agama Sirappu," states, "The Lord with the dark body, who has a part of the Goddess, has 23 Agamas." This refers to the 28 Agamas. These 28 Agamas are as follows: Kamikam, Yogajam, Chintiyam, Karanam, Ajitham, Deeptham, Sookshmam, Sahasram, Amshuman, Suprabhedam, Vijayam, Nisvasam, Svayambhuva, Analam, Veeram, Rauravam, Makutam, Vimalam, Chandragnanam, Mukhabimbam, Protkeetham, Lalitham, Siddham, Santana, Sarvottamam, Paramesvaram, Kiranam, and Vatulam. There are 207 Upagamas (sub-Agamas), from Narasingam to Visvanmikam, for these 28 Agamas. These 207 are Upagamas. It is said that these 28 Agamas and Upagamas were revealed by Lord Shiva through the five faces on the crown of his Maha Sadashiva Murti form. Thiruvachakam's Keerthithiru Agaval states, "He revealed the Agamas spoken on the great Mahendra mountain," and "He received the great Agamas," and "He commanded them through the five faces on Mahendra." Each of these 28 Agamas has four sections: Gnana (knowledge), Yoga (meditation), Kriya (rituals), and Charya (conduct). Both the Vedas and Shiva Agamas are divine pronouncements. Therefore, the Vedas and Shiva Agamas are self-valid, without any higher authority. Shiva Agamas are called "Vedanta Thelivam Saiva Siddhantam" (the clear essence of Vedanta, Saiva Siddhanta). It is the Saiva tradition to refer to the Vedas as Sutras and the Agamas as their commentaries. Shiva Agamas describe all things in such a way as to reveal the Lord to souls and unite souls with the Lord. Therefore, Shiva Agamas are called the ultimate conclusion or Siddhanta. In the Gnana Pada (knowledge section) of these Agamas, the three entities of Pati, Pasu, and Pasam are explained. The Yoga Pada describes Shiva Yoga. The Kriya Pada describes the initiation of mantras, Sandhyavandanam, Puja, Japa, Homa, etc. The Charya Pada describes the religious observances. In Shiva temples built according to the Agamas, daily, occasional, and special pujas, as well as temple construction, consecration, karmic observances for souls, initiation, Atmaratha Puja, etc., must all be performed according to the Agamas. All these matters mentioned above are found only in the Agamas. They are not found in the Vedas or other scriptures. In Periya Puranam, in the Puranam of Poosana Nayanar, the temple built by Poosalar Nayanar in his mind, and the stone temple built by the Kadava king mentioned in his story, along with their consecrations, were all established according to the Agamas. We also saw in Thirunagara Sirappu that Manuneethi Chozhan ruled in such a way that the pujas in Shiva temples were performed according to the Agamas. In Thiruvarur, the Chola kings established many endowments for the Lord who resided in the anthill (Puttidam Konda Nathar), ensuring that the Vedic and Agamic rituals were performed. We can also see in Naminandi Adigal's Puranam that arrangements were made for the special pujas of Panguni Uthiram for Veethi Vidanga Peruman. Furthermore, we will see in the Puranam of Thirukurippu Thonda Nayanar the nature of the Parartha Puja performed by Umaiyamaiyar according to all the Agamic rules. We will also see in the Puranam of Chandeesvara Nayanar the nature of the Atmaratha Puja performed by Chandeesvara Nayanar according to the Agamas. The Lord graciously taught all these special Agamas to Umaiyamaiyar. After listening to them, the Lord also graciously told the Goddess that the truth desired by the Lord, who spoke countless Agamas, is worship. Based on that, the Goddess performs her daily puja, guiding souls to attain worldly pleasures and liberation, and bestowing boons. These can be found in verses 50 to 71 of the Thirukurippu Thonda Nayanar Puranam. Here, in the Kannappa Nayanar Puranam, we will see that Sivakochariyar performed puja and other rituals according to the Agamic rules, and that Thinnanar also followed the same path, performing Abhishekam and other worship, and that the Lord bestowed grace upon both Thinnanar, who became Kannappa Nayanar, and Sivakochariyar. As usual, Sivakochariyar arrived today to perform the Parartha Puja for the Lord. When he approached the Lord and saw roasted meat and bones in front of the Lord's sanctum, he was greatly distressed. He ran away, stepping over them, crying out, "Oh, I am ruined! Who did this inappropriate act?" Immediately, Sivakochariyar thought that hunters must have done this. He thought that hunters, who are not afraid to come directly in front of the Lord, must have done this. The Shiva Agamas, which prescribe temple construction, consecration, and puja, have rules about who should come to the sanctum and to what extent. According to those rules, hunters are not allowed to come directly. But Sivakochariyar was heartbroken that hunters, who are not allowed to come directly in front of the Lord, had come without fear of Shiva's offense. Sivakochariyar performed puja according to the Shiva Agamas, so he was distressed and heartbroken to see an act that violated those rules. Furthermore, Sivakochariyar first wondered who did this act. He only saw the event that occurred here. He did not see the person who did it. There was roasted meat and bones in front of the Lord's sanctum, so he concluded by inference that hunters, who are not afraid to kill, must have done this. Moreover, the footprints of a hunter wearing sandals and the footprints of a dog were also there, which supported his conclusion. Thus, Sivakochariyar, distressed, addressed the Lord, "O Lord of Lords, how can they commit this inappropriate act in front of you and leave? How can you allow them to do such inappropriate things and come and go?" He cried out in great sorrow. Sivakochariyar addresses the Lord as "Deva Devesana" (Lord of Lords), doesn't he?

The Lord is the great Lord of all gods. The Lord, the God of gods, has the nature to punish the gods when they commit offenses against Shiva. Therefore, it implies that the Lord, the God of gods, has the power and nature to inflict appropriate punishment for this offense against Shiva committed by the hunters. Even so, Sivakochariyar concluded that this act could not have happened without the Lord's divine consent. That is why he asks the Lord, "How can you, O Lord, allow such inappropriate things to happen?" He asks the Lord, crying out in distress. If this act had happened without the Lord's divine consent, then both the act and the perpetrator would have been destroyed, like what happened in Daksha's Yagna. But this inappropriate act has happened completely. Therefore, the Lord's divine consent must be there. Sivakochariyar asks the Lord, crying out in distress, "Is it beautiful for you to allow such inappropriate things to happen? Is this becoming of you?" However, the time for Shiva Puja is getting delayed, isn't it? Sivakochariyar is an ascetic sage who performs puja and meditates according to the Agamas. Therefore, he performs both external and internal worship of the Lord. In the internal worship, the Atmaratha Puja, he worships the Lord as a great light. That great light is enshrined here on the Kalatthi mountain as a blazing flame. Parartha Puja must be performed for this blazing light. He is distressed that the time for it is delayed. He thinks that this inappropriate act has happened, and the Lord's puja is being delayed. So, Sivakochariyar decides that he must remove this meat and bones and perform purification. Therefore, he removes the meat and bones that were there. He cleanses the footprints of the hunter wearing sandals and the footprints of the dog with a sacred broom. The author has not mentioned until now that a dog came with Thinnanar. But here, by saying that Sivakochariyar sees the footprints of a dog, we understand that a dog must have come with Thinnanar. The same idea is found in Thirukkannappa Thevar Maram, written by Nakkirar Deva Nayanar. After removing all these inappropriate things, Sivakochariyar descends the mountain and bathes in the sacred waters of the Ponmugali river. He had touched and removed the inappropriate items like meat, bones, and other remains. Therefore, to cleanse himself from the impurity caused by touching them, he again immersed himself in the Ponmugali river and bathed in its sacred waters. This implies that the sacred Ponmugali river has the power to purify both body and soul. After bathing in the sacred waters of the Ponmugali river, he collects the necessary items for the Lord from there and reaches the Lord's sanctum. Although Sivakochariyar had brought all the necessary puja items when he first came, they were now considered impure. Therefore, he discarded them and, after bathing in the Ponmugali river, brought the flowers and water available there. If he were to collect all the puja items again, it would take a long time. So, he took the flowers and leaves available at that moment and came for the puja. He came to the Lord's sanctum. The Agamic rule is that if there are any defects in the daily puja, a ritual to rectify them must be performed before starting the daily puja. Therefore, Sivakochariyar performs purification for Lord Shiva there and then begins the puja. "Pavithram" means purity, sanctity. The actions performed during the Pavithra Puja create purity.

Therefore, it is called Pavithram. Thus, rectifying the faults that occurred in Shiva Puja is called Pavithram. It is also commonly referred to as Prayaschittam (expiation). The rules for this are all stated in the Shiva Agamas. These include Punniyagam, Snapana, Shanti Homam, Japa, etc. These are called Pavithram here. If any deficiency occurs in the daily pujas in Shiva temples, the expiation performed immediately for that deficiency is called Nithyangam. This is done as an expiation for known errors in Shiva Puja, leading to a deficiency in the daily ritual. However, to perform expiation for unknown deficiencies, the Pavithrotsavam festival is also prescribed in the Shiva Agamas annually. This is performed as an expiation for unknown errors that occur in the Lord's Parartha Nithya Puja throughout the year, and it is called Pavithrotsavam. Here, Sivakochariyar performs Nithyangam, which is called Pavithram, as an expiation for these known errors, and then proceeds with the puja. We will continue to reflect. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 28 — Kannappa Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam. Periya Puranam, Part 151, Ilai Malinda Sarukkam, Kannappa Nayanar Puranam. Sivakochariyar, the exalted Shiva Brahmin sage, having realized that inappropriate acts had occurred in his daily Parartha Puja, removed all of them. He then descended from the Thirukalatthi mountain, bathed in the sacred Ponmugali river, collected the available items necessary for the Lord's puja, and again reached the sanctum of Kudumi Thevar. Previously, when he first came, he had brought all the necessary items. However, since they were now considered defiled, he bathed in the Ponmugali river and brought the flowers, leaves, and sacred water available there at that moment. It is also an Agamic rule that if certain items are unavailable, one can perform puja by filling the void with love, using the available items. Sivakochariyar, a highly revered sage who performed puja strictly according to the Agamic principles, as we mentioned earlier, performed purification and is now commencing the Parartha Puja. Sivakochariyar, a Saivachariyar, is a Shiva sage and Shiva Brahmin who has learned and understood all the Agamas. He is fully endowed with the characteristics of an Acharya (preceptor) as described in the Agamas. The Agamas state that Acharyas and Sivacharyas who perform Parartha Puja must be knowledgeable in the Vedas, their auxiliary branches, various mantra sastras, tantra sastras, mudras, kriyas, and Shilpa sastras. Furthermore, the Agamas state that Sivacharyas must be of good conduct, possess good qualities, have a good physique, be auspicious, and that only an Adisaiva Shiva Brahmin, fully endowed with these characteristics, is qualified to perform Parartha Shiva temple worship. Moreover, Adisaiva Sivacharyas must possess philosophical knowledge, contemplate the truths of the six Adhvas, be knowledgeable in Bharata Sastra and Ashtanga Yoga, impart Shiva Gnana, have a pure soul through Acharya Abhishekam, be compassionate towards all beings, be learned in astrological sastras, and bring auspiciousness through their words. The Agamas state that those who possess these qualities are the best among Acharyas. Sivakochariyar is endowed with all these characteristics. Currently, Sivakochariyar is performing purification at the sanctum of Kudumi Thevar and beginning the daily puja. First, he performs the removal of Nirmalyam (withered offerings), and then he performs Thirumanjanam (sacred bath) for Kudumi Thevar. While performing Thirumanjanam, he performs the japa, dhyana, and bhavana associated with it, without any deviation, with unity of mind, speech, and body. Sivakochariyar performs all the rituals mentioned in the Shiva Agamas for Kudumi Thevar, such as adorning the Lord's body with sacred clothes after Thirumanjanam, decorating with leaves and flowers, performing Archana (offering flowers), offering food, circumambulating, bowing, and meditating. He praises Lord Shiva with Vedic mantras, as the Vedas repeatedly declare Shiva to be the supreme God. The Vedas praise Lord Shiva in various ways with Vedic mantras, such as Vishva Karanam (cause of the universe), Vishvarupan (universal form), Vishvadhikan (transcendent to the universe), and Vishva Seviyan (worshiped by the universe). Sivakochariyar praises the Lord with all these. Then, as stated in Namakam and Chamakam, he praises Lord Shiva as the supreme God, stating that Shiva is all, and Shiva is not all. There is no one equal to Shiva or greater than him. Shiva possesses eight qualities. Lord Shiva alone is without birth and death. Shiva is the author of all scriptures, including the Vedas and Agamas. Lord Shiva has countless eyes, feet, and heads. He is called by all names and is all. He is the light of lights, the smallest of the small, the greatest of the great. Shiva is Pasupati. He is the God of gods. Sivakochariyar praises Kudumi Thevar, Lord Shiva, according to the Agamas, reciting these Vedic statements and the secret teachings of Atharva Shikha, Shvetashvatara Upanishad, etc. He praises with the eight names such as Bhavan and at the end of the puja, offers Ashtapushpam (eight flowers). After completing the day's pujas in this manner, Sivakochariyar, with a calmed mind, returns to his hermitage. Sekkizhar Peruman refers to Sivakochariyar as having a "calmed mind." A calmed mind means a mind that has conquered evil qualities like lust, conquered the five senses, and is endowed with profound tranquility. This is the nature of the minds of great sages. This is also one of the characteristics of an Acharya. A question might arise in us: "Didn't Sivakochariyar become agitated and distressed when he saw the inappropriate acts in front of Lord Shiva's sanctum?" Sivakavimani explains that this agitation and distress arose from the love for protecting the Saiva principles. Therefore, they are not faults. "Those who do not know say that love is only for righteousness; it is also a support for trees," says Thirukkural, isn't it? Furthermore, in the 1037th verse of Thirugnanasambandar Puranam, Sekkizhar says, "He shone with the principle of destroying hostile religions that are without attachment." Moreover, Thirunandi Devar asks for a boon from the Lord, "A mind that cannot tolerate the condemnation of Vedas and Saivism." Thus, if there is condemnation or deficiency in the Vedic path or Saiva religion, becoming agitated and distressed upon seeing it is a Saiva principle. These are not faults. Because this agitation and distress arise when the dharmas prescribed in the Vedas and Agamas are affected, and they arise without self-interest. Therefore, these are also Saiva principles. Hence, Sivakavimani explains that saying Sivakochariyar was distressed upon seeing the inappropriate acts in Shiva Puja is not at all contradictory to Sekkizhar now describing him as having a calmed mind. Thus, after performing worship in this manner, bowing to the Lord at the end of the worship, and taking leave of the Lord, Sivakochariyar goes to his hermitage. Now, Sekkizhar Peruman begins to sing about Thinnanar. Thinnanar had already set out for hunting to offer nectar to the Lord. Now, Sekkizhar Peruman begins to describe the hunts performed by Thinnanar. Sekkizhar Swamigal sings, "Thus the great sage departed. Now, I will describe the skill of the dark-haired hunter, the great Lord, who bent his bow and hunted alone in the forest, and I will remove the power of evil deeds." Now, Thinnanar is performing a solo hunt. This is not a hunt like the one Thinnanar performed the previous day with many other hunters. Now, Thinnanar is performing a solo hunt. Moreover, this is not like the hunt performed the previous day. This is a hunt performed by Thinnanar, having lost himself to Kudumi Thevar, completely forgetting himself, thinking only of Kudumi Thevar, and for Kudumi Thevar. It is a hunt performed for that unique Lord. Therefore, it is a solo hunt. This is a hunt that occurs by the grace of the Lord. It is not like the cruel hunt performed by other hunters. It is a hunt performed by remembering the Lord, being subservient to him, and by the grace of the Lord. Therefore, this hunt performed by Thinnanar also becomes an act of grace. The Agamas state that by describing the gracious acts of the Lord, our evil deeds will be removed. The conclusion of Gnana Sastras is that describing or listening to the gracious acts of the Lord is one of the means to remove our evil deeds. Therefore, here, since Thinnanar performs this hunt by the grace of the Lord, Sekkizhar Swamigal says that by singing about this hunt, he will remove all evil deeds. Therefore, this hunt is not an evil act of killing. It becomes a good deed that removes our evil deeds, who listen to this hunt performed by Thinnanar. This hunt happened by grace. Therefore, Sekkizhar Peruman guarantees that the evil deeds of those who read and listen to the glory of the hunt performed by Thinnanar will all be removed. Sekkizhar Peruman describes the gracious glory of the hunt in this way to make the readers focus on the gracious nature of this hunting act, without thinking about any other matter. This is Sekkizhar Peruman's poetic tradition. Earlier, in the Puranam of Thadutthattkonda, when describing the wedding of Sundaramurthi Swamigal, when describing the wedding attire of Sundaramurthi Swamigal, he says that he is going to sing about the wedding attire of Sundaramurthi Swamigal, and by doing so, he will attain salvation. He says in the 173rd verse, "I will describe what happened there, how our Lord, in his beautiful wedding attire, descended from the horse in front of the wedding pandal, where white conches everywhere roared like a great rain, and I attained salvation." There, Sekkizhar Swamigal says that he attained salvation by singing about the wedding attire of Sundaramurthi Swamigal. Similarly, here, Sekkizhar Peruman sings that he will sing about this gracious hunt performed by Thinnanar, and by doing so, he will remove all evil deeds. Thinnanar begins to hunt for the Lord. He hunts large boars that graze in the clearings on the slopes between two mountains. He hunts deer of all kinds. Thus, Thinnanar kills many kinds of boars and deer. Here, Sekkizhar Peruman uses the word "Aruli" (graciously) for the act of killing. Killing is generally considered a sinful act. But Sekkizhar Peruman says that the act of killing itself became an act of grace here. Because these animals, born in lower forms of life, without intellectual clarity, roam in the forest destroying crops and other living beings, and one day they will die. They will be reborn according to their karma. This is what happens to those who take animal bodies. Since they have taken animal bodies, they cannot perform any other karma by violating their limitations. Just as souls who take human bodies can perform Shiva pujas, Shiva worship, and other services with the clarity of intellect in the human body, animals, due to their lack of intellectual clarity and deficiencies in their inner instruments, cannot perform Shiva worship or Shiva pujas like souls in human bodies. This is a limitation of taking animal bodies.

But here, Thinnanar hunts and kills these animals. These animals are killed by Thinnanar's sacred hands. As a result, the bodies of these animals become sacred food pleasing to Lord Shiva. Because they were killed by Thinnanar's sacred hands, and because Thinnanar offers the bodies of these animals as nectar to Lord Shiva, and through Thinnanar's intention and action, these animal bodies, as meat, serve as sacred food for the Lord, those souls attain Shiva Punya (merit). By attaining Shiva Punya in this way, those souls who took animal bodies attain salvation. If they had not been killed by Thinnanar's sacred hands, the opportunity for those animals to realize Lord Shiva and attain salvation by offering their body, wealth, and life for Lord Shiva would never have occurred. Here, Thinnanar, having lost himself, becoming subservient to Shiva, with all his senses becoming divine instruments, and acting by grace, performs his hunting. Therefore, all the lives killed in this gracious act attain salvation by contributing to Shiva Punya. Therefore, this act of killing performed by Thinnanar becomes an act of grace. That is why Sekkizhar Peruman sings "Konru Aruli" (killed graciously) here. The Shiva Agamas have rules specifying which trees, plants, and creepers, and which flowers, leaves, blossoms, and tender shoots should be plucked and used for Shiva Puja. Therefore, devotees of Shiva use such plants from Nandavanams (flower gardens) and other places dedicated to Lord Shiva, taking their parts like flowers, leaves, tender shoots, and roots for Shiva Puja. By offering their parts for Shiva Puja in this way, all those plants attain salvation. Because their parts serve as causes for Shiva Punya and earn Shiva Punya. Just as plants in Nandavanams and other places that assist in Shiva Puja attain salvation, similarly, all the lives killed in the gracious hunt performed by Thinnanar attain salvation. They earn Shiva Punya. If they had not been killed by Thinnanar's sacred hands, this opportunity would not have been available to those souls. That is why Sekkizhar Swamigal sings "Konru Aruli" at this point. Because the hunt Thinnanar is performing now is not like the hunt he performed the previous day with other hunters. Others hunt out of self-awareness, ego, and individual consciousness. For all their hunting activities, there are sins. But what Thinnanar is performing now is a hunt where all his senses have become divine instruments, subservient to grace and Shiva. That is why, when beginning to read about this hunt, Sekkizhar Peruman sings that by singing about this gracious hunt, all evil deeds will be removed. Thus, Thinnanar gathers all the hunted animals in one place. He draws his sword, cuts down dry trees, makes a fire drill, and starts a fire. He plucks teak leaves, stitches them together to form a vessel. He collects honey from small plants and flowers and gathers it in the teak leaf vessel. He collects firewood, creates fire with a fire drill, and then cooks parts of the animal bodies in the fire he has created, using his arrow to separate them.

Thus, he tastes the cooked meat with his pure sacred mouth, and collects the delicious meat in vessels made of teak leaves. The Havish (offering) meant for the gods is first given to Agni Deva (the fire god). Agni Deva then gives this Havish to the gods. Similarly, Thinnanar first tastes the sacred nectar (meat) meant for Thirukalatthinathar with his sacred mouth, and then offers that sacred nectar to Thirukalatthinathar. In Yagnas (fire rituals), the Havish offered to the gods is offered into the sacrificial fire, Agni. Agni Bhagavan removes all impurities from the items offered in the Yagna, purifies the Havish offered in the Yagna, and then delivers them to the respective gods. Similarly, generally, meat is considered inappropriate for the Lord. But that inappropriate meat becomes nectar to be offered to the Lord after it is placed in Thinnanar's sacred mouth. By being placed in Thinnanar's sacred mouth, all the inappropriateness of the meat is destroyed, and it becomes extremely pure, pure enough for the Lord, and becomes sacred nectar for the Lord. The rule is that Thirumanjanam (sacred bath) should be performed for the Lord only with Ganga water. But Lord Shiva himself will later say that the Thirumanjanam water spat from Thinnanar's sacred mouth is purer than Ganga. Similarly, the inappropriate meat, by touching Thinnanar's sacred mouth, becomes sacred nectar acceptable to the Lord. The gods cannot directly consume the Havish. They can only accept that Havish through Agni Bhagavan. But this Agni is one of the Lord's Ashtamurtis (eight forms). That is why the inner meaning is that Aran (Shiva) acts as the chief of the Yagna, receives the Havish, and gives it to the other gods. Thus, Lord Shiva, as the chief of the Yagna, gives the Havish to all other gods. But it is our Thinnanar who offers nectar to Lord Shiva himself. Just as the Havish offered in Yagnas becomes a Vedic प्रमाण (proof) and sweet food for the gods, the meat offered by Thinnanar, without being a Vedic explanation, became superior and purer than that Havish for Lord Shiva. The meat offered by Thinnanar becomes the most excellent Naivedyam (food offering). This is what Adi Shankara, the Smartha Acharya, also sings in the 63rd verse of Sivananda Lahari: "Kinchit Bakshita Mamsa Sesha Kapalam Navvopaharayate" (The remaining portion of meat, bitten a little, becomes like a new offering). Adi Shankara praises Kannappa Nayanar and his devotion, saying that the remaining portion of meat, bitten a little by the hunter living in the forest, becomes like a new offering. Thus, after preparing the meat and pouring the honey he had collected into it, holding this sacred nectar in the vessel made of teak leaves, and tucking the Thiruppallithamam (flowers and leaves) into his hair as he did the previous day, and taking the necessary Thirumanjanam water for the Lord in his pure sacred mouth, Kannappa Nayanar swiftly proceeds towards Kudumi Thevar's sanctum. Thinnanar, the leader of the forest hunters, just as he did the previous day, removes all the Nirmalyam from the puja performed by Sivakochariyar and performs his own puja for Lord Shiva, the leader of the gods. Thinnanar is the leader of the forest hunters. Lord Shiva is the leader of the gods.

Both are leaders. He is the one who attains, and He is the one to be attained. The hunt and the method of preparing the nectar on the previous day are different now. But Thinnanar's puja performed the previous day and the puja performed now are not different. Therefore, Sekkizhar Swamigal, who explained the different parts in detail, briefly describes Thinnanar's puja, saying that he performed it just as he did the previous day. Just as he removed the Nirmalyam with his sandal-clad foot, performed Thirumanjanam for the Lord with the sacred water he brought in his mouth, placed the flowers from his head on the Lord's sacred head, and offered the nectar he brought to the Lord, he does the same now. Thus, Thinnanar, with ever-increasing love for Thirukalatthi Appar, guards him at night. During the day, he hunts. Then, he offers the sacred nectar he brought from the hunt to the Lord. In the same way, four days passed from the second night to the fifth night. Similarly, on the sixth day, only a day hunt occurred, and Thinnappan became Kannappan and sat on the right side of Thirukalatthi Appar on the path of no return. Sekkizhar Peruman, who described the events of the intervening days from verse 802 to 806, explains the events of the sixth day from verse 809 to 819. Just as he performed puja during the day and guarded at night on the first day, similarly, from the second night to the fifth night, for four nights and three days, Thinnanar hunted during the day and performed puja, and guarded at night. Similarly, Sekkizhar Peruman, after stating that the pujas performed by Sivakochariyar on the third, fourth, and fifth days occurred in the same way as on the second day, is now going to describe the events that occurred on the fifth night. We will continue to reflect. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 29 — Kannappa Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam Periyapuranam Part 152 Ilaimalinda Sarukkam Kannappa Nayanar Puranam. After Thinnanar saw Kudumithevar, on the second, third, and fourth days, he hunted during the day, offered food to the Lord, and guarded Him without sleeping at night. Sivagochariya Munivar, on the second day after Thinnanar saw Kudumithevar, removed the Nirmalyam (offerings) made by Thinnanar, purified the place, and performed the Nitya Parartha Puja according to the Agamas. He did the same on the third and fourth days. Sivagochariyar, realizing that Thinnanar's puja was improper and harmful, removed them and performed puja according to the Agamas. Although it was not truly harmful, Sivagochariyar only understood this after the Lord Himself appeared in his dream and revealed that it was not harmful. Until then, Sivagochariyar performed pujas according to the Agamas, believing Thinnanar's puja to be improper. As we have seen before, he first purified and then performed archana. Here, "archana" is a term that encompasses all aspects of puja, such as purification, holy bath, offering flowers, offering food, and chanting mantras. Archana has generally been used as a generic name for puja. Meanwhile, did Nanan and Kadan return to Uduppur after seeing Thinnanar's state on the first day? They informed Thinnanar's father about Thinnanar's condition. Nagan, his father, was very worried upon hearing that his son was in a divine trance, oblivious to the world. Consequently, Nagan also lost his appetite and sleep. Thinnanar, who was born to him at a very old age after a long period of childlessness, was his only son, capable of upholding his lineage. Thinnanar, who had taken over the rule of the forest according to his tradition on the first day, was now in a divine trance. Nagan's worry grew immensely, fearing that he had lost his son. In this state, Nagan, Thinnanar's father, along with Devaratti, came to Thinnanar and tried to bring him back to their side by dispelling his trance. However, their efforts were in vain. Here, Sekkizhar Peruman composes a verse as a poetic statement. "Just as iron turns into gold through the touch of the philosopher's stone by the grace of the Lord of Thirukkalathi, so too Thinnanar's body, its nature, his two karmas, and the three impurities (malas) were removed, and he wandered as a mass of love. How could he be swayed by the thoughts of his father Nagan and Devaratti?" Sekkizhar Peruman states this as a poetic utterance. Thinnanar was not swayed by the thoughts of his father Nagan and Devaratti. Because the gracious gaze of Thirukkalathiappar fell upon Thinnanar, just as iron turns into gold through the touch of the philosopher's stone, so too Thinnanar, his body's nature, his karmas, and the three impurities were removed, and he wandered as a mass of love. Thinnanar, having shed his former state of being a soul (pasu-bodham), now wandered in the form of Shiva-bodham. Sekkizhar Peruman states that Thinnanar, being in such a state, would not be swayed by the thoughts of Nagan, Devaratti, and other forest hunters.

We have seen in detail in previous verses how Kudumithevar's gracious gaze fell upon Thinnanar and how it affected him. Just as iron turns into gold through alchemy, so too Thinnanar attained Shiva-bodham, shedding his soul-consciousness, by the gracious gaze of Kudumithevar. As a result, Thinnanar's bodily nature was removed. His karmas were removed. The three impurities were removed. Sekkizhar Peruman sings this as "yaakkai than parisum vinai irandum saaru malam moonrum ara" (the body's nature, the two karmas, and the three impurities were removed). "Yaakkai than parisu" means the nature of the body. In Tamil, the body is also called "yaakkai." "Yaakkai" means that which is bound. This body is bound by nerves and other things. Therefore, this body is called "yaakkai." "Kal koduthu elumbu mooti karu narambaakkai yaathu thol uduthu uthiramatti thogumayir meyndha koorai" (giving legs, joining bones, binding black nerves, covering with skin, filling with blood, a roof thatched with hair) is the third verse of Thirunerisai Thirukkundeeswaram by Appar Swamigal. The meaning of this verse is that this body is a roof made by giving two legs, joining bones, connecting nerves inside the body, covering it with skin, filling it with blood, and thatching it with hair. Because it is bound in this way, this body is called "yaakkai." The nature of this "yaakkai" was removed for Thinnanar. Thanu, karanam, buvanam, bhogam are the four things that are Mayan. That is, they are derived from Maya. Here, the nature of the body refers to both "thanu" (body) and "karanams" (instruments). "Thanu" means body. "Karanams" are the instruments present in this body, which are external and internal instruments. What does it mean for their nature to be removed? That is, Sekkizhar Peruman has stated that after the gracious gaze of the Lord falls, the nature of this body is removed. The nature of these Mayan things is that when there is no gracious gaze of the Lord, their function and nature is to prevent the partial consciousness that comes in accordance with the karmas that one has brought. Not like preventing the partial consciousness that comes in accordance with karma before receiving the gracious gaze, but the "thanu" and "karanams" of Thinnanar, that is, both of them, lose their nature and stand subdued before the great light of the Lord's grace. Sekkizhar Peruman sings this as "yaakkai than parisu aruthal" (the removal of the body's nature). Now he says that the two karmas were removed. "Vinai irandu" means dharma and sin. The dharma and sins committed by souls are the cause of rebirth. The removal of these dharma and sins means that they cease to yield their fruits of pleasure and pain. That is, the fruits of dharma and sin do not cling to the soul but remain as the fate of the body and are removed. That is the meaning of "vinai irandum aruthal." Now Sekkizhar Peruman sings that the three impurities were also removed. The nature of the body was removed. The two karmas were removed. Similarly, Sekkizhar Peruman sings that the three impurities were removed for Thinnanar. The three impurities are Anavam, Mayai, and Kanmam. Among these, Anavam is called Sahajamalam. The Sivagnanabodham Chitturai explains the nature of Anavamalam as follows. In Sivagnanabodham Chitturai, Sivagnanabodham Sutra Four, Adhikaranam Four,

the nature of Anavam is explained as follows: Sahajamalam, which is Anavamalam, is a deluder of knowledge, a substance, having the quality of ignorance, like rust on copper, a defect of the soul, an eternal bondage, inert, one, causing the distinctions of Vignanakalar, Pralayakalar, and Sakalar, distinct and separate in each soul, concealing and having many powers that are removed at their appointed time, the root cause for souls to experience three states, eternal, pervasive. This is the nature of Anavam. As described above, this Sahajamalam causes the pervasive soul to become atomic. Therefore, it is called Anavam. This Anavamalam is the cause of various beginnings such as Pasutvam, Pasu Neekaram, Mrityu, Moorchai, Malam, Anjanam, Avittai, Aaviruthi. Sekkizhar sings that the three impurities were removed. We have seen the nature of Anavam among those three impurities. Now, the second is Kanmam. The Kanmam here refers to Moola Kanmam (root karma). Because Sekkizhar Peruman has already sung "yaakkai than parisum vinai irandum arunthuvittathu" (the body's nature and the two karmas were removed). Now, the Kanmam among the three impurities refers to Moola Kanmam. This Moola Kanmam is the primary cause for souls to first acquire a subtle body. We have seen the nature of Anavam among the three impurities. We have seen that Kanmam refers to Moola Kanmam. Now, Mayai is the primary cause for the "thanu, karanam, buvanam, bhogam" (body, instruments, worlds, experiences) given to souls for the experience of the fruits of the two karmas. These are the three impurities: Anavam, Kanmam, and Mayai. The effects of these impurities are seven. They are of seven types due to the variations in the effects of the root impurity. Those seven effects are Moham, Madham, Ragam, Kavalai, Thabam, Vaattam, Vichitram. This is explained in Sivagnana Mapadiyam Sutra Two, Adhikaranam Two, as follows: Moham is that which is inherent in souls until the six types of bondage, such as Madham, are removed. If there is no Moham, then other things like Madham will not occur. Therefore, Moham is the primary one. What is this Moham? It is the cause of the delusion we experience in worldly matters, even after knowing what is stated in all scriptures, including ethical texts, such as what should be done, what should not be done, what should be accepted, and what should be rejected. Now, what is Madham? It is the cause of our praising a desired object as superior to all others due to the delusion caused by Moham mentioned above, and becoming engrossed in that matter, believing that no other matter is superior to it. Now, the third is Ragam. This is the increasing desire for an object or matter that we like. Next is Kavalai (worry). If we lose the object we possess due to the power of fate, or if we are about to lose it, then shedding tears and experiencing great sorrow is called Kavalai. That which causes worry is Kavalai. Next is Thabam (suffering).

Thus, when a desired object leaves us, after losing it, the internal burning and anguish is Thabam. Next is Vaattam (withering). This internal burning causes fainting, and the mind and body wither. Finally, what is Vichitram? We see various worldly matters and worldly life in this way. "He is mine. He is my relative. She is my rightful wife. These are my properties. This is my house. This is my land. How to protect them? How to increase them further? How to protect the family?" The seventh effect, Vichitram, is the cause of such various thoughts. Thus, Sivagnana Mapadiyam explains the effects of the impurities as Moham, Madham, Ragam, Kavalai, Thabam, Vaattam, and Vichitram. For Thinnanar, these three impurities mentioned above are removed. If the three impurities are removed, it means that the three impurities cease to perform their functions. What does it mean for Anavam to be removed? Anavam is called darkness. Just as darkness subsides when the sun rises, so too the power of Anavam subsides when the Lord's grace manifests in souls. This is the removal of Anavam. Sekkizhar Peruman says that these three impurities were removed for Thinnanar, doesn't he? But here, Thinnanar had the feeling "I will hunt and offer food. I will guard the Lord at night," and he had a body and the actions of internal and external instruments. He wandered in the world with these. So, were all these actions of hunting, guarding the Lord, and offering food to the Lord, which Thinnanar performed for these six days, the actions of the three impurities? No, these were not the actions of the three impurities. These did not happen for Thinnanar due to the actions of Anavam, Kanmam, and Mayai. How? Even though Thinnanar had a body, its actions, and that consciousness, he was without the feeling of "I" and "mine," such as "my body," for his own sake. After the Lord's grace fell, there is no mention that he ate food during these six days. He had no thought that he would become weak if he did not eat. He guarded the Lord for these six days. He remained awake. He had no feeling that he would get tired if he did not sleep or rest. We see that these two feelings were absent. Therefore, we should understand that other feelings like these were also absent. He did not even have the feeling "I will die." Thinnanar was in this state, under the sway of divine grace. That is why later, in Sivagochariyar's dream, Lord Shiva says, "All his knowledge is the knowledge that knows us." Thinnanar's entire consciousness knew only Kudumithevar. He did not know himself. The Anavam, which is self-consciousness, remained only as a momentary cause for his experience of Shiva-ananda. Now, Thinnanar hunted. He traveled. If one asks if these actions existed, even these were not done by Thinnanar for his own sake. Lord Shiva Himself says, "All his actions are pleasing to us." Because these actions of hunting and traveling performed by Thinnanar did not yield the fruits of karma and did not cause further karmas to sprout. The Prarabdha karma, which caused him to take a body, would remain as long as Thinnanar's body existed. Nevertheless, Thinnanar wandered as a mass of love, as the very form of love. Therefore, Thinnanar's knowledge did not go through the five senses for his own experience. So, his Prarabdha remained only at the level of his body. It did not cling to Thinnanar's soul. Unlike other souls where Prarabdha clings to the soul, for Thinnanar, it did not cling to his soul. His Prarabdha remained only at the level of his body. This is the explanation of how Prarabdha, a part of karma, did not affect Thinnanar. Now, the huge accumulation of Sanchita karma that Thinnanar had accumulated earlier, that collection of karma, was burnt and destroyed like cotton in fire the moment Kudumithevar cast his gracious gaze upon Thinnanar. Thus, we saw that Prarabdha did not affect him and remained at the bodily level. We saw that Sanchita was destroyed and burnt. Now, what remains is Agamiyam. Thinnanar stood only for the service of the Lord. Therefore, this Agamiyam also ceased to accumulate. Agamiyam accumulates only when we act for our own sake with ego. Thinnanar's actions were for the service of the Lord, for the sake of the Lord. Therefore, Agamiyam ceased to accumulate. So, even though Thinnanar performed actions like traveling and hunting, which are karmas, they did not cause Agamiyam for him. Thus, we have seen the explanation of how the three divisions of karma—Sanchita, Agamiyam, and Prarabdha—were removed from Thinnanar. Now, the Mayan things, which are the effects of Maya Malam, such as the body and instruments, ceased to have their nature. Thinnanar's body, by the gracious gaze of the Lord, became a form of love in the shadow of light. Thinnanar had a body, internal instruments, and external instruments. They also performed actions. Nevertheless, Thinnanar was completely under the sway of the Lord, and in His shadow, as a form of love, his jiva-bodham (soul-consciousness) was destroyed, and he stood in Shiva-bodham. Therefore, his actions were not his own but the actions of Shiva. As stated in the Gnana Sasthra called Thiruvunthiyar, "After we, who knew our actions, ceased to exist, the Lord's action is His own," and "He gave Himself," so sing the Thiruvunthiyar. This is the state that Thinnanar attained. Thus, Thinnanar, in the shadow of the Lord, with the nature of his body removed, the two karmas removed, and the three impurities removed, existed and wandered as a form of love, as a mass of love. Sekkizhar Peruman explains in this verse that Thinnanar, being in such a state, would not be swayed by the thoughts of Nagan, the hunters, or Devaratti. We will continue to reflect. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 30 — Kannappa Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam Periyapuranam Part 153 Ilaimalinda Sarukkam Kannappa Nayanar Puranam. Thinnanar offers holy food to the Lord. Sivagochariyar, believing it to be improper, performs daily pujas to the Lord according to the Agamas. On the fifth day, Sivagochariyar, after completing his puja for the day, bows to Lord Shiva and asks Him, "My Lord, I cannot bear to see such improprieties being committed in this place. By Your grace, please prevent these improprieties from happening." The Agamas are the word of the Lord, therefore they are eternal, unchangeable, and cannot be refuted by any other texts; they are established. Thinnanar performed puja according to the path he learned through direct perception (katchi alavai), inference (anumana alavai), and the words of others (urai alavai) that he heard. Sivagochariyar, the sage, performed puja based on the Agamas, which he understood as the word of the Lord, through Agamapramana (scriptural authority). Thinnanar's instruments (karanams) transformed into Shiva's instruments, and his knowledge became the knowledge of Lord Shiva, and he performed puja with that knowledge. Sivagochariyar, the sage, on the other hand, performed puja with his own instruments, guided by the Agamas, using his own knowledge. Thinnanar's puja was a puja known by the Lord Himself. The puja performed by the sage was a puja revealed by the Lord through the Agamas. Both were pleasing to the Lord. It is noteworthy that through the fruit of Sivagochariyar's puja, the Lord Himself revealed Thinnanar's love. Sivagochariyar asks, addressing Shiva as "My Lord." "My Lord" means "the master who owns me as a servant." Self-realization in Saiva Siddhanta means realizing the Master who owns oneself. Therefore, Sivagochariyar, who had realized his Master, asks Lord Shiva, "You are the Lord of my soul, the life of my life. Therefore, You know my heart. I ask this only because I desire purity in Your presence." He prays, "By Your grace, please remove all these improprieties." The inner meaning of this is that these improprieties will cease to occur only by the Lord Shiva's grace and will, and by nothing else. On that night, the fifth night, when Sivagochariyar made his appeal to the Lord, it was the auspicious day when the Lord willed to manifest His grace for both Sivagochariyar and Thinnanar to see. On that night, Lord Shiva appeared in Sivagochariyar's dream. The Lord, considering the maturity of souls, manifests His grace to some in their waking state and to others in their dreams, according to their maturity. Moreover, it is known from the scriptures that for those with one impurity, He reveals Himself from within; for those with two impurities, He appears in their presence with a deer, axe, four arms, and a blue throat; and for those with three impurities, He comes directly as a Guru in their dream in the third person. Here, on the sixth day, He is going to bless Thinnanar directly in his waking state. Now, on the fifth night, He is going to bless Sivagochariyar in his dream. "You are unknown to the gods even in dreams, yet You have graced this dog-like me even in waking," says the sacred utterance.

Sekkizhar Peruman sings in the 805th verse: "That night, in the dream of the gracious sage, the Brahmin with the radiant matted crown appeared and said, 'Do not think of him as a fierce hunter. Know that all his form is love for us, all his knowledge is the knowledge that knows us, and all his actions are pleasing to us. Know his state thus.'" Sekkizhar Peruman also sings in the 806th verse. Lord Shiva appears in Sivagochariyar's dream as a Brahmin with a radiant matted crown. This is the form in which the Lord appeared in Sivagochariyar's dream. He came as a Brahmin with matted hair, revealing His true nature, with three eyes and a blue throat, and other identifying marks. Among these sacred marks, the matted hair (Jatamudi) is primary, so it is mentioned first as "min thigazhum sadai mouli" (radiant matted crown). This Jatamudi of the Lord is such a primary sacred form, which is why the name "Kabardhine Namaha" is found in the Shiva Ashtothram for the Lord. Thus, He comes in a dream in the form of a Brahmin with matted hair, three eyes, and a blue throat. The Vedas state that Lord Shiva alone is the Brahmin. Sivagochariyar was one who praised the Lord daily with the words of the Vedas in his Parartha Puja, believing Him to be the unique and supreme Lord. Therefore, to Sivagochariyar, a Shiva Brahmin and a Vedic sage, the Lord appears in his dream in the form of a Brahmin, just as he had meditated upon and praised Him. To show that He is the same Lord Shiva whom Sivagochariyar praises daily, He comes in the dream in the form of a Brahmin, with matted hair, three eyes, and a blue throat as His identifying marks. Having come, Lord Shiva then says the following to Sivagochariyar: "Do not think of him as a hunter. Even though he belongs to the hunter tribe, he is not to be considered as one of the hunters. Nor is he just another human being. His nature is different. I will tell you his nature, listen." Lord Shiva then begins to speak. He says, "All his form is love for us." That is, Thinnanar's form is the very embodiment of love. Lord Shiva says that Thinnanar is the form that love would take if it were to gather and take a shape. Next, He says, "All his knowledge is the knowledge that knows us." Lord Shiva says that all his knowledge was directed only towards Him and did not go anywhere else. Furthermore, Lord Shiva says, "All his actions are pleasing to us." He says that all his actions were done to please Him. That is, all of Thinnanar's actions, such as waking up in the morning, going hunting, bringing food, offering food, bathing the deity, removing flowers with his footwear, and guarding at night without sleeping, are all pleasing to Him. He says that these were all done for Him, for His sake. Even if Thinnanar's actions are not considered pleasing according to the Agamic rules prescribed by Him, the Agamas themselves prescribe love above all else. Therefore, He says that all the actions performed by Thinnanar, who is the embodiment of love, are pleasing to Him. Love is desire; that is, He says that all of Thinnanar's form is love for Him. This is Ichcha (desire). He says that all his knowledge is the knowledge that knows Him. This is Gnanam (knowledge). He says that all his actions are pleasing to Him. This is Kriya (action). Thus, Thinnanar's Ichcha, Gnanam, and Kriya were all directed towards the Lord. They existed for the Lord. Therefore, Lord Shiva blessed him, saying, "You know his state." He further says in the next verse: "To show you his actions, tomorrow you hide and see. You will see all his devotion to me. Cast away your mental worries," and having blessed the great sage, the Lord with the matted crown adorned with water departed. This is the 807th verse. Lord Shiva, having appeared in Sivagochariyar's dream and told him that Thinnanar's Ichcha, Gnanam, and Kriya were all directed towards Him and happening for His sake, further blessed Sivagochariyar. "Tomorrow, you hide without the hunter seeing you. We will show you all his actions. We will show you how much love he has for me. You will see the state of his love tomorrow. Therefore, cast away your mental worries now," Lord Shiva blesses the great sage Sivagochariyar in his dream. Lord Shiva says, "Tomorrow you hide and see. See from a hidden place." That is the sixth day. Lord Shiva tells Sivagochariyar to hide in a secluded place so that Thinnanar does not see him, but Sivagochariyar can see Thinnanar's actions. Because the next day, Kudumithevar shows Thinnanar blood flowing from his eyes. Thinnanar then becomes very angry and searches for who did this. If Sivagochariyar were to be caught in front of Thinnanar's eyes at that time, something bad would happen. Therefore, the Lord commanded Sivagochariyar, the sage, to hide and watch in his dream. Furthermore, the Lord says in the dream, "We will show you all his loving qualities tomorrow. You will see." Not only the Lord's gracious acts, but everything can only be seen by us if the Lord shows it. Having said all this, Sivagochariyar, who had come with great mental worry and had prayed during the Shiva puja that night, was told by Lord Shiva to cast away his mental worries, and then He disappeared. According to the Thirukkural, "It is difficult to remove mental worries except for those who have joined the feet of the incomparable One." Therefore, Sivagochariyar sought the grace of Lord Shiva and meditated only on His feet. Therefore, that very night, the Lord blessed him, saying, "Let Sivagochariyar's mental worries be removed." Thus, Lord Shiva, who has the Ganga on His matted crown, disappeared from the dream after blessing him. Sekkizhar Peruman refers to Lord Shiva as "Punal Sadila Thirumudiyar" (the Lord with the matted crown adorned with water) in this verse to indicate that just as He bore the Ganga for Bhagiratha, He blessed Sivagochariyar to remove his mental worries. Some also say that the Agamas, which are spread out like the Vedas, are the matted hair, and the grace that can explain the meanings of those Agamas is the Ganga. Thus, the Lord, having revealed His grace in the dream, Sivagochariyar emerges from the dream state into the waking state. Sivagochariyar, having attained the waking state, remains awake until dawn, meditating on that dream. The dream state is called Svapna Avastha. The waking state is called Jagrat Avastha. Jagrat, Svapna, Sushupti, Turiya, Turiyatita are the different states of the soul in the body.

It is also said that the soul resides in the middle of the eyebrows, neck, heart, navel, and Mooladhara respectively in these states. In the dream state (Svapna Avastha), the soul acts through the subtle body from the place where it saw. Then, when it comes to the waking state (Jagrat Avastha), it acts through the gross body from the middle of the eyebrows, perceiving through the five senses. This is stated in the Gnana Sasthras. Generally, it is the nature of the soul to forget many things seen in the waking state when in a dream, and not to perceive many things seen in a dream when in the waking state. But here, it was not so; these were things revealed by the Lord in a dream. Therefore, it is mentioned here that Sivagochariyar, the sage, perceived in his waking state all that the Lord revealed in his dream. Furthermore, it is a tradition that those who have a good dream will lose the benefit of that good dream if they sleep after having the dream. Also, they say that a dream seen in the last watch of the dawn will yield immediate results. This dream seen by Sivagochariyar also yielded immediate results. Therefore, it can be inferred that this was a dream seen in the early morning. Therefore, it can also be understood that he remained awake, expecting the Lord's grace. Furthermore, it is a rule not to sleep after the fourth watch of the night. Therefore, Sivagochariyar, who came to the waking state from the dream, meditating on the dream he had seen, remained awake thereafter, meditating on the Lord's grace. Wonder arose in his mind. A feeling of pride arose. When he realized what he did not know about that hunter, the greatness of Thinnanar, the hunter, as revealed by the Lord, and his own smallness and errors in comparison, and how the Lord had forgiven those errors and blessed him, Sivagochariyar felt fear. With this wonderful feeling and fear, Sivagochariyar performs the puja on the sixth day. As on the previous day, he comes, bathes, and performs puja. That is, taking a holy bath, searching for flowers, water, and other materials for puja, climbing the hill, and meditating on the Lord's grace all happen as before. Sivagochariyar performs puja as before. What is the difference? Even on this sixth day's puja, there are meat, bones, and stones, just as he saw on the previous days. But Sivagochariyar does not remove these, purify himself again by bathing in the river, and make prayers about it today. Because he has received the power of the Lord's grace, and his mind has melted in thought of that grace and attained peace. He has come for this sixth day's puja after the Lord revealed it in his dream on the fifth night. What did the Lord say in the dream? He revealed to Sivagochariyar the greatness of the hunter and the greatness of the puja performed by the hunter, saying that all the hunter's actions were for Him. Therefore, Sivagochariyar now finds the meat and bones placed by Thinnanar on the previous day in the Lord's shrine. Did he remove them? Yes, he removed them. He removed them today as well. But with what feeling did he remove them? With what thought did he remove them? He removed them thinking that these things, which Thinnanar had removed, were said by the Lord to be pleasing to Him. Therefore, for the previous five days, he had removed them thinking they were improper. Just as he removed the Nirmalyams (offerings) of the previous day's Shiva puja, he removed them on this sixth day's Shiva puja. On the previous days, he had removed them thinking that the meat and bones were improper. After that, he bathed in the Ponmugali river, brought puja materials, purified them, and performed puja. But the Lord had said in the dream on the previous day that these were pleasing to Him, hadn't He? Therefore, now, on the sixth day, his act of removing the meat and bones was like removing the Nirmalyams offered in the previous day's Shiva puja. After that, he performed the daily Nitya Puja. Then, as instructed by the Lord in the dream, he stood hidden behind the trees behind Kalathinathar. The puja performed by Sivagochariyar on this day was different from the pujas he performed on previous days. Because on previous days, he performed puja with mental worries. But now, on the sixth day, the Lord had told him to cast away his mental worries. Accordingly, on this sixth day, he performed puja with a peaceful mind, free from worries. Having completed the daily pujas, Sivagochariyar stands hidden behind the trees behind Kalathinathar, as per the Lord's command. We will continue to reflect on what happens on that day. Thiruchitrambalam.

Chapter 31 — Kannappa Nayanar

Thiruchitrambalam Periyapuranam Part 154 Ilaimalinda Sarukkam Kannappa Nayanar Puranam. On the fifth night after Thinnanar began worshipping Kudumithevar, Lord Shiva appeared in Sivagochariyar's dream, spoke of Thinnanar's greatness, and told him to finish his puja the next day (the sixth day) and then observe what happened from a hidden place. Accordingly, the next morning, Sivagochariyar performed the daily puja for Kudumithevar and waited, hidden among the trees, to observe the events. At the same time, on the sixth morning, as usual, Thinnanar set out for hunting at dawn. This was the sixth day of Thinnanar's worship, having attained Shiva-nature through the gracious gaze of Thirukkalathiappar. This sixth day was the auspicious day when Thinnanar attained Shiva-peru (liberation). Thinnanar goes on a great hunt. The hunt Thinnanar undertook on this sixth day was not like the hunts he had undertaken on the previous four days; it was a unique and great hunt. The hunts Thinnanar undertook on the previous four days provided the meat-offering needed for his puja. Therefore, the hunts that occurred in the past four days were a means to the Shiva-peru that Thinnanar attained. The hunt Thinnanar undertakes on this sixth day became the fruit of the achievements he accomplished in the previous four days. Therefore, the hunt on this sixth day becomes a unique and great hunt. What did Thinnanar do on this sixth day? "He came near the Lord of Thirukkalathi, who is the nectarine wealth for those who seek Him, with incomparable meat-offering, good holy water, and fresh flowers placed on his head, arranged in various ways." Lord Shiva, who resides on Thirukkalathi hill, is the nectarine wealth for those who seek Him. Lord Shiva is the nectarine wealth for those who seek Him. "Theruvar" means those who have clear knowledge, having clearly understood that Lord Shiva is the primordial Pasupathi. Sekkizhar Peruman, in this verse, refers to "Theruvar" as those who have mastered the four stages of hearing, contemplation, clarity, and absorption (nishtai). For such "Theruvar" who have attained absorption, the Lord is like nectar. The Lord is the nectarine wealth for those who seek Him. "The wealth that gives endless joy to those who cling to His feet, yet never diminishes," says the 22nd verse of Thiruvidaimarudur Mummanikkovai, composed by Pattinathu Adigal. Those who excel in knowing nothing but serving Pasupathi as His devotees are called "Theruvar." The Thiruvempavai verses 9 and 19 from Thiruvachagam, which say, "We will bow to Your devotees' feet, we will be their companions. They will be our husbands. We will serve as they wish. If our Lord grants us this, we have no lack. Let our breasts not join the shoulders of those who are not Your devotees. Let our hands do no service but Yours. Let our eyes see nothing else day and night. If our Lord grants us this," also describe the nature of devotees called "Theruvar."

Lord Shiva of Thirukkalathi, the nectarine wealth for those who seek Him. To approach this Thirukkalathiappar, Thinnanar comes with the intention of approaching Him with incomparable meat-offering, good holy water, and fresh flowers placed on his head. Thinnanar hurries, thinking that it has become too late. But he encounters many bad omens. Birds, which indicate good and bad events that are about to happen through their sight and sound, now show bad omens to Thinnanar. It is a tradition that if crows, sparrows, owls, and nightjars cry out and make a commotion, it indicates destruction and is a bad omen. "Owls and nightjars, being evil birds, fight among themselves and foretell destruction," says the 632nd verse of Thirugnanasambandar Puranam. Similarly, some say that if one sees a flow of blood on the way, it is also a bad omen. All these bad omens appear to Thinnanar. As Thinnanar goes to Thirukkalathiappar, carrying the meat-offering, holy water, and flowers needed for the Lord after hunting, all these bad omens appear to him. Generally, if bad omens appear when someone goes out, they would think that evil will befall them due to those omens. But for Thinnanar, his knowledge knew nothing but Thirukkalathiappar. Therefore, all the bad omens he saw appeared to him as evil things that would happen to the Lord. So, great compassion arose in him. "Therefore, I am ruined," says Thinnanar. "I am ruined! What has happened to my Father?" Thinnanar trembles. The knowledge that birds foretell omens and show blood came to Thinnanar through his previous experience. Sekkizhar Peruman describes these events in the 812th verse as follows: "As he hurried, thinking, 'I have delayed so long,' all the gathered omens repeatedly foretold evil. As these evil birds showed blood, he thought, 'What has happened to my Father? I am ruined!' and approached." Sekkizhar Peruman continues to sing in the 813th verse: "The Lord of Thirukkalathi, to show Thinnanar's devotion to the sage, made one of His sacred eyes suddenly bleed. The great archer, seeing this from afar, ran swiftly." This is the verse. Thirukkalathiappar, as He had told Sivagochariya Munivar in his dream, to show the power of Thinnanar's love, made blood suddenly flow from one of His sacred eyes. Thinnanar, seeing this from afar, ran very swiftly. In this sacred verse, Sekkizhar Peruman has depicted the three forms of the Lord: the formless (aruvathirumeni), the formless-with-form (aruvuruvathirumeni), and the form-with-form (uruvathirumeni). Calling Thirukkalathiappar "Annalar" (the Lord) is the formless form. It says, "blood flowed from one of His eyes." So, there must be an eye. This is the form-with-form.

The Kudumi Thevar, praised as Kalathi Adigalar, who was the cause of this, is the Linga form, which is the formless-with-form. Thus, in this verse, the formless, formless-with-form, and form-with-form of the Lord are seen together, giving a vision. Why does the Lord take these forms—formless, formless-with-form, and form-with-form? It is due to His compassion, which came to remove the dark bodies (karmic bodies) of souls. "Karumeni" refers to the births that souls take. The Lord takes these three forms out of compassion to remove the sufferings of birth and to remove the defects of souls. This is found in the Sivagnana Siddhiyar verse: "It is said that He took a form, yet we saw His formless form. When He took another form, we saw both His forms. All three are the result of His compassion that came to remove our dark bodies." This is the 55th verse of the first Sutra of Pramanaviyal, Svapakkam, Sivagnana Siddhiyar. Therefore, in the Sivalinga form of Thirukkalathiappar, one face with three eyes was seen, and from the right eye, blood was flowing suddenly. In reality, the Lord's true body is not a body made of the seven dhatus (elements) that are the product of Maya. Therefore, blood did not actually flow from that body. So, we should understand that what was seen here was a sight as if blood was flowing. Because the bodies of worldly people like us are made of seven dhatus. Blood is one of those seven dhatus. But the body of the Lord is a body that came with His compassion as its form. Therefore, when it is said that the Lord showed blood flowing from His sacred eyes, it means that blood was not actually flowing. We should understand that the Lord showed a sight as if blood was flowing. Thinnanar had already seen bad omens. Therefore, he ran in a panic, overwhelmed by love, wondering what had happened to the Lord. When he arrived, even from a distance, the Lord showed Thinnanar as if blood was flowing from His right eye on His sacred face. Thinnanar, seeing this from afar, ran very fast towards the Lord. Sekkizhar describes Thinnanar's state in the next verse. "He who came saw blood and fainted. The good water in his mouth spilled, the meat and bow in his hand scattered and fell. The garland of fresh flowers on his head swayed and came loose. The one with a garland on his chest fell to the ground, collapsing." This is the verse in which Sekkizhar Swamigal explains Thinnanar's state. Thinnanar saw blood flowing from the Lord's sacred face even as he approached from a distance. When he came very close to the Lord, he still saw that blood. Upon seeing it, he fainted. The sight itself caused him to faint. But only cowards faint upon seeing blood. But was Thinnanar a coward? Sekkizhar Swamigal uses the epithet "alangal marpar" (one with a garland on his chest) to indicate that he was not. This idiom refers to the valor of the chest. It indicates one's valor and the strength of the chest. Therefore, Sekkizhar says "alangal marpar." This indicates Thinnanar's valor and strength. Thinnanar was a warrior who engaged in hunting and bloodshed. But here, he fainted upon seeing Kudumi Thevar's blood, which was due to his immense love for Kudumi Thevar. Fainting means losing one's senses. The sight of blood caused him to faint, and so the sense organs that support the body and the nerves that make them function properly went awry. Therefore, his body trembled, and Thinnanar fell to the ground. This is what Sekkizhar Swamigal says, connecting "saw, fainted, trembled, and fell." As he fell to the ground, the holy water he had brought in his mouth-pot spilled. The bow he held in his hand fell. The flowers, the sacred garland for the Lord, which Thinnanar had brought on his head, scattered and fell. These were all things Thinnanar had lovingly and painstakingly collected and arranged for Kudumi Thevar. Furthermore, Thinnanar had come with compassion, thinking that it was already late today. Thinnanar protected these items as more precious than his own life. The fact that he was unable to protect them and became weary shows us the intensity of his weariness. All these items, which he had painstakingly collected, became useless for puja today. Because on the previous night, the Lord had accepted all these puja items. Therefore, they had now become Nirmalyam (discarded offerings). So, there is also a hint here that they became useless for puja. Due to Thinnanar's agitation, the meat-offering he held in his hand for the Lord fell to the ground. The bow and arrow he held in his other hand also fell. The meat-offering held in his right hand fell. The bow held in his left hand fell. The bow Thinnanar held in his hand helped him hunt for the Lord's meat-offering during the day. The same bow served the Lord's work at night, accompanying Thinnanar when he guarded the Lord. That glorious bow also fell. The holy water for the Lord, which he had brought in his mouth-pot, slowly spilled and scattered. The fresh sacred garlands for the Lord were those Thinnanar had brought on his head. They also scattered and fell. Thus, Thinnanar fell, fainting. Now, Sekkizhar Peruman explains further. "He who fell, rose and wiped the blood. He saw that the bleeding did not stop. He did not know what to do. He sighed and fell again. 'Who did this?' he asked, and rose. He looked everywhere. He picked up his bow and arrow and thought, 'Did fierce warriors, my enemies, do this in this mountain? Or did wild animals, like lions, cause this?' He did not know, and searched far and wide in the dense, dark mountain range." The above two are verses composed by Sekkizhar Peruman. Thinnanar thus fell to the ground in agitation and then rose. He saw blood flowing from one of the Lord's sacred eyes. He wiped the blood. Even as he wiped, the bleeding did not stop. He did not know what to do. His mind was distraught and agitated, and he fell to the ground again. Then, regaining his composure, he rose, thinking, "Who did this?" Valor and anger arose in Thinnanar to find and punish the one who had done this to the Lord.

It is human nature for the desire for revenge to take precedence over seeking a solution for gold. The same human nature now manifests in Thinnanar for the sake of the Lord. He picked up the bow and arrow that had fallen earlier. This was to punish those who had done this to the Lord. Thinnanar's state was one where he had forgotten worldly attachments and even himself. Would such a Thinnanar have thoughts of enmity and anger? We saw earlier that Thinnanar did not experience hunger, sleep, etc., for his own sake. Hunger and sleep, which occur for Thinnanar's sake, did not come to him. He forgot them. But he engaged in hunting to satisfy the Lord's hunger, considering it his primary concern. Similarly, the valor and anger that arose in Thinnanar now were due to his immeasurable love and affection for Kudumithevar. He considered those who caused such suffering to Kudumithevar as his enemies. This was due to his love for Kudumithevar. Therefore, for the sake of Kudumithevar, due to his love for Kudumithevar, the remnants of his previous state, such as enmity and other related feelings, came back to Thinnanar's mind. Here we can see the difference between Jivanmukti and Paramukti. Jivanmuktas are united with the Lord's service, becoming one with it, and remain connected with the Lord with body-consciousness due to that service. Paramuktas, on the other hand, are absorbed in the Lord and experience Him in worship. The state of Jivanmukta is to attain and remain in liberation even while having a body and instruments. Therefore, the Ichcha (desire), Gnanam (knowledge), and Kriya (action) of Jivanmuktas are solely for the Lord's service. When there is an obstruction to the Lord's service, they experience a worldly feeling due to residual impressions (vasana) and act to remove that obstruction. This is described as the nature of the "anainthor" (those who have attained union) in the 12th Sutra of Sivagnanabodham. Thinnanar attained the state of Jivanmukta with the help of Thirukkalathiappar's gracious gaze the moment he saw Thirukkalathiappar. After that, Thinnanar lived in the world for six days in that state of Jivanmukta.
